Chapter 1
PROPs and TRAPs for QFT

I introduce structures that might help rigorously define Feynman rules for Quantum Field Theories.
Most of the results presented in this chapter were proven in
[
PCP20
]
and
[
CLS22
]
.

Introduction

Statement of the problem

Feynman graphs, introduced in
[
Fey18
]
, have somehow secured themselves into popular culture as well as into many many textbooks
[
PS95
,
Wei95
]
. One can easily argue that they are the most well-known sight in the broad field of QFT. This is probably due to their unique graphical representation, apt to strike the imagination as carrying arcane meanings.

Is is not the point of this work to say that Feynman graphs are not mysterious. Quite the contrary: when, as a master student, I stubbled upon some Feynman graphs that needed to be computed, I was so unable to grasp how one is supposed to perform the computations that I decided to never compute any Feynman graphs. It is now more than ten years later and I have betrailed all I once hold dear and holy: I now wish to deal with them, although I still do not know how to evaluate them.

What I do know is that I don’t want to simply apply Feynman rules. This is not only out of lazyness and bad memories. It is also because at some point in the last ten years, I have become a mathematician and I now do care if a computation has a meaning when I performe it. Therefore I first and foremost wish to understand Feynman rules, and prove that they are well-defined.

Before giving precisions about the question at hand, let me recall that for a QFT
\tau
, its lagrangian tells us which type of interactions can appear in its perturbative series. To compute the terms of the perturbative series, Feynman devised in 1948 his aforementioned
Feynman graphs
(or Feynman diagrams). The
Feynman rules
dictate which diagrams appear in a given theory and are a
set of prescriptions that tell how to associate to each graph an analytical expression (typically an integral) that has to be evaluated. So formally speaking the Feynman rules of a QFT
\tau
can be seen as a map

F_{\tau}:\mathcal{G}_{\tau}\longrightarrow X

with
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}
the set of Feynman graphs of the theory and
X
some analytical space to be determined. In Section
1.8
below we investigate what
X
should be. It turns out that
X
is a space of distribution valued meromorphic germs.

Phycisists notoriously do not care so much of the well-definess of their computations. This is not a judgement: they just have better things to do. So they do not try to rigourously define the map
F_{\tau}
and actually do not necessarily think of it as a map. They instead apply it (or rather apply the “rules”) to some graphs and observe that they end up in some analytical space.

This approach is extremely successful but quite frustrating for the mathematicians. In order to deal with this frustration I would like to
define
the map
F_{\tau}
and show that
F_{\tau}(G)
lies in the right space
X
for any Feynman graph
G
. So this is a very analytical problem. It was actually solved in some cases and in particular recently for a QFT on a compact Riemannian manifold in
[
DZ21
]
. In this paper the authors use a plethora of analytical methods to deal with the many challenges they encounter. The approach I wish to present here is quite different.

Indeed, I like to deal with analytical problem by finding a structure and a universal property that will solve the problem. So I wish to look for a category
\mathcal{C}
such that

•

the space
X
is an object in
\mathcal{C}
,

•

\mathcal{C}
admits a free object that can be described in term of graphs.

I then wish to see the Feynman graphs as the free object of the category
\mathcal{C}
. Their universal property will then give us the existence of essential properties of the map
F_{\tau}
, i.e. of the Feynman rules.

Content and main results

In order to make the first steps of the program presented above, my collaborators Sylvie Paycha from Potsdam Universität and Loïc Foissy from Université du Littoral and myself started to peruse literature looking for a category with the desired properties. We found out the PROPs (PROducts and Permutations) were good candidates. I will present PROPs in details below so won’t say more about their definition here. The key point was a “folklore theorem”, namely that graphs are a free PROP. This statement is usually stated in a very abstract categorical framework. For our purpose we needed to make it more pedestrian. We then set to prove it in the pedestrian approach. This is presented in the first three sections of this chapter.

In Section
1.1
I introduce PROPs, first in their categorical setting (Definition
1.1.7
). I then work down this abstract definition to obtain a pedestrian definition of PROPs (Definition
1.1.9
).

This pedestrian approach allows me to give examples of PROPs in Section
1.2
. In particular, I show in Theorem
1.5.7
that morphisms of tensor products of a nuclear Fréchet spaces can be endowed with a PROP structure. Since nuclear Fréchet spaces have similarities with the space
X
where Feynman rules evaluate Feynman graphs, this is a step in the right direction. Another important example of a PROP is Theorem

1.2.25
that (isoclasses of generalised) graphs have a PROP structure. These objects are introduced in Definitions
1.2.19
and
1.2.21
.

The first main result of this chapter in in Section
1.3
. It is Theorem
1.3.10
, stating that the PROP of graphs introduced earlier is freely generated by indecomposable graphs. This result is a precise statement of the aforementioned “folklore theorem” that graphs are a free PROP. However, as it is discussed after the sketch of proof in Subsection
1.3.2
, this result does not provide enough help to rigorously build Feynman rules.

This issues come from the existence of ‘‘big loops’’ in graphs that cannot be tamed by the PROP structure of graphs. Sylvie Paycha, Loïc Foissy and myself then started to look for another category that would be able to deal with these big loops. We came up with the notion of TRAPs (Traces and Permutations)
1
1

1

to our dismay, it was pointed out to us that a version of TRAPs had already been introduced under the name of “wheeled PROPs”, see Remark
1.4.3
.
which are the subject of the next four sections of this chapter.

In Section
1.4
I define the category of TRAPs (Definitions
1.4.1
and
1.4.7
). Notice that only the pedestrian version of the definition is given. A categorical definition exists for unitary TRAPs but not, as far as I am aware, for non-unitary TRAPs.

Examples of TRAPs are presented in Section
1.5
. In particular, Theorem
1.5.7
states that morphisms of tensor products of a nuclear Fréchet spaces admit a TRAP structure; and in Theorem
1.5.11
I obtain the same result for smoothing pseudo-differential operators.

The most important example of TRAP is build in Section
1.6
: it is the TRAP of corolla oriented graphs (Definitions
1.6.1
and
1.6.2
). Theorem
1.6.5
is an important result: graphs also have a TRAP structure. But Section
1.6
also contains the second main result of this chapter, namely Theorem
1.6.8
. It states that the TRAP of graphs is actually freely generated by the decorations of the vertices of the graphs.

Section
1.7
is then devoted to the applications of these results. First I show in Proposition-Definition
1.7.1
how TRAPs are related to PROPs. I then introduce in Definition
1.7.6
the notion of generalised traces for TRAPs and give some of their properties in Proposition
1.7.7
. The most important of these applications for QFT is probably the notion of
P
-amplitude (Definition
1.7.9
). Properties of
P
-amplitudes are given in Proposition
1.7.12
. These various constructions (vertical concatenation in TRAPs, generalised traces and amplitudes) are then applied in Theorem
1.7.15
to the TRAP of smoothing pseudo-differential operators already mentioned.

In the final Section
1.8
I attempt to draw a plan on how to use the universal property of graphs in the category of TRAPs to define Feynman rules. I start by expanding the above discussion on Feynman rules to try to interpret some formulae that can be found in excellent Physics textbook
[
Riv91
]
. This leads me to introduce various relevant analytical spaces, and in particular the space of distribution-valued meromorphic germs in Definition
1.8.7
. I can then precisely formulate our goal of defining Feynman rules in Conjecture
1.8.8
. I then conjecture that a relevant space of distributon-valued meromorphic germs carries a TRAP structure (Conjecture
1.8.11
). Then I precisely explain in Definition
1.8.12
how this Conjecture, if true, allows to rigorously define Feynamn rules and how it would solve our initial problem (Proposition
1.8.14
). Along the way I discuss the various challenges one would have to overcome to fulfill this program and sometimes what methods could be successful to do so.

1.1
The category of PROPs

1.1.1
Elements of category theory

Category theory is not the main point of this thesis and as such I will not write down a full introduction to category theory. Not only it would be needlessly long and cumbersome to read, but also not essential since I will mostly use a pedestrian definition for PROPs. We refer the author to the many great introductions to category theory available in the literature; for example
[
ML98
]
from which the following definitions are borrowed.

Definition 1.1.1
.

A
strict monoidal category
is a triple
(\mathcal{C},\otimes,e)
with

1.

\mathcal{C}
a category.

2.

\otimes
a bifunctor
\otimes:\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}
which is associative:

\otimes\circ(\otimes\times\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}})=\otimes\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{\mathcal{C}}\times\otimes)

(where, as in
[
ML98
]
, we identify
\mathcal{C}\times(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})
and
(\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{C})\times\mathcal{C}
).

3.

e\in Obj(\mathcal{C})
is a unit for
\otimes
:

\otimes(e\times A)=A=\otimes(A\times e)

for any
A\in Obj(\mathcal{C})
.

Remark 1.1.2
.

I define only
strict
monoidal category since I will not need the more general notion of monoidal category, which would require the definition of natural morphisms.

To be more precise, one should specify that we will actually work with non-strict monoidal categories. Very roughly speaking, for these objects the equal signs of the definition above should be replaced by natural isomorphisms as the associator and the unitors satisfying the well-known pentagon and triangle diagrams. However, MacLane’s coherence theorem
[
ML63
]
for monoidal categories essentially guarantees that one can always “strictify” monoidal
categories which is a common practice among specialists. We will follow this practice here and refer the reader to
[
Kas12
, XI.5]
for a classical introduction to this topic, or
[
Bec23
]
for a recent review.

Notice that the fact that
\otimes
is a bifunctor implies in particular for any objects
A,B
of
\mathcal{C}
we have

\mathrm{Id}_{A}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{B}=\mathrm{Id}_{A\otimes B}.

Furthermore, for any objects
A
,
B
,
C
,
A^{\prime}
,
B^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}
and any morphisms
f:A\longrightarrow B
,
g:B\longrightarrow C
,
{f^{\prime}:}A\longrightarrow B^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}:B^{\prime}\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{C}
we have

{(g\otimes g^{\prime})\circ(f\otimes f^{\prime})=(g\circ f)\otimes(g^{\prime}\circ f^{\prime}).}

(1.1)

Example 1.1.3
.

The archetypal example of a (non-strict) monoidal is the category
\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}
of vector spaces over a field
\mathbb{K}
, with linear maps as morphisms and the usual tensor product as monoidal product (hence the notation for general monoidal products). Of course, in practice, one does not think of
\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}
as not being strict. For example, we know how with which isomorphisms we should identity
(A\otimes B)\otimes C
and
A\otimes(B\otimes C)
.

We will need monoidal categories where
A\otimes B\simeq B\otimes A
.

Definition 1.1.4
.

(
[
ML98
]
)
A
symmetric monoidal category
is a strict monoidal category with isomorphisms
\gamma_{A,B}:A\otimes B\longrightarrow B\otimes A
such that:

•

the
\gamma_{A,B}
are natural, i.e. for any objects
A,A^{\prime}
and
B,B^{\prime}
and for any
f:A\longrightarrow A^{\prime}
and
g:B\longrightarrow B^{\prime}
we have

A\otimes B
B\otimes A
A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}
B^{\prime}\otimes A^{\prime}
\gamma_{A,B}
f\otimes g
\gamma_{A^{\prime},B^{\prime}}
g\otimes f

•

for any objects
A
,
B
and
C
,
we have

A\otimes B\otimes C
B\otimes C\otimes A
B\otimes A\otimes C
\gamma_{A,B\otimes C}
\gamma_{A,B}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{C}
\penalty\ \penalty\ \mathrm{Id}_{B}\otimes\gamma_{C,A}

•

for any objects
A
and
B

{\gamma_{B,A}\circ}\gamma_{A,B}=\mathrm{Id}_{A\otimes B}.

Remark 1.1.5
.

For non-strict monoidal categories, the diagram above has to be enlarged with associators. Writing these various isomorphisms as
\alpha
we have to require the extended diagram
1.1.5
below to commute.

(A\otimes B)\otimes C
A\otimes(B\otimes C)
(B\otimes C)\otimes A
(B\otimes A)\otimes C
B\otimes(A\otimes C)
B\otimes(C\otimes A)
\alpha
\gamma_{A,B\otimes C}
\alpha
\gamma_{A,B}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{C}
\alpha
\mathrm{Id}_{B}\otimes\gamma_{A,C}

Recall that a category is
locally small
if for any objects
A
and
B
the morphisms between
A
and
B
form a set and not a proper class.

Definition 1.1.6
.

let
\mathcal{D}
be a (strict) monoidal category. A locally small category
\mathcal{C}
is
enriched over
\mathcal{D}
if
for any
(A,B)\in Obj(\mathcal{C})^{2}
,
\Hom(A,B)
(the set of morphisms from
A
to
B
) is a object of
\mathcal{D}
.

As for (strict) monoidal categories, the archetypal example of an enriched category is
\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}
, which is enriched over itself. Indeed the set of linear morphisms between any two vector spaces has the structure of a vector space.

Enriched categories seem to have been considered for the first time in
[
ML65
]
, where PROPs were also introduced. Indeed enriched categories enter the categorical definition of PROPs, which we are now able to give. In the meantime, the definitive text on enriched categories seems to be
[
K.05
]
.

1.1.2
PROPs: categorical definition

PROPs (PROducts and Permutations) seem to have appeared first in
[
ML65
]
. They have been since a very active field of research and it is not within the scope of this thesis to review the full literature on the topic. Let us just quote
[
Mar08
]
for a (fairly) recent review of the topic.

To relate them to a structure that is perhaps more familiar to the reader, one could present PROPs as a generalisation of operads and co-operads. While operads can be seen as dealing with operations with several inputs and one outputs, and co-operads with operations with several outputs and one inputs, PROPs allow to treat operations with several inputs
and
outputs. On top of
[
Mar08
]
, a classical reference to operads is
[
LV12
]
.
[
BD16
]
is another, more recent, introduction on operads which emphazises computational aspects.

Let us now dive in and give a compact definition of PROPs, taken from
[
AL19
]
and also from
[
Mar08
]
.

Definition 1.1.7
.

A
PROP
(PROducts and Permutations) is a strict symmetric monoidal category enriched over
\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}
whose objects are indexed by natural number
\{[n]\}
and whose monoidal product is given by the addition:

[n]\otimes[m]=[n+m].

(1.2)

We can also state a categorical definition of morphisms of PROP, which we quote from
[
AL19
]
.

Definition 1.1.8
.

Let
P
and
Q
be two PROPs. A
morphism of PROPs
between
P
and
Q
is a strict monoidal functor
F:P\longrightarrow Q
, i.e. a functor such that
F([n]_{P})\otimes_{Q}F([m]_{P})=F([n]_{P}\otimes_{P}[m]_{P})
.

We write
\mathbf{PROP}
the category of PROPs.

Let us now discuss this discussion in order to make it clearer to the layman mathematician. In this discussion we will not aim at mathematical rigour. The complete axioms will be given in the next Subsection. A PROP consists of a category whose objects can be identified with the integers, therefore:

•

A PROP is only characterised by its morphisms, and the sets of morphisms
2
2

2

these are indeed sets since the category, being enriched, is locally small (Definition
1.1.6
)
between any two objects
[l]
and
[k]
are vector spaces since the category is enriched over
\mathbf{Vect}_{\mathbb{K}}
. A PROP can thus be written as a family
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}}
of vector spaces with
P(k,l):=\Hom([k],[l])
.

•

Since the elements of the vector spaces
P(k,l)
are actually morphisms from
[l]
to
[k]
, we can compose elements
if their domain and image coincide. Thus we have associative maps

\circ:P(l,m)\otimes P(k,l)\longrightarrow P(k,m).

This is item
3a
of Definition
1.1.9
below.

•

Since any objects
[n]
admits a unit
\mathrm{Id}_{n}
, the maps
\circ
, seen as products, are unital. This is item
3b
of Definition
1.1.9
below.

•

The monoidal structure of the category gives another product in a PROP. By the previous point, equations (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) the monoidal product gives a family of products

\otimes:P(k,l)\otimes P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\longrightarrow P(k+k^{\prime},l+l^{\prime})

which are associative (by definition of a strict monoidal category) and commutative (since the category is symmetric). These requirements are items
2a
and
2c
of Definition
1.1.9
below.

•

The monoidal unit implies that the products
\otimes
are unital. Furthermore, Equation (
1.2
) implies that the monoidal unit is the object
[0]
and that the unit for the products
\otimes
is a element of
P(0,0)
. This is item
2b
of Definition
1.1.9
below.

•

Equation (
1.1
) gives a compatibility condition between the products
\circ
and
\otimes
. It is item
4
of Definition
1.1.9
below.

•

For
m\geqslant 1
, identifying the object
m
with
[m]=\{1,\cdots,m\}
, Equation (
1.2
) implies
[m]=[1]^{\otimes m}
. Then the symmetry of the monoidal product (Definition
1.1.4
) together with the definition of the
P(k,l)
implies that the “flip” operation
\tau=\gamma_{[1],[1]}
defined by
\tau(x\otimes y)=y\otimes x
lies in
P(2,2)
. Then the functoriality of the tensor product gives maps
\mathrm{Id}_{[i]}\otimes\tau\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{[m-i-2]}:[1]^{\otimes[m]}\longrightarrow[1]^{\otimes[m]}
. These maps are transpositions acting on
[m]
, and composing them we that the symmetry group with
m
elements

\mathfrak{S}_{m}
is a subgroup of
\Hom([m],[m])
. More precisely, there is a map
f_{\sigma}\in{\Hom([m],[m])}
for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
. This induces a left action of
\mathfrak{S}_{m}
on
\Hom([n],[m])
by setting
\sigma.p:=f_{\sigma}\circ p
. With the same argument we also obtain a right action of the symmetry group. Thus a PROP is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module (item
1
of Definition
1.1.9
below).

•

Finally, since these actions of the symmetry groups are given by the categorical structures, we have compatibility axioms between these actions and the product
\otimes
and
\circ
. These are items
5
and
6
of Definition
1.1.9
below.

1.1.3
PROPs: pedestrian definition

From the discussion above we can reformulate Definition
1.1.7
to obtain a definition of PROPs less compact but more tractable for examples.

Definition 1.1.9
.

A
PROP
is a family
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}}
of vector spaces such that:

1.

P
is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module, that is to say, for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
P(k,l)

is a
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-module. In other words, there exist maps

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times P(k,l)&\longrightarrow&P(k,l)\\
(\sigma,p)&\longmapsto&\sigma\cdot p,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(k,l)\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}&\longrightarrow&P(k,l)\\
(p,\tau)&\longmapsto&p\cdot\tau,\end{array}\right.

such that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\tau,\tau^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}^{2}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{2}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}_{[l]}\cdot p

\displaystyle=p\cdot\mathrm{Id}_{[k]}=p,

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot p)

\displaystyle=(\sigma\sigma^{\prime})\cdot p,

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(p\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=(\sigma\cdot p)\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle(p\cdot\tau)\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=p\cdot(\tau\tau^{\prime}).

2.

For any
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, there exists a product
*
from
P(k,l)\otimes P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
to
P(k+k^{\prime},l+l^{\prime})

such that:

(a)

For any
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{6}
, for any
(p,p^{\prime},p^{\prime\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\times P(k^{\prime\prime},l^{\prime\prime})
,

p*(p^{\prime}*p^{\prime\prime})=(p*p^{\prime})*p^{\prime\prime}.

(b)

There exists
I_{0}\in P(0,0)
, such that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

p*I_{0}=I_{0}*p=p.

(c)

The product
*
is commutative in the following sense: for any
(k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, for any
(p,p^{\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,

c_{l,l^{\prime}}\cdot(p*p^{\prime})=(p^{\prime}*p)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}},

(1.3)

where for any
(m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
c_{m,n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
is defined by:

\displaystyle c_{m,n}(i)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}i+n\mbox{ if }i\leqslant m,\\
i-m\mbox{ if }i>m.\end{cases}

(1.4)

This product
*
is called the
horizontal concatenation
.

3.

For any
(k,l,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, there exists a product
\circ
from
P(l,m)\otimes P(k,l)
to
P(k,m)
such that:

(a)

For any
(k,l,m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, for any
(p,q,r)\in P(m,n)\times P(l,m)\times P(k,l)
,

p\circ(q\circ r)=(p\circ q)\circ r.

(b)

There exists
I_{1}\in P(1,1)
, such that for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

p\circ I_{k}=I_{l}\circ p=p,

where we put
I_{n}=I_{1}^{*n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, with the convention
I_{1}^{*0}=I_{0}
.

This product
\circ
is called the
vertical concatenation
.

4.

The vertical and horizontal concatenations are compatible: for any
(k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime},m,m^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{6}
,
for any
(p,p^{\prime},q,q^{\prime})\in P(l,m)\times P(l^{\prime},m^{\prime})\times P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,

(p*p^{\prime})\circ(q*q^{\prime})=(p\circ q)*(p^{\prime}{\circ}q^{\prime}).

5.

The vertical concatenation and the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
are compatible:
for any
(k,l,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{3}
, for any
(p,q)\in P(l,m)\times P(k,l)
, for any
(\sigma,\tau,\nu)\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot(p\circ q)

\displaystyle=(\sigma\cdot p)\circ q,

\displaystyle(p\circ q)\cdot\nu

\displaystyle=p\circ(q\cdot\nu),

\displaystyle(p\cdot\tau)\circ q

\displaystyle=p\circ(\tau\cdot q).

6.

The horizontal concatenation and the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
are compatible:
for any
(k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime})\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
, for any
(p,p^{\prime})\in P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,
for any
(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\tau,\tau^{\prime})\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l^{\prime}}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k^{\prime}}
,

\displaystyle(\sigma\cdot p)*(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(p*p^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(p\cdot\tau)*(p^{\prime}\cdot\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(p*p^{\prime})\cdot(\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime}),

where for any
\alpha\in\mathfrak{S}_{m}
,
\beta\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
,
\alpha\otimes\beta\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
is defined by:

\alpha\otimes\beta(i)=\begin{cases}\alpha(i)\mbox{ if }i\leqslant m,\\
\beta(i-m)+m\mbox{ if }i>m.\end{cases}

We can also write the pedestrian definition of morphisms of PROPs.

Definition 1.1.10
.

Let
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
and
Q=(Q(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be two PROPs. A
morphism of PROPs
is a family

\phi=(\phi_{k,l})_{k,l\geqslant 0}
of linear maps
\phi_{k,l}:P(k,l)\mapsto Q(k,l)
which form a morphism for the horizontal
concatenation, the vertical concatenation and the actions of the symmetric groups. More precisely, for any
(k,l,m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
:

•

\forall(p,q)\in P(l,m)\times P(k,l),\penalty\ \phi_{k,m}(p\circ q)=\phi_{l,m}(p)\circ\phi_{k,l}(q)
,

•

\forall(p,q)\in P(k,l)\times P(n,m),\penalty\ \phi_{k+n,l+m}(p*q)=\phi_{k,l}(p)*\phi_{n,m}(q)
,

•

\forall(\sigma,p)\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times P(k,l),\penalty\ \phi_{k,l}(\sigma.p)=\sigma.\phi_{k,l}(p)
,

•

\forall(p,\tau)\in P(k,l)\times\mathfrak{S}_{k},\penalty\ \phi_{k,l}(p.\tau)=\phi_{k,l}(p).\tau
.

We will write
\mathbf{PROP}
the category of PROPs.

By abuse of notation, we shall write
\phi(p)
instead of
\phi_{k,l}(p)
for
p\in P(k,l)
.

1.2
Examples of PROPs

1.2.1
The PROP of linear morphisms

We start with the most classical example of PROP (see for example
[
Val03
]
and
[
Mar08
]
) namely the PROP of linear morphisms between tensor products of a finite dimensional vector space.

Definition-Proposition 1.2.1
.

Given a finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
V
, the PROP

\Hom_{V}
is defined in the following way:

1.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Hom_{V}(k,l):=\Hom(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l}).

2.

For any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, let
\theta_{\sigma}
be the endomorphism of
V^{\otimes n}
defined by

\theta_{\sigma}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n}):=v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(n)}.

This defines a left action of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
on
V^{\otimes n}
.
For any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any
f\in\Hom_{V}(k,l)
, for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, we set:

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot f

\displaystyle:=\theta_{\sigma}\circ f,

\displaystyle f\cdot\tau

\displaystyle:=f\circ\theta_{\tau}.

3.

The horizontal concatenation is the tensor product of maps and
I_{0}:\mathbb{K}\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
is the identity
map
I_{0}:=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathbb{K}}
.

4.

The vertical concatenation is the usual composition of maps and
I_{1}:V\longrightarrow V
is the identity map
I_{1}:=\mathrm{Id}_{V}
.

Remark 1.2.2
.

Following the convention that for a PROP
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}}
, an element in
P(k,l)
has “
k
entries and
l
exits”, for the PROP
\Hom_{V}
, an element
f\in\Hom_{V}(k,l)
has “
k
entries and
l
exits”.

Proof.

1.

The maps
\theta_{\sigma}
turns
\Hom_{V}
into a
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-module by associativity of the composition product.

2.

The horizontal concatenation is associative as a result of the associativity of the tensor product
\otimes
, and we trivially have that
\otimes
maps
\Hom_{V}(k,l)\otimes\Hom_{V}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
to
\Hom_{V}(k+k^{\prime},l+l^{\prime})
. Furthermore, if
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and
f\in\Hom_{V}(k,l)
, for any
v\in V^{\otimes k}
, we have

(I_{0}\otimes f)(v)=(I_{0}\otimes f)(1.v):=I_{0}(1)\otimes f(v)=1_{\mathbb{K}}\otimes f(v)=f(v)

3.

The vertical concatenation is associative as the consequence of the associativity of the composition product. We furthermore have

I_{n}:=I_{1}^{\otimes n}=\mathrm{Id}_{V}^{\otimes n}=\mathrm{Id}_{V^{\otimes n}}
where the last identity follows
from the definition of the tensor product of maps.

4.

For any
f\in\Hom_{V}(l,m)
,
f^{\prime}\in\Hom_{V}(l^{\prime},m^{\prime})
,
g\in\Hom_{V}(k,l)
,
g^{\prime}\in\Hom_{V}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,
v\in V^{\otimes k}
and

v^{\prime}\in V^{\otimes k^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle(f\otimes f^{\prime})\circ(g\otimes g^{\prime})(v\otimes v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(f\otimes f^{\prime})(g(v)\otimes g^{\prime}(v^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=(f\circ g)(v)\otimes(f^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime})(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=[(f\circ g)\otimes(f^{\prime}\circ g^{\prime})](v\otimes v^{\prime}).

Thus, the horizontal and vertical concatenation are compatible.

5.

The vertical concatenation and the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
are compatible by associativity of the composition product.

6.

For any
f\in\Hom_{V}(k,l)
,
f^{\prime}\in\Hom_{V}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l^{\prime}}
,
v\in V^{\otimes k}
,

v^{\prime}\in V^{\otimes k^{\prime}}
we have

\displaystyle(\sigma.f)\otimes(\sigma^{\prime}.f^{\prime})(v\otimes v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=({\theta_{\sigma}}\circ f)\otimes({\theta_{\sigma^{\prime}}}\circ f^{\prime})(v\otimes v^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\theta_{\sigma}}(f(v))\otimes({\theta_{\sigma^{\prime}}}f^{\prime}(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=({\theta_{\sigma}}\otimes{\theta_{\sigma^{\prime}}})[f(v)\otimes f^{\prime}(v^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime}).(f\otimes f^{\prime})(v\otimes v^{\prime}).

Similarly, we have
(f.\tau)\otimes(f^{\prime}.\tau^{\prime})=(f\otimes f^{\prime}).(\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime})
and
c_{l,l^{\prime}}\cdot(f*f^{\prime})=(f^{\prime}*f)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}}
, therefore the horizontal action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
are compatible.

∎

Since in QFT one has typically to deal with infinite dimensional space, we have in
[
PCP20
]
generalised the PROP
\Hom_{V}
to a PROP of morphisms of tensor products of infinite dimensional vector spaces.

1.2.2
A PROP for Fréchet nuclear spaces

It is well-known that when dealing with tensor products of infinite dimensional vector spaces on has to be much more careful than in the finite dimensional case. In his seminal work
[
Gro54
]
, Grothendieck showed that the notion of “nuclear spaces” is the right framework to deal with these difficulties. We will therefore work with nuclear spaces which, for the sake of simplicity, we will assume to be Fréchet. One could also work in some more general frameworks, for example with barreled nuclear spaces. We found out we do not need this level of generality for our task at hand.

Topologies on tensor products

The first challenge when dealing with tensor products of infinite dimensional (topological) vector spaces is that one can define many different non-equivalent topologies on the tensor product. We recall the main ones here.

A first possibility is the so-called
\epsilon
-topology
;
[
Trè67
, Definition 43.1]
. For two topological vector spaces
E
and
F
, one
can show (
[
Trè67
, Proposition 42.4]
) the isomorphism of vector spaces

E\otimes F\simeq\mathcal{B}^{c}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma},\mathbb{K})
where

\mathcal{B}^{c}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma},\mathbb{K})
denotes the space of continuous bilinear maps from
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
to
\mathbb{K}
and
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}
(resp.

F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
) the topological dual of
E
(resp.
F
) for
\sigma
, the weak topology.

Recall that a bilinear map
f:E\times F\longrightarrow K
is called separately continuous if,
for any pair
(x,y)\in E\times F
, the maps
z\mapsto f(x,z)
and
z^{\prime}\mapsto f(z^{\prime},y)
are continuous. We then clearly have that continuous bilinear maps
build a linear subspace of the space
\mathcal{B}^{sc}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
of separately continuous bilinear maps.

The space
\mathcal{B}^{sc}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
can be equipped with the topology of uniform convergence on products of equicontinuous subsets of
E^{\prime}_{\sigma}
with
equicontinuous subsets of
F^{\prime}_{\sigma}
. Recall that, for a topological space
X
and a topological vector space
G
, a set
S
of maps from
X
to
G
is
said to be equicontinuous at
x_{0}\in X
if, for any
V\subseteq G
neighbourhood of zero, there is some neighbourhood
V(x_{0})\subseteq X
of
x_{0}
, such
that

\forall f\in S,\penalty\ x\in V(x_{0})\Rightarrow f(x)-f(x_{0})\in V.

In our case,
G
is
\mathbb{K}
and
X
is
E_{\sigma}
(resp.
F_{\sigma}
). This topology induces a topology on the subspace

\mathcal{B}^{c}(E^{\prime}_{\sigma}\times F^{\prime}_{\sigma},\mathbb{K})
and thus on
E\otimes F
. We denote by
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
the topological vector space obtained by
endowing
E\otimes F
with this topology.

There is another topology on
E\otimes F
called the
projective topology
;

[
Trè67
, Definition 43.2]
. The projective topology is defined as the strongest
locally convex topology on
E\otimes F
such that the canonical map
\phi:E\times F\longrightarrow E\otimes F
is continuous.
We write
E\otimes_{\pi}F
the topological vector space obtained by
endowing
E\otimes F
with this topology.

The neighbourhoods of zero of the projective topology can be simply described in terms of neighbourhoods of zero in
E
and
V
. A convex subset
S
of

E\otimes F
containing zero is a neighbourhood of zero if it exist a neighbourhood
U
(resp. V) of zero in
E
(resp.
F
) such that

U\otimes V:=\{u\otimes v|u\in U\wedge v\in V\}\subseteq S
.

Nuclear Fréchet spaces

Most of the results stated here can be found in
[
Gro52
,
Gro54
]
. We also
refer the readerto the more recent presentation
[
Trè67
]
which has notations closer to our own.

We recall that

•

A topological vector space is
Fréchet
if it is Hausdorff, has its topology induced by a countable family of semi-norms and is complete with respect to this family of semi-norms.

•

A topological vector space is called
reflexive
if
E^{\prime\prime}=(E^{\prime})^{\prime}=E
, where
E^{\prime}
is the topological dual of
E
.

In the following
E
and
F
are two topological vector spaces and
\Hom^{c}(E,F)
is the set of continuous linear maps from
E
to
F
.

Remark 1.2.3
.

When
E
and
F
are finite dimensional, we have
\Hom^{c}(E,F)
=
\Hom(E,F)
.

In order to build the PROP
\Hom^{c}_{V}
in the infinite dimensional case, we need Grothendieck’s completion of the tensor product, a notion we recall
here in the setup of locally convex topological
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces.

Let
E
and
F
be two vector spaces. Recall that there exists a vector space
E\otimes F
, and a bilinear map

\phi:E\times F\longrightarrow E\otimes F
such that for any vector space
V
and bilinear map
f:E\times F\longrightarrow V
, there is a unique
linear map
\tilde{f}:E\otimes F\to V
satisfying
f=\tilde{f}\circ\phi
. The space
E\otimes F
is unique modulo isomorphism and is called the
tensor product
of
E
and
F
.

Given two topological vector spaces,
E
and
F
one can a priori equip
E\otimes F
with several topologies, among which the

\epsilon
-topology
and the
projective topology
presented above. We denote as before by
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
(resp.
E\otimes_{\pi}F
) the space
E\otimes F
endowed with the
\epsilon
-topology (resp. the projective topology) and by
E\widehat{\otimes}_{\epsilon}F
(resp.
E\widehat{\otimes}_{\epsilon}F
) of
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
(resp.
E\otimes_{\epsilon}F
) their completion with respect to the

\epsilon
-topology (resp. projective topology). These two spaces differ in general but coincide for nuclear spaces.

Definition 1.2.4
.

[
Gro54
]

A locally convex topological vector space
E
is
nuclear
if, and only if, for any locally convex topological vector space
F
,

E\widehat{\otimes}_{\epsilon}F=E\widehat{\otimes}_{\pi}F=:E\widehat{\otimes}F

holds, in which case
E\widehat{\otimes}F
is called the
completed tensor product
of
E
and
F
.

There are other equivalent definitions of nuclearity, see for example
[
GV64
,
HS08
]
.

Given a locally convex topological vector space
E
, its topological dual
E^{\prime}
can be endowed with various topologies. An important one for our applications will be
the
strong topology
, generated by the family of semi-norms of
E^{\prime}
defined, on any
f\in E^{\prime}
:

\text{for any bounded set }B\text{ of }E,\penalty\ ||f||_{B}:=\sup_{x\in B}|f(x)|.

(1.5)

The topological dual
E^{\prime}
endowed with this topology is called the
strong dual
.

For Fréchet spaces, nuclearity is preserved under strong duality.

Proposition 1.2.5
.

•

[
Trè67
, Proposition 50.6]

A Fréchet space is nuclear if and only if its strong dual is nuclear.

•

[
Trè67
, Proposition 36.5]
A Fréchet nuclear space is reflexive.

Many spaces relevant to renormalisation issues are Fréchet and nuclear. We list here some examples.

Example 1.2.6
.

Any finite dimensional vector space can be equipped with a norm and for any of these norms, they are trivially Banach, hence Fréchet and nuclear.
If
E
and
F
are finite dimensional vector spaces we have

\Hom^{c}(E,F)=\Hom(E,F)\simeq E^{*}\otimes F
, where
\Hom(E,F)
stands for the space of
F
-valued linear maps on

E
and where the dual
E^{*}
is the
algebraic dual
.

Example 1.2.7
.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Take
E=C^{\infty}(U)=:{\mathcal{E}}(U)
. The topological dual
is the space
{E^{\prime}}={\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(U)
of distributions
on
U
with compact support.

Then
E
is Fréchet (
[
Trè67
]
, pp. 86-89), and
E^{\prime}
is nuclear (
[
Trè67
]
, Corollary p. 530). By Proposition

•
‣
1.2.5
,
E
is also nuclear.

Remark 1.2.8
.

Note that the dual
E^{\prime}
of a Fréchet space
E
is never a Fréchet space (for any of the natural topologies on
E^{\prime}
), unless
E
is actually a Banach space (see for example

[
Köt69
]
). In particular,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(U)
is generally not Fréchet.

We now sum up various results of
[
Trè67
]
of importance for later purposes.

Theorem 1.2.9
.

[
Trè67
, Equations (50.17)–(50.19)]

Let
E
and
F
be two Fréchet spaces, with
E
nuclear. The following isomorphisms of topological vector spaces hold.

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}F\simeq\Hom^{c}(E,F)

(1.6)

\displaystyle E\widehat{\otimes}F\simeq\Hom^{c}(E^{\prime},F)

(1.7)

\displaystyle E^{\prime}\widehat{\otimes}F^{\prime}\simeq(E\widehat{\otimes}F)^{\prime}\simeq{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(E\times F,\mathbb{K}).

(1.8)

with
{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
the set of continuous bilinear maps

K:E\times F\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
. Here the duals are endowed with the strong dual topology,

\Hom^{c}(E,F)
with the strong topology and
{\mathcal{B}}^{c}(E\times F,\mathbb{K})
with the topology of uniform convergence on products of bounded sets.

We also need the stability of Fréchet nuclear spaces under completed tensor products.

Proposition 1.2.10
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space. Then

\left(V^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\right)^{\prime}\simeq\left(V^{\prime}\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}

(1.9)

holds for any
k\geqslant 1
, where the duals are endowed with their strong topologies.

Proof.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space. The case
k=1
is trivial. Then Equation (
1.9
) with
k=2
holds by Equation (
1.8
)
with
E=F=V
. The cases
k\geqslant 2
are proved by induction, using
E=V^{\widehat{\otimes}k-1}
and
F=V
. Now, if
E
and
F
are two nuclear spaces then
E\widehat{\otimes}F
is a nuclear space (
[
Trè67
, Equation (50.9)]
). It is moreover complete since

E\widehat{\otimes}F
is obtained by completion. Thus the completed tensor product
E\widehat{\otimes}F
of two Fréchet nuclear spaces is a Fréchet nuclear space and the induction holds.
∎

A PROP for Fréchet nuclear spaces

We start by recalling the definition of distributions over a finite dimensional smooth manifold
{X}
. We quote
[
Hö89
, Definition 6.3.3]
.

Definition 1.2.11
.

To every coordinate system

\kappa:U_{k}\subset{X}\longrightarrow V_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
we associate
a distribution
u_{k}\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(V_{k})
such that

u_{k^{\prime}}=(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime-1})^{*}u_{k}

in
\kappa^{\prime}(U_{k}\cap U_{k^{\prime}})
; with
(\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime-1})^{*}u_{k}
the pullback of

u_{k}
by
\kappa\circ\kappa^{\prime-1}
whose existence and uniqueness
is given by

[
Hö89
, Theorem 6.1.2]
. Then the system
u_{k}
of distributions is called a distribution on
{X}
. The set of distributions on
{X}
is written

\mathcal{D}^{\prime}({X})
. Similarly we define
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}({X})
, the set of distributions with
compact support.

We can now state the properties that will allow us to obtain a PROP for infinite dimensional vector spaces. We state it without a rigorous proof but give precise reference to the existing proofs of the statements.

Proposition 1.2.12
.

\mathcal{E}({X})
is a Fréchet nuclear space and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}({X})
is a nuclear, but non Fréchet space.

The fact that
\mathcal{E}({X})
is Fréchet a classical result of functional analysis that the space of functions over a smooth manifold is Fréchet (see for example
[
vdBC13
, Exercise 2.3.2]
). The fact that it is nuclear is a folklore result, often stated without proof nor references and the only proof known to the author is in
[
BDLGR18
, p. 4]
. It then follows from Proposition
•
‣
1.2.5
, that the space
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(X)
is also nuclear. From Remark
1.2.8
the
space
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}(X)
is
not
Fréchet since the dual of a Fréchet space
F
is Fréchet if and only if
F
is Banach (see for example
[
Köt69
]
) which is not the case of
\mathcal{E}({X})
.

One further useful result is

Proposition 1.2.13
.

Let
{X}
and
{Y}
be two finite dimensional smooth manifolds. Then

\Hom^{c}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}({X}),\mathcal{E}({Y}))\simeq\,\mathcal{E}({X})\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{E}({Y})\simeq\mathcal{E}({X}\times{Y})

holds.

The second isomorphism
[
Gro52
, Chap. 5, p. 105]
can be proved using a version of the Schwartz kernel theorem for smoothing operators

[
vdBC13
, Theorem 2.4.5]
by means of the identification
\Hom^{c}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}({X}),\mathcal{E}({Y}))\simeq\mathcal{E}({X}\times{Y})
. The result then follows from
(
1.7
) applied to
\mathcal{E}(X)
and
\mathcal{E}(Y)
which are Fréchet nuclear spaces.

We can now introduce the spaces that will carry a PROP structure generalising the PROP
\Hom_{V}
of Subsection
1.2.1
to the infinite dimensional case.

Definition 1.2.14
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space. For any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, we set

\Hom_{V}^{c}(k,l)=\Hom^{c}(V^{\hat{\otimes}k},V^{\hat{\otimes}l})\simeq(V^{\prime})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\widehat{\otimes}V^{\widehat{\otimes}l},

where, as before
V^{\prime}
stands for the strong topological dual. Furthermore we set
\Hom^{c}_{V}:=(\Hom_{V}^{c}(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
.

For any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, let
\theta_{\sigma}
be the endomorphism of
V^{\otimes n}
defined by

\theta_{\sigma}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})=v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(n)}.

It extends to a continuous linear map

\overline{\theta_{\sigma}}
on the closure

V^{\widehat{\otimes}n}
.
For any
f\in\Hom_{V}^{c}(k,l)
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, we set:

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot f

\displaystyle=\overline{\theta_{\sigma}}\circ f,

\displaystyle f\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=f\circ\overline{\theta_{\tau}}.

In the above definition, the superscript “c” stands for continuous. As advertised, the family
\Hom_{V}^{c}
carries a PROP structure.

Theorem 1.2.15
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space.
\Hom_{V}^{c}
, with the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\mathrm{op}}
described above, is a PROP. Its horizontal
concatenation is the usual (topological) tensor product of maps with
I_{0}:\mathbb{K}\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
is the constant map
I_{0}(x):=1_{\mathbb{K}}
and
its vertical concatenation is the usual composition of maps and
I_{1}:V\longrightarrow V
is the identity map.

Proof.

The proof is exactly the same as the proof of Definition-Proposition
1.2.1
.
∎

Example 1.2.16
.

For a finite dimensional vector space
V
the classical PROP
\Hom_{V}
of Proposition-Definition

1.2.1
coincides with the the PROP
\Hom_{V}^{c}
.

Example 1.2.17
.

Let
U
be an open of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. From Example
1.2.7
and Equation
(
1.9
)
the family
({\mathcal{K}}_{U}(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
, with

{\mathcal{K}}_{U}(k,l)=\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(U)\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,\left({\mathcal{E}}(U)\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}l}

defines a PROP.

Example 1.2.18
.

Let
X
be a smooth finite dimensional manifold.
From Proposition
1.2.12
and Equation (
1.9
)
the family
({\mathcal{K}}_{X}(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
,
with
{\mathcal{K}}_{X}(k,l)=\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(X)\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,{\mathcal{E}}(X)^{\widehat{\otimes}l}

defines a PROP.

1.2.3
The PROP of graphs

A folklore result is that graphs have a PROP structure and are the free PROP. To rigorously prove the latter statement we need to precisely define the PROP structure that exists on a family of (generalised) graphs.

Generalised graphs

Definition 1.2.19
.

A
graph
is a family
G=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\alpha,\beta)
, where:

1.

V(G)
(set of vertices),
E(G)
(set of internal edges),
I(G)
(set of input edges),

O(G)
(set of output edges) and
IO(G)
(set of input-output edges) are finite (maybe empty) sets.

2.

s:E(G)\sqcup O(G)\longrightarrow V(G)
is a map (source map).

3.

t:E(G)\sqcup I(G)\longrightarrow V(G)
is a map (target map).

4.

\alpha:I(G)\sqcup IO(G)\longrightarrow[i(G)]
is a bijection, with
i(G)=|I(G)|+|IO(G)|

(indexation of the input edges).

5.

\beta:O(G)\sqcup IO(G)\longrightarrow[o(G)]
is a bijection, with
o(G)=|O(G)|+|IO(G)|

(indexation of the output edges).

Note that this definition differs from
[
PCP20
, Definition 1.3.1]
since here the loops of graphs will play no role, neither for PROPs nor for TRAPs.

Example 1.2.20
.

Here is a graph
G
:

\displaystyle V(G)

\displaystyle=\{x,y\},

\displaystyle E(G)

\displaystyle=\{a,b\},

\displaystyle I(G)

\displaystyle=\{c,d\},

\displaystyle O(G)

\displaystyle=\{e,f\},

\displaystyle IO(G)

\displaystyle=\{g\},

and:

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}a&\mapsto&y\\
b&\mapsto&x\\
e&\mapsto&y\\
f&\mapsto&y,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}a&\mapsto&x\\
b&\mapsto&y\\
c&\mapsto&x\\
d&\mapsto&x,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}c&\mapsto&1\\
d&\mapsto&2\\
g&\mapsto&3,\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\beta

\displaystyle:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}e&\mapsto&3\\
f&\mapsto&1\\
g&\mapsto&2.\end{array}\right.

This is graphically represented as follows:

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{a}
\textstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}

Definition 1.2.21
.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two graphs. An
(resp. iso-)morphism
of graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a family of (resp. bijections) maps
f=(f_{V},f_{E},f_{I},f_{O},f_{IO})
with:

\displaystyle f_{V}:V(G)\longrightarrow

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime}),\qquad\qquad f_{E}:E(G)\longrightarrow E(G^{\prime}),\qquad\qquad f_{I}:I(G)\longrightarrow I(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle f_{O}\,:\penalty\

\displaystyle O(G)\longrightarrow O(G^{\prime}),\qquad\qquad f_{IO}:IO(G)\longrightarrow IO(G^{\prime}),

such that:

\displaystyle s^{\prime}\circ f_{E}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ s_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle s^{\prime}\circ f_{O}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ s_{\mid O(G)},

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\circ f_{E}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ t_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle t^{\prime}\circ f_{I}

\displaystyle=f_{V}\circ t_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\circ f_{I}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}\circ f_{IO}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mid IO(G)},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\circ f_{O}

\displaystyle=\beta_{\mid O(G)},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}\circ f_{IO}

\displaystyle=\beta_{\mid IO(G)}.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote by
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
the space generated by the isoclasses of graphs
G
such that

i(G)=k
and
o(G)=l
, i.e.
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
is the quotient space of graphs with
k
input edges and
l
output edges by the equivalence relation given by isomorphism.

In what follows, we shall write
graphs
for
isoclasses of graphs
.

Example 1.2.22
.

The isomorphism class of the graph of Example
1.2.20
is represented by:

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

The PROP structure

We now want to equip the set
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
of isoclasses of graphs with a PROP structure.

•

Let us first define an action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
on graphs.
Let
G=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
.
Then:

\tau\cdot G\cdot\sigma={(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\sigma^{-1}\circ\alpha,\tau\circ\beta)}.

(1.10)

•

We now define the
horizontal concatenation
. If
G
and
G^{\prime}
are two disjoint graphs,
we define a graph
G*G^{\prime}
in the following way:

\displaystyle V(G*G^{\prime})=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime}),\qquad E(G*G^{\prime})=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle I(G*G^{\prime})=I(G)\sqcup

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime}),\qquad O(G*G^{\prime})=O(G)\sqcup O(G^{\prime}),\qquad IO(G*G^{\prime})=IO(G)\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}).

The source and target maps are given by:

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)\sqcup O(G)}

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})\sqcup O(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime},

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)\sqcup I(G)}

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})\sqcup I(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=t^{\prime}.

The indexations of the input and output edges are given by:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G)\sqcup IO(G)}

\displaystyle=\alpha,

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=i(G)+\alpha^{\prime},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G)\sqcup IO(G)}

\displaystyle=\beta,

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=o(G)+\beta^{\prime}

with an obvious abuse of notation in the definition of the second column.
Notice that this product is not commutative in the usual sense for
G*G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}*G
might differ by the indexation of their input and output
edges. However, it is commutative in the sense of Axiom
2c
of PROPs.
Roughly speaking,
G*G^{\prime}
is the disjoint union of
G
and
G^{\prime}
, the input and output edges of
G^{\prime}
being indexed
after the input and output edges of
G
.
G
1
k
\ldots
1
l
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle*\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G^{\prime}
1
k^{\prime}
\ldots
1
l^{\prime}
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle=\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
1
l
\ldots

G^{\prime}
k+1
k+k^{\prime}
\ldots
l+1
l+l^{\prime}
\ldots

.

This product of graphs induces a product
*:\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)\otimes\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})\longrightarrow\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k+k^{\prime},l+l^{\prime})
. If
G
,
G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
are three graphs, clearly

G*(G^{\prime}*G^{\prime\prime})=(G*G^{\prime})*G^{\prime\prime}.

Hence, the product
*
is associative. Its unit
I_{0}
is the unique graph such that

V(I_{0})=E(I_{0})=I(I_{0})=O(I_{0})=IO(I_{0})=\emptyset
.

•

We now define the
vertical concatenation
. Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be disjoint graphs such that
o(G)=i(G^{\prime})
. We define a graph

G^{\prime\prime}=G^{\prime}\circ G

in the following way:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime}),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e^{\prime})\in O(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\},

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G)\sqcup\{(f,e^{\prime})\in IO(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\},

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G)\sqcup\{(f,e^{\prime})\in O(G)\times IO(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\},

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=\{(f,e^{\prime})\in IO(G)\times IO(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e^{\prime})\}.

Its
source
and
target
maps are given by:

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)}

\displaystyle=s_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}((f,e^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=s(f),

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G)}

\displaystyle=t_{\mid E(G)},

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=s^{\prime}_{\mid E(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle t^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G)}

\displaystyle=s_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle s^{\prime\prime}((f,e^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=t^{\prime}(e^{\prime}).

The indexations of its input and output edges are given by:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}_{\mid I(G)}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{\mid I(G)},

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime\prime}((f,e))

\displaystyle=\alpha(f),

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})}

\displaystyle=\beta^{\prime}_{\mid O(G^{\prime})},

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}((f,e))

\displaystyle=\beta^{\prime}(e).

Roughly speaking,
G^{\prime}\circ G
is obtained by gluing together the outgoing edges of
G
and the incoming
edges of
G^{\prime}
according to their indexation.
G^{\prime}
1
l
\ldots
1
m
\ldots

\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
1
l
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle=\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

71.5pt\raise-66.47221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 101.5pt\raise-90.94443pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{4\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.05273pt\raise-64.38023pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

We can now state and prove the main result of this section.

Theorem 1.2.25
.

The family
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}=(\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}_{k,l})_{k,l\in\mathbb{N}}
, equipped with this
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-action and these horizontal and vertical concatenations,
is a PROP.

Proof.

•

We check the
associativity
of
\circ
. Let
G
,
G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
be three graphs with
o(G)=i(G^{\prime})
and
o(G^{\prime})=i(G^{\prime\prime})
.
The graphs
(G^{\prime\prime}\circ G^{\prime})\circ G
and
G^{\prime\prime}\circ(G^{\prime}\circ G)
may be different, but both are isomorphic to the graph
H

defined by:

\displaystyle V(H)

\displaystyle=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime})\sqcup V(G^{\prime\prime}),

\displaystyle E(H)

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime})\sqcup E(G^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\}\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G^{\prime})\times I(G^{\prime\prime}):\beta^{\prime}(f)=\alpha^{\prime\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,f^{\prime},e)\in O(G)\times IO(G^{\prime})\times I(G^{\prime\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(f^{\prime}),\beta^{\prime}(f^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle I(H)

\displaystyle=I(G)\sqcup\{(f,e)\in IO(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,f^{\prime},e)\in IO(G)\times IO(G^{\prime})\times I(G^{\prime\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(f^{\prime}),\beta^{\prime}(f^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle O(H)

\displaystyle=O(G^{\prime\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G^{\prime})\times IO(G^{\prime\prime}):\beta^{\prime}(f)=\alpha^{\prime\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,f^{\prime},e)\in O(G)\times IO(G^{\prime})\times IO(G^{\prime\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(f^{\prime}),\beta^{\prime}(f^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle IO(H)

\displaystyle=\{(f,f^{\prime},e)\in IO(G)\times IO(G^{\prime})\times IO(G^{\prime\prime}):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(f^{\prime}),\beta^{\prime}(f^{\prime})=\alpha^{\prime\prime}(e)\},

with immediate source, target and indexation maps. So
\circ
induces an associative product
\circ:\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(l,m)\otimes\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,m)
.

•

Let
I_{1}
be the graph such that

\displaystyle V(I_{1})

\displaystyle=E(I_{1})=I(I_{1})=O(I_{1})=\emptyset,

\displaystyle IO(I_{1})

\displaystyle=[1].

We show that
I_{1}
is the
unit
for
\circ
:
The indexation maps are both the identity of
[1]
.
For any integer
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
I_{1}^{*n}
is isomorphic to the graph
I_{n}
such that

\displaystyle V(I_{n})

\displaystyle=E(I_{n})=I(I_{n})=O(I_{n}))=\emptyset,

\displaystyle IO(I_{n})

\displaystyle=[n],

the indexation maps being both the identity of
[n]
. If
G
is a graph and
k=i(G)
, then
H=G\circ I_{k}

is the graph such that:

\displaystyle V(H)

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle I(H)

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha(e),e):e\in I(G)\},

\displaystyle E(H)

\displaystyle=E(G),

\displaystyle IO(H)

\displaystyle=\{(\alpha(e),e):e\in IO(G)\},

\displaystyle O(H)

\displaystyle=O(G),

with immediate source, target and indexation maps. This graph
H
is isomorphic to
G
, via the isomorphism given by:

\displaystyle f_{V}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{V(G)},

\displaystyle f_{I}((\alpha(e),e))

\displaystyle=e,

\displaystyle f_{E}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{E(G)},

\displaystyle f_{IO}((\alpha(e),e))

\displaystyle=e,

\displaystyle f_{O}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{O(G)}.

Similarly,
I_{l}\circ G
and
G
are isomorphic. Hence,
I_{1}
is the unit of
\circ
in
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
.

•

We check the
compatibility
of the horizontal and vertical concatenations.
Let
G
,
G^{\prime}
,
H
and
H^{\prime}
be graphs such that
o(G)=i(H)
and
o(G^{\prime})=i(H^{\prime})
. The graphs

(H*H^{\prime})\circ(G*G^{\prime})
and
(H\circ G)*(H^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime})
are both isomorphic to the graph
K
, such that:

\displaystyle V(K)

\displaystyle=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime})\sqcup V(H)\sqcup V(H^{\prime}),

\displaystyle E(K)

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime})\sqcup E(H)\sqcup E(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times I(H):\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G^{\prime})\times I(H^{\prime});\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle I(K)

\displaystyle=I(G)\sqcup I(G^{\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e)\in IO(G)\times I(H);\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,e)\in IO(G^{\prime})\times I(H^{\prime});\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle O(K)

\displaystyle=O(H)\sqcup O(H^{\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times IO(H);\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G^{\prime})\times IO(H^{\prime});\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle IO(K)

\displaystyle=\sqcup\{(f,e)\in IO(G)\times IO(H);\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\}

\displaystyle\sqcup\{(f,e)\in IO(G^{\prime})\times IO(H^{\prime});\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

with obvious source, target and indexation maps. Hence, the vertical and the horizontal concatenations are compatible.

•

We check the
module
structure of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
over the symmetric group.
Let
G
be a graph,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G)}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i(G)}
. We set:

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot G

\displaystyle=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\alpha,\sigma\circ\beta),

\displaystyle G\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\tau^{-1}\circ\alpha,\beta).

(1.11)

This induces a structure of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module over
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
.

•

Let us prove the compatibility of this action with the vertical concatenation. Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two graphs
such that
o(G)=i(G^{\prime})
, and let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G^{\prime})}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G)}
,

\nu\in\mathfrak{S}_{i(G)}
. Clearly, the graphs
\sigma\cdot(G^{\prime}\circ G)
and
(\sigma\cdot G^{\prime})\circ G
are equal;
the graphs
(G^{\prime}\circ G)\cdot\nu
and
G^{\prime}\circ(G\cdot\nu)
are equal. Let us compare the graphs

H=(G^{\prime}\cdot\tau)\circ G
and
H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}\circ(\tau\cdot G)
. Their set of vertices coincide. Moreover:

\displaystyle E(H)

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\beta(f)=\tau^{-1}\circ\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

\displaystyle E(H^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup E(G^{\prime})\sqcup\{(f,e)\in O(G)\times I(G^{\prime}):\tau\circ\beta(f)=\alpha^{\prime}(e)\},

so
E(H)=E(H^{\prime})
. Similarly,
I(H)=I(H^{\prime})
,
O(H)=O(H^{\prime})
,
IO(H)=IO(H^{\prime})
and
L(H)=L(H^{\prime})
.
Moreover, the source, target and indexation maps are the same for
H
and
H^{\prime}
, so
H=H^{\prime}
.

•

We prove the
compatibility
of the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-action with the horizontal composition.
Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two graphs,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G)}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G^{\prime})}
. We put

H=(\sigma\cdot G)*(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot G^{\prime})
and
H^{\prime}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(G*G^{\prime})
.
They have the same set of vertices, whether internal, input, output and input-output edges, and the source,
target and indexation of output edges maps for
H
and
H^{\prime}
coincide.
Both indexations of the set of output edges are given by:

\sigma^{\prime\prime}(e)=\begin{cases}\sigma\circ\beta(e)\mbox{ if }e\in O(G)\sqcup IO(G),\\
o(G)+\sigma^{\prime}\circ\beta^{\prime}(e)\mbox{ if }e\in O(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}).\end{cases}

So
H=H^{\prime}
.

•

Finally we prove the commutativity of
*
. Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be graphs. We set

H=c_{o(G),o(G^{\prime})}\cdot(G*G^{\prime})
and
H^{\prime}=(G^{\prime}*G)\cdot c_{i(G),i(G^{\prime})}
, where
c_{m,n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{m+n}
was defined in (
1.4
). They have the same sets of vertices, internal,
input, output and input-output edges, and the same source and target maps. The indexations maps are given by:

\displaystyle\alpha_{H}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha(e)+i(G^{\prime})\mbox{ if }e\in I(G)\sqcup IO(G),\\
\alpha^{\prime}(e)\mbox{ if }e\in I(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}),\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{H}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta(e)\mbox{ if }e\in O(G)\sqcup IO(G),\\
\beta^{\prime}(e)+o(G)\mbox{ if }e\in O(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}),\end{cases}

\displaystyle\alpha_{H^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha^{\prime}(e)\mbox{ if }e\in I(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}),\\
\alpha(e)+i(G^{\prime})\mbox{ if }e\in I(G)\sqcup IO(G),\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{H^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta^{\prime}(e)+o(G)\mbox{ if }e\in O(G^{\prime})\sqcup IO(G^{\prime}),\\
\beta(e)\mbox{ if }e\in O(G)\sqcup IO(G),\end{cases}

so
H=H^{\prime}
.

∎

1.3
Freeness of the PROP of graphs

In this Section we prove the folklore result that a free PROP can be described in terms of graphs. Notice that a free PROP was already build in
[
HR12
]
. However, this constrction of Hackney and Robertson is in the category of megagraphs. It is categorical in nature and thus not very well adapted to applications that are more down to earth, such as the ones we have in mind. Indeed, we will need a more explicit description of the free PROP.

1.3.1
Indecomposable graphs

It is well-known that free operads can be described by trees (see
[
GK94
]
or
[
BD16
]
). Thus it should be no suprise that free PROPs can be described by graphs. We now introduce the appropriate graphs.

Definition 1.3.1
.

We call a graph
G

indecomposable
if the four following conditions hold:

1.

V(G)\neq\emptyset
.

2.

IO(G)=\emptyset
.

3.

If
G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
are two graphs such that
G=G^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime\prime}
, then
V(G^{\prime})=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
.

4.

If
G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime\prime}
are two graphs and
\sigma
,
\tau
are two permutations such that

G=\sigma\cdot(G^{\prime}*G^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau
, then
V(G^{\prime})=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
.

For any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
, the subspace of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
generated by isoclasses of indecomposable graphs
G
with
i(G)=k

and
o(G)=l
is denoted by
\mathbf{Gr}^{\circlearrowright}_{\mathrm{ind}}(k,l)
.

Remark 1.3.2
.

The permutations in the fourth item of the definition of indecomposable graphs play an
important role: without them, one would allow for non connected graphs to be indecomposable, which can well happen when
the indexations of the inputs and outputs of the various connected components do not match. For
example, the graph

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{4}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

would be indecomposable. Permuting inputs we obtain

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{2}
\textstyle{3}
\textstyle{4}
\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{2\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{3\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

which is decomposable. The same requirement does not arise for the vertical concatenation since
one can write
\sigma.(P\circ Q).\tau=(\sigma.P)\circ(Q.\tau)=P^{\prime}\circ Q^{\prime}
.

Clearly,
\mathbf{Gr}^{\circlearrowright}_{\mathrm{ind}}
is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-submodule. Let us make this statement rigourous.

Proposition 1.3.3
.

Let
G
be a graph,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G)}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i(G)}
. Then

G
is indecomposable if, and only if,
\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
is indecomposable.

Proof.

Let us assume that
H=\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
is indecomposable. Then
V(G)=V(H)\neq\emptyset

and
IO(G)=IO(H)=\emptyset
. We are left to show that if
G=G^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime\prime}
or
G=\sigma\cdot(G^{\prime}*G^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau
, then
V(G^{\prime})=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
.

Let us assume that
G=G^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime\prime}
. Then:

H=\sigma\cdot(G^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau=(\sigma\cdot G^{\prime})\circ(G^{\prime\prime}\cdot\tau).

As
H
is indecomposable,
V(G^{\prime})=V(\sigma\cdot G^{\prime})=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime\prime})=V(G^{\prime\prime}\cdot\tau)=\emptyset
.
Let us now assume that
G=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(G^{\prime}*G^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau^{\prime}
. Then:

H=\sigma\cdot(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(G^{\prime}*G^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau^{\prime})\cdot\tau=((\sigma\sigma^{\prime})\cdot G^{\prime})*(G^{\prime\prime}\cdot(\tau\tau^{\prime})).

As
H
is indecomposable,
V(G^{\prime})=V\left((\sigma\sigma^{\prime})\cdot G^{\prime}\right)=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime\prime})=V(G^{\prime\prime}\cdot(\tau\tau^{\prime}))=\emptyset
.

Conversely, if
G
is indecomposable, then
G=\sigma^{-1}\cdot H\cdot\tau^{-1}
is indecomposable,
so by the first point
H
is indecomposable.
∎

In order to characterise indecomposable graphs, we need to introduce some notations for restrictions of graphs.

Notations 1.3.1
.

Let
G
be a graph.

1.

Let
J\subseteq V(G)
. We define (non uniquely due to the non uniqueness of the maps
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
) the graph
G_{\mid J}
by:

\displaystyle V(G_{\mid J})

\displaystyle=J,

\displaystyle E(G_{\mid J})

\displaystyle=\{e\in E(G):s(e)\in J,t(e)\in J\},

\displaystyle I(G_{\mid J})

\displaystyle=\{e\in I(G):t(e)\in J\}\sqcup\{e\in E(G):s(e)\notin J,t(e)\in J\},

\displaystyle O(G_{\mid J})

\displaystyle=\{e\in O(G):s(e)\in J\}\sqcup\{e\in E(G):s(e)\in J,t(e)\notin J\},

\displaystyle IO(G_{\mid J})

\displaystyle={\emptyset}.

The source and target maps are defined by:

\displaystyle\forall e\in E(G_{\mid J})\sqcup O(G_{\mid J}),

\displaystyle s_{G_{\mid J}}(e)

\displaystyle=s(e),

\displaystyle\forall e\in E(G_{\mid J})\sqcup I(G_{\mid J}),

\displaystyle t_{G_{\mid J}}(e)

\displaystyle=t(e),

The indexation of the input edges is any indexation map
\alpha^{\prime}
such that:

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\in\left(I(G)\sqcup IO(G)\right)\cap\left(I(G_{\mid J})\sqcup IO(G_{\mid J})\right),

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}(e)<\alpha^{\prime}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\alpha(e)<\alpha(e^{\prime}).

The indexation of the output edges is any indexation map
\beta^{\prime}
such that:

\displaystyle\forall f,f^{\prime}\in\left(O(G)\sqcup IO(G)\right)\cap\left(O(G_{\mid J})\sqcup IO(G_{\mid J})\right),

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(f)<\beta^{\prime}(f^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\beta(f)<\beta(f^{\prime}).

2.

We denote by
\tilde{G}
the graph obtained from
G
by deleting all its input-output edge. Rigorously,
\tilde{G}
is defined by:

\displaystyle I(\tilde{G})

\displaystyle=I(G),

\displaystyle O(\tilde{G})

\displaystyle=O(G),

\displaystyle IO(\tilde{G})

\displaystyle=\emptyset,

\displaystyle V(\tilde{G})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(\tilde{G})

\displaystyle=E(G),

\displaystyle\tilde{s}

\displaystyle=s,

\displaystyle\tilde{t}

\displaystyle=t.

The indexation of the input edges is the unique indexation map
\tilde{\alpha}
such that:

\displaystyle\forall e,e^{\prime}\in I(G),

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}(e)<\tilde{\alpha}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\alpha(e)<\alpha(e^{\prime}).

The indexation of the output edges is the unique indexation map
\tilde{\beta}
such that:

\displaystyle\forall f,f^{\prime\prime}\in O(G),

\displaystyle\tilde{\beta}(f)<\tilde{\beta}(f^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\beta(f)<\beta(f^{\prime}).

We also need to introduce the notions of paths and cycles of graphs.

Definition 1.3.4
.

Let
G
be a graph.

1.

A
path
in
G
is a sequence
p=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k})
of internal edges of
G
such that for any
i\in[k-1]
,

t(e_{i})=s(e_{i+1})
. The source of
p
is
s(e_{1})
and its target is
t(e_{k})
, and we shall say that

p
is a path from
s(e_{1})
to
t(e_{k})
of length
k
. By convention, for any
x\in V(G)
,
there exists a unique path from
x
to
x
of length
0
.

2.

We shall say that a path
p
is a
cycle
if its source and its target are equal
and if its length is nonzero.

We consider oriented-pathwise connected components of graphs.

Lemma 1.3.5
.

Let
G
be a graph such that
V(G)\neq\emptyset
. We denote by
\mathcal{O}(G)
the set of nonempty
subsets
I
of
V(G)
such that for any
x\in I
, for any
y\in V(G)
, if there exists a path in
G
from
x
to
y
,
then
y\in I
. Then:

1.

If
I,J\in\mathcal{O}(G)
, either
I\cap J=\emptyset
or
I\cap J\in\mathcal{O}(G)
.

2.

For any
x\in V(G)
, there exists a unique element
\langle x\rangle\in\mathcal{O}(G)
which contains
x

and is minimal for the inclusion. Moreover:

\langle x\rangle=\{y\in V(G):\mbox{ there exists a path in $G$ from $x$ to $y$}\}.

Notice that, for any
x\in V(G)
, if
G_{x}
is the connected component of
G
that contains
x
then
\langle x\rangle\subseteq G_{x}
, but we do not necessarily have an equality, as the edges are
oriented
.

Proof.

1.

If
I\cap J\neq\emptyset
, let
x\in I\cap J
and
y\in V(G)
such that there exists a path in
G
from
x
to
y
. As
I,J\in\mathcal{O}(G)
,
y\in I\cap J
, so
I\cap J\in\mathcal{O}(G)
.

2.

Note that
V(G)\in\mathcal{O}(G)
. Let
x\in V(G)
; by the first item, the following element of
\mathcal{O}(G)
is the minimal (for the inclusion) element of
\mathcal{O}(G)
that contains
x
:

\langle x\rangle=\bigcap_{I\in\mathcal{O}(G),\>x\in I}I.

On the one hand, a set
I
in
\mathcal{O}(G)
contains
x
if and only if any path emanating from
x
ends at an element of
I
. So it contains all the ending vertices of such paths and hence the set

I_{x}:=\{y\in V(G):\mbox{ there exists a path in $G$ from $x$ to $y$}\}.

Thus,
I_{x}\subseteq\langle x\rangle
since
\langle x\rangle\in\mathcal{O}(G)
.
On the other hand, let
y\in I
and
z\in V(G)
, such that there exists a path from
y
to
z
in
G
.
As there exists a path from
x
to
y
in
G
, there exists a path from
x
to
z
, so
z\in I_{x}
.
Hence,
I_{x}
lies in
\mathcal{O}(G)
and in turn contains
x
, so
\langle x\rangle\subseteq I_{x}
.

∎

As it turns out, every graph can be decomposed into indecomposable graphs.

Proposition 1.3.6
.

Let
G
be a graph such that
V(G)\neq\emptyset
. We denote by
J_{1},\ldots,J_{k}
the minimal elements
(for the inclusion) of the set
\mathcal{O}(G)
of nonempty
subsets
I
of
V(G)
stable under paths as in Lemma
1.3.5
, and we set
G_{i}=\tilde{G}_{\mid J_{i}}
for any
i\in[k]
.
Then
G_{1},\ldots,G_{k}
are indecomposable graphs and there exists a graph
G_{0}
, an integer
p
and a permutation
\gamma
such that:

G=(\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0}).

(1.12)

Such a decomposition will be called
minimal
.

Remark 1.3.7
.

The minimal decomposition of a graph is not unique. It depends on the indexation of the minimal elements of
\mathcal{O}(G)

and of the choice of the indexation of their input and output edges. Importantly, it only depends on that.

Proof.

By definition,
V(G_{i})=J_{i}\neq\emptyset
and
IO(G_{i})=\emptyset
for any
i
.
Let us assume that
G_{i}=G^{\prime}\circ G^{\prime\prime}
. If
V(G^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
, then clearly
V(G^{\prime})\in\mathcal{O}(G_{i})

and, as
J_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(G)
, we deduce that
V(G^{\prime})\in\mathcal{O}(G)
. As
J_{i}
is minimal in
\mathcal{O}(G)
,

V(G^{\prime})=J_{i}=V(G_{i})
, so
V(G^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
. Similarly, if
G_{i}=\sigma\cdot(G^{\prime}*G^{\prime\prime})\cdot\tau
,
then
V(G^{\prime})=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
: we proved that
G_{i}
is indecomposable.

Let us assume that
I=V(G_{i})\cap V(G_{j})\neq\emptyset
. Then by the first point of Lemma
1.3.5

I\in\mathcal{O}(G)
and, by minimality of
J_{i}
and
J_{j}
,

J_{i}=J_{j}=I
, so the
J_{i}
are disjoint.

Let us set
K:=V(G)\setminus(J_{1}\cup\ldots\cup J_{k})
and
G^{\prime}:=G_{\mid K}
. As
J_{1},\ldots,J_{k}
lie in
\mathcal{O}(G)
,
there is no internal edge of
G
from a vertex of
G_{i}
to a vertex of
G^{\prime}
, and any outgoing edge of
G^{\prime}
is either
glued in
G
to an incoming edge of
G_{i}
or is an outgoing edge of
G
. Hence,
there exist permutations

\gamma
,
\sigma
and
\tau
, and
two integers
p:=|IO(G)|
and
q:=|\{e\in I(G):t(e)\in J_{1}\cup\ldots\cup J_{k}\}|
such that:

G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ(\sigma\cdot(I_{q}*G^{\prime})\cdot\tau).

We conclude in taking
G_{0}=\sigma\cdot(I_{q}*G^{\prime})\cdot\tau
.
∎

We can finally fully characterise indecomposable graphs.

Proposition 1.3.8
.

Let
G
be a graph such that
V(G)\neq\emptyset
and
IO(G)=\emptyset
. The graph
G
is indecomposable if, and only if, for any
x,y\in V(G)
, there exists a path from
x
to
y
in
G
.

Proof.

First notice that if
|V(G)|=1
the result trivially holds. In the following, we therefore assume that
|V(G)|\geqslant 2
.

Let
G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0}
be a minimal decomposition of
G
. We prove the two directions of the implication separately.

\Longrightarrow
: Note that
V(G_{1})\neq\emptyset
. As
G
is indecomposable,
necessarily
V(G_{0})=\emptyset
, and there exists a permutation

\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{q}
such that
G_{0}=I_{q}\cdot\tau
. Therefore,

G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\cdot\tau
. As
G
is indecomposable,
k=1
and
V(G)=V(G_{1})=J_{1}
. Thus Proposition
1.3.6
implies that
V(G_{1})
is the unique minimal element of
\mathcal{O}(G)
for the inclusion. Furthermore, since any element of
\mathcal{O}(G)
is a subset of
V(G)=V(G_{1})
,
V(G_{1})
is also the maximal element for the inclusion of
\mathcal{O}(G)
. Consequently
\mathcal{O}(G)
is reduced to the singleton
\{V(G_{1})\}=\{V(G)\}
.

Therefore, by the second point of Lemma
1.3.5
,
for any
x\in V(G)
,
\langle x\rangle=V(G)
, so for any
y\in V(G)
,
there exists a path from
x
to
y
in
G
.

\Longleftarrow
:
If
k\geqslant 2
, there is no path in
G
from any vertex of
G_{1}
to any vertex of
G_{2}
, so
k=1
.
Thus,
V(G_{0})=\emptyset

and there exists a permutation
\tau
such that
G_{0}=I_{p}\cdot\tau
. We obtain that

G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*I_{p})\cdot\tau.

As
IO(G)=\emptyset
, we obtain that

p=0
, so
G=\gamma\cdot G_{1}\cdot\tau
is indecomposable by Proposition
1.3.3
.
∎

Remark 1.3.9
.

Another way to formulate the above Proposition is to say that a graph
G
is indecomposable if, and only if, for any pair
(x,y)
of its vertices,
a cycle of strictly positive length goes through
x
and
y
.

1.3.2
Free PROP

We now state and give a sketch of the proof of one of the main results of this section, namely the freeness of the PROP
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
.

Theorem 1.3.10
.

Let
P
be a PROP and
\phi:\mathbf{Gr}^{\circlearrowright}_{\mathrm{ind}}\longrightarrow P
be a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules.
There exists a unique PROP morphism
\Phi:\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}\longrightarrow P
such that
\Phi_{\mid\mathbf{Gr}^{\circlearrowright}_{\mathrm{ind}}}=\phi
.
In other words,
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
is the free PROP generated by
\mathbf{Gr}^{\circlearrowright}_{\mathrm{ind}}
.

Proof.

We provide here a sketch of the proof, and refer the reader to Subsection
1.3.3
for a full proof.
We define
\Phi(G)
for any graph
G
by induction on its number
n
of vertices. If
n=0
, there exists a permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
such that
G=\sigma\cdot I_{k}
. We set

\Phi(G)=\sigma\cdot I_{k}.

If
n>0
and
G
is indecomposable, we set
\Phi(G)=\phi(G)
. Otherwise,
let

G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0}

be a minimal decomposition of
G
. As
V(G_{1})\neq\emptyset
,
|V(G_{0})|<n
, we set:

\Phi(G)=\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}).

One can prove that this does not depend on the choice of the minimal decomposition of
G
with the help of the PROP axioms
applied to
P
. Using minimal decompositions of vertical or horizontal concatenations of graphs,
one can show that
\Phi
is compatible with both concatenations.
∎

The most technical parts of the proof described above is to find the minimal decompositions of
G*G^{\prime}
and
G^{\prime}\circ G
in terms of the minimal decompositions of
G
and
G^{\prime}
.

Now we see that we have made a step toward to our initial goal. Embedding Feynman graphs into our graphs given by Definition
1.2.19
, one obtains a Feynman rule
F:\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}\mapsto\mathcal{A}
if one can show:

1.

That the target algebra
\mathcal{A}
carries a PROP structure,

2.

That it exists a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules
f:\mathbf{Gr}^{\circlearrowright}_{\mathrm{ind}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{A}
.

While the first point can be conjectured for reasons that will be clarified below, the real issue lies with the second point. Indeed, an indecomposable graph can be arbitrarily complicated. Therefore if one were to use Theorem
1.3.10
to build a Feynman rule, one would still has to prove that the building block of the Feynman rule, i.e. the map
f
is well-defined and well-behaved on an infinity of complicated diagrams. We see that PROPs do not simplify enough our work for practical purposes.

There is a workaround, namely to work only with graphs without closed loops. In
[
PCP20
, Proposition 3.3.3]
it was shown that such graphs form a sub-PROP of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
which is the free PROP generated by a infinite family of simple graphs with only one vertices. One could then use the fact that the internal edges of any graph can have their orientation changed such that the obtained graph has no closed loops. It would still be necessary to show that the obtained evaluation of the Feynman rule on a graph with oriented loops does not depend on the choice made to remove the oriented loops.

While it seems like a tractable task, we have chosen not to follow this direction for at least three reasons:

Firstly, closed loops in Feynman graphs have a tendency to create singularities that should then be removed by renormalisation. It is now a commonly accepted stance that these singularities actually carry relevant information about the structure of the Feynman graph being regularised. Permuting the internal edges would darken the meaning of the singularities.

Secondly, proving that the target algebra
\mathcal{A}
has a PROP structure seems to be an even more formidable task than proving that it has a TRAP (see Section
1.4
below) structure.
