Thirdly, closed loops are so normal in QFT, so ingrained in the QFT culture that it seem a huge
faux pas
to not try to deal with them. And this is not mentioning the fact that this way to removing closed loops severely lacks elegance.

Therefore, in
[
PCP20
]
and
[
CLS22
]
we have instead chosen to introduce a new category of structure more suited for our goals. Nonetheless, before introducing and studying TRAPs, let us give a detailed proof of Theorem
1.3.10
.

Remark 1.3.11
.

Equivalent freeness results (for graphs and cycleless graphs) also hold for decorated and planar graphs: see
[
PCP20
, Theorems 3.4.3 and 4.1.4]
. These results are particularly important in the case of graphs decorated by a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\rm op}
-modules, where they allow to build an endofunctor of the category
\mathbf{PROP}
. Applied to the PROP
\Hom^{c}_{V}
for a Fréchet nuclear space
V
, this construction gives rise to a generalised composition of operators (
[
PCP20
, Corollaries 4.4.2 and 4.43]
). However, since according to the discussion above, PROPs are not the right structure for perturbative QFT, I have chosen not to include these results in this thesis.

1.3.3
Proof of freeness of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}

Here is a full proof of Theorem
1.3.10
.

Proof.

Let us define
\Phi(G)
for any graph
G
by induction on
n=|V(G)|
, such that for any permutation

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{i(G)}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G)}
,

\Phi(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau.

If
n=0
, there exists a unique permutation
\gamma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
such that
G=\gamma\cdot I_{k}
. We put

\Phi(G)=\gamma\cdot I_{k},

where we used the same notation
I_{k}
for the units of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
and
P
.

If
\sigma,\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\Phi(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=\Phi((\sigma\gamma)\cdot I_{k}\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=\Phi((\sigma\gamma\tau)\cdot I_{k})

\displaystyle=(\sigma\gamma\tau)\cdot I_{k}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\gamma\cdot I_{k})\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau

where we used the facts that
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
is an
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module and that, for any
k\geqslant 1
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
we have
I_{k}\cdot\tau=(I_{k}\cdot\tau)\circ I_{k}=I_{k}\circ(\tau\cdot I_{k})=\tau\cdot I_{k}
.
Let us assume that
\Phi(G^{\prime})
is defined for any graph
G^{\prime}
such that
|V(G^{\prime})|<n
.
Let

G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0}

be a minimal decomposition of
G
. If
G
is indecomposable, we set
\Phi(G)=\phi(G)
. Otherwise, as
V(G_{1})\neq\emptyset
,
|V(G_{0})|<n
. We put:

\Phi(G)=\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}).

Let us first prove that this does not depend on the choice of the minimal decomposition of
G
.
Starting from a minimal decomposition of
G
, one obtains all possible minimal decompositions
of
G
by a finite sequence of operations of type A and B:

•

Type A: changing the indexations of the input and output edges of the graphs
G_{i}
.
We obtain a minimal decomposition
G=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{k}*I_{p})\circ G^{\prime}_{0}
,
such that there exists permutations
\alpha_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
, with:

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}\cdot G_{i}\cdot\beta_{i},

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=(\beta_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\beta_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot G_{0},

\displaystyle{\gamma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle={\gamma}(\alpha_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p}).

•

Type B: permuting
G_{l}
and
G_{l+1}
for
l\in[k-1]
.
We obtain another minimal decomposition
G=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{k}*I_{p})\circ G^{\prime}_{0}
, with:

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{i}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}G_{l+1}\mbox{ if }i=l,\\
G_{l}\mbox{ if }{i}=l+1,\\
G_{i}\mbox{ otherwise};\end{cases}

\displaystyle G^{\prime}_{0}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{l-1})}\otimes c_{i(G_{l+1}),i(G_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{l+2})+\ldots+i(G_{k})+p}){\cdot}G_{0},

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{l-1})}\otimes c_{o(G_{l}),o(G{l+1})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{l+2})+\ldots+o(G_{k})+p}).

Let
G=\gamma\cdot(G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{k}*I_{p})\circ G^{\prime}_{0}
be another minimal decomposition of
G
.
Then it is enough to prove that

\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0})=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G_{1}^{\prime})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k}^{\prime})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}^{\prime}).

We can assume that this new minimal decomposition is obtained from the initial one by a single operation of type A or of type B.
If it is of type A:

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(\alpha_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot(\phi(\alpha_{1}\cdot G_{1}\cdot\beta_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\phi(\alpha_{k}\cdot G_{k}\cdot\beta_{k})*I_{p})

\displaystyle\circ\Phi((\beta_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\beta_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot G_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(\alpha_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot(\alpha_{1}\cdot\phi(G_{1})\cdot\beta_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{k}\cdot\phi(G_{k})\cdot\beta_{k}*I_{p})

\displaystyle\circ((\beta_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\beta_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot\Phi(G_{0}))\quad{\text{by the induction hypothesis}}

\displaystyle=\gamma(\alpha_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})(\alpha_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\alpha_{k}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot(\phi(G_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})

\displaystyle\circ(\beta_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\beta_{k}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})(\beta_{1}^{-1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\beta_{k}^{-1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{p})\cdot\Phi(G_{0})\quad{\text{since $\phi$ is a morphism of $\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}$-modules}}

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}).

If it is of type B:

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{l-1})}\otimes c_{o(G_{l}),o(G_{l+1})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{l+2})+\ldots+o(G_{k})+p})

\displaystyle\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{l+1})*\phi(G_{l})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})

\displaystyle\circ\Phi((\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{l-1})}\otimes c_{i(G_{l+1}),i(G_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{l+2})+\ldots+i(G_{k})+p})\cdot G_{0})

\displaystyle{=}\gamma(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{l-1})}\otimes c_{o(G_{l}),o(G_{l+1})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{l+2})+\ldots+o(G_{k})+p})

\displaystyle\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{l+1})*\phi(G_{l})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\quad{\text{by the induction hypothesis}}

\displaystyle\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{l-1})}\otimes c_{i(G_{l+1}),i(G_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{l+2})+\ldots+i(G_{k})+p})\cdot\Phi(G_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*c_{o(G_{l}),o(G_{l+1})}\cdot(\phi(G_{l+1})*\phi(G_{l}))\cdot c_{i(G_{l+1}),i(G_{l})}*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0})

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{l})*\phi(G_{l+1}))*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0})\quad{\text{by commutativity of }*}

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})\otimes\ldots\otimes\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}).

So
\Phi(G)
is well-defined. Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G)}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{i(G)}
. We put
H=\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
. Then
H
is indecomposable if, and only if,
G
is (Proposition
1.3.3
). For these graphs,
\Phi
coincides with
\phi
which is a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules by assumption. Otherwise, a
minimal decomposition of
H
is given by:

\displaystyle H_{0}

\displaystyle=G_{0}\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle H_{i}

\displaystyle=G_{i}\mbox{ if }i\in[k],

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma\gamma.

Hence:

\displaystyle\Phi(H)

\displaystyle=\sigma\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=\sigma\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0})\cdot\tau\quad{\text{by the induction hypothesis}}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}))\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau.

Consequently, we have defined a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules
\Phi:\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}\longrightarrow P
, extending the morphism
\phi
of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules.
Let us prove that it is compatible with both concatenations.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two graphs. Let us prove that
\Phi(G*G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime})
by induction on
n^{\prime}=|V(G^{\prime})|
.
If
n^{\prime}=0
, there exists
q\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{q}
such that
G^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot I_{q}
.
We proceed by induction on
n=|V(G)|
. If
n=0
, there exists
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}
, such that

G=\sigma\cdot I_{p}
. Then
G*G^{\prime}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot I_{p+q}
, and:

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot I_{p+q}

\displaystyle=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(I_{p}*I_{q})

\displaystyle=(\sigma\cdot I_{p})*(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot I_{q})\quad{\text{by compatibility of $\cdot$ and $*$}}

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

Otherwise (i.e. if
n>0
, still in the case
n^{\prime}=0
), let
G=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0}
be a minimal decomposition of
G
.
A minimal decomposition of
G*G^{\prime}
is:

G*G^{\prime}=(\gamma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p+q})\circ(G_{0}*I_{q}).

We can use the induction hypothesis on
G_{0}
: we have
|V(G_{0})|<|V(G)|
since otherwise
G
the graphs
G_{i}
would be of the form
\sigma_{i}.I_{p_{i}}
, which is in contradiction with the fact that the
G_{i}
are indecomposable (Proposition
1.3.6
) and the definition of indecomposable graphs (Definition
1.3.1
). So, using the induction hypothesis on
G_{0}
:

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p+q})\circ\Phi(G_{0}*I_{q})

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p}*I_{q})\circ(\Phi(G_{0})*I_{q})

\displaystyle{=(\gamma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})\circ\Phi(G_{0})*(I_{q}\circ I_{q})\quad\text{by compatibility of $*$ and $\circ$}}

\displaystyle=(\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}))*(\sigma^{\prime}\cdot I_{q})\quad{\text{by compatibility of $*$ and $\cdot$}}

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

So the result holds at rank
n^{\prime}=0
.

Let us assume that the results hold at any rank
{|V(G^{\prime})|}<n^{\prime}
. Let us consider minimal decompositions of
G
and
G^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0},

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{l}*I_{q})\circ G^{\prime}_{0},

with the convention
k=0
if
V(G)=\emptyset
. To obtain a minimal decomposition of
G*G^{\prime}
, let us set

a:=G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k},\qquad b:=G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{l}.

Then we have

\displaystyle G*G^{\prime}

\displaystyle{=\big(\gamma\cdot(a*I_{p})\circ G_{0}\big)*\big(\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(b*I_{q})\circ G^{\prime}_{0}\big)}

\displaystyle{=\big[\big(\gamma\cdot(a*I_{p})\big)*\big(\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(b*I_{q})\big)\big]\circ(G_{0}*G_{0}^{\prime})\quad\text{by compatibility of $*$ and $\circ$}}

\displaystyle{=\big[(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})\cdot(a*I_{p}*b*I_{q})\big]\circ(G_{0}*G_{0}^{\prime})\quad\text{by compatibility of $\cdot$ and $*$}}

\displaystyle{=\big[(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})\cdot\big(a*(c_{p,o(b)}\cdot(b*I_{p})\cdot c_{i(b),p})*I_{q}\big)\big]\circ(G_{0}*G_{0}^{\prime})\quad\text{by commutativity of $*$}}

\displaystyle{=\big[(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})(\mathrm{Id}_{o(a)}\otimes c_{p,o(b)}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})\cdot(a*b*I_{p+q})\cdot(\mathrm{Id}_{i(a)}\otimes c_{i(b),p}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})\big]\circ(G_{0}*G_{0}^{\prime})}

by compatibility of
\cdot
and
*
. Using the compatibility of
\cdot
and
\circ
and noticing that the numbers of inputs and outputs of
a
(resp.
b
) are given by the sum of the numbers of inputs and outputs of the
G_{i}
s (resp. of the
G^{\prime}_{j}
s) we obtain the following minimal decomposition of
G*G^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle G*G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{k})}\otimes c_{p,o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})

\displaystyle\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{l}*I_{p+q})

\displaystyle\circ((\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{k})}\otimes c_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+i(G^{\prime}_{l}),p}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q}))\cdot(G_{0}*G^{\prime}_{0})).

We apply the induction assumption
\Phi(G*G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime})
for
|V(G^{\prime})|<n^{\prime}
to
G^{\prime}_{0}
whose number of vertices is smaller than that of

G^{\prime}
and hence smaller than
n^{\prime}
for the same reason than the one given in the case
n^{\prime}=0
.

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{k})}\otimes c_{p,o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})

\displaystyle\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*I_{p+q})

\displaystyle\circ{\Phi\big((\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{k})}\otimes c_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+i(G^{\prime}_{l}),p}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})\cdot(G_{0}*G^{\prime}_{0})\big)}

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{k})}\otimes c_{p,o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})

\displaystyle\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*I_{p+q})

\displaystyle\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{k})}\otimes c_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+i(G^{\prime}_{l}),p}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q}))\cdot\Phi(G_{0}*G^{\prime}_{0})

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{k})}\otimes c_{p,o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q})

\displaystyle\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*I_{p}*I_{q})

\displaystyle\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{1})+\ldots+i(G_{k})}\otimes c_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\ldots+i(G^{\prime}_{l}),p}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{q}))\cdot(\Phi(G_{0})*\Phi(G^{\prime}_{0}))

\displaystyle=(\gamma\otimes\gamma^{\prime})\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p}*\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*I_{q})\circ(\Phi(G_{0})*\Phi(G^{\prime}_{0}))

\displaystyle=(\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}))*(\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*I_{q})\circ(\Phi(G^{\prime}_{0}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

So for any graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
we have
\Phi(G*G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime})
.

Now once again, let
G
,
G^{\prime}
be two graphs. Let us prove that
\Phi(G^{\prime}\circ G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})\circ\Phi(G)
.
We proceed by induction on
n=|V(G)|+|V(G^{\prime})|
. If
V(G^{\prime})=\emptyset
,
there exists
p\in\mathbb{N}
and a permutation
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{p}
such that
G^{\prime}=\sigma\cdot I_{p}
. Then:

\displaystyle\Phi(G^{\prime}\circ G)

\displaystyle=\Phi(\sigma\cdot G)=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)=\sigma\cdot(I_{p}\circ\Phi(G))=(\sigma\cdot I_{p})\circ\Phi(G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})\circ\Phi(G).

Similarly, if
V(G)=\emptyset
,
\Phi(G^{\prime}\circ G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})\circ\Phi(G)
. Thus we have proved the case
n=0
.

Let us assume that it holds up to rank
N
, and take
G
and
G^{\prime}
such that
n=N+1
. By the previous argument,

\Phi(G\circ G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)\circ\Phi(G^{\prime})
if
V(G)=\emptyset
or
V(G^{\prime})=\emptyset
.
We now assume that
V(G)
and
V(G^{\prime})
are nonempty. Let us work out the case where both
G
and
G^{\prime}
are indecomposable. Then
\Phi(G)=\phi(G)
and
\Phi(G^{\prime})=\phi(G^{\prime})
. Set
H:=G^{\prime}\circ G
. This is a minimal decomposition (Equation (
1.12
)) of
H
with
\gamma
being the identity,
k=1
,
p=0
,
H_{1}=G^{\prime}
and
H_{0}=G
. Then we have by definition of
\Phi

\Phi(H)=\phi(G^{\prime})\circ\Phi(G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})\circ\Phi(G).

For the general case, let
us consider minimal decompositions of
G
and
G^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\gamma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{k}*I_{p})\circ G_{0}

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{l}*I_{q})\circ G^{\prime}_{0}.

In
G^{\prime}\circ G
, the output edges of
G
are glued with an input or an input-output edge of
G^{\prime}
.
In particular, for any
i
, output edges of
G_{i}
are glued with input edges or input-output edges of
G^{\prime}
.
Up to a change of indexation of the
G_{i}
s we assume that there is some
r
such that:

•

For all
i\leqslant r
, at least one output edge of
G_{i}
is glued with an input edge of
G^{\prime}
.

•

If
i>r
, all output edges of
G_{i}
are glued with input-output edges of
G^{\prime}
.

Let us compute the minimal decomposition of
H:=G^{\prime}\circ G
.

A particular sub-case
. We assume that the input-output edges of
G^{\prime}
glued with an output of one of the

G_{i}
are the input edges of
G^{\prime}
with the greatest indices.
Then
G^{\prime}_{0}=G^{\prime\prime}_{0}*I_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k})}
(and
q\geqslant o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k})
) and
\gamma
decomposes into two permutations

\gamma=\gamma_{1}\otimes\gamma_{2},\qquad\gamma_{1}\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G_{1})+\ldots+o(G_{r})+p},\qquad\gamma_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k})}.

Let us write a minimal decomposition of
H

H=\gamma^{\prime\prime}\cdot(H_{1}*\cdots*H_{m}*I_{N})\circ H_{0}.

(1.13)

Then by minimality of the
H_{i}
, the hypothesis of the sub-case directly gives

(H_{1},\cdots,H_{m})=(G^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,G^{\prime}_{l},G_{r+1},\cdots,G_{k}).

Indeed, for
i\leqslant r
, there exists a path from
v\in V(G_{i})
to
v^{\prime}\in V(G^{\prime}_{0})\cup V(G^{\prime}_{j})
, so
G_{i}
is not minimal (Lemma
1.3.5
). This implies
N=q-o(G_{r+1})-\cdots-o(G_{k})
which is indeed positive.

Now, by the hypothesis of the subcase, the outputs indices of
\gamma_{2}\cdot(G_{r+1}*\cdots*G_{k})
are the highest. Therefore this term can go through
G^{\prime\prime}_{0}*I_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k})}
and we obtain

\displaystyle H=\gamma^{\prime}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})+N}

\displaystyle\otimes\gamma_{2})\cdot(G^{\prime}_{1}*\cdots*G^{\prime}_{l}*I_{N}*G_{r+1}*\cdots*G_{k})\circ(G^{\prime\prime}_{0}*I_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})

\displaystyle\circ(\gamma_{1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\cdot(G_{1}*\cdots*G_{r}*I_{p}*I_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\circ G_{0}.

One can straightforwardly check that this expression is well-defined. We have almost a minimal decomposition for
H
, we simply need to exchange
I_{N}
and
G_{r+1}*\cdots*G_{k}
. Using the commutativity of
*
we have

I_{N}*G_{r+1}*\cdots*G_{k}=c_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k}),N}\cdot(G_{r+1}*\cdots*G_{k}*I_{N})\cdot c_{N,G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})}.

Putting the
c_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k}),N}
out on the left and
c_{N,i(G_{r+1})+\cdots+i(G_{k})}
out on the right we obtain that the minimal decomposition (
1.13
) is given by

\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime\prime}=\gamma^{\prime}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})+N}\otimes\gamma_{2})

\displaystyle\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes c_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k}),N}),

\displaystyle(H_{1},\cdots,H_{m})

\displaystyle=(G^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,G^{\prime}_{l},G_{r+1},\cdots,G_{k}),

\displaystyle N=q

\displaystyle-o(G_{r+1})-\cdots-o(G_{k}),

\displaystyle H_{0}=(\mathrm{Id}_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+i(G^{\prime}_{l})}

\displaystyle\otimes c_{N,i(G_{r+1})+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\cdot(G^{\prime\prime}_{0}*I_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})

\displaystyle\circ(\gamma_{1}\otimes

\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\cdot(G_{1}*\cdots*G_{r}*I_{p+i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\circ G_{0}.

Since
l\geqslant 1
and the
G_{i}^{\prime}
s are indecomposable, we have
|V(H_{0})|=|V(G_{1})|+\ldots+|V(G_{r})|+|V(G_{0})|+|V(G_{0}^{\prime})|<n
, we can apply the induction hypothesis on
H_{0}
. Then we can undo all each of the manipulations we performed to obtain the minimal decomposition of
H_{0}
.

\displaystyle\Phi(H)

\displaystyle={\gamma^{\prime}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})+N}\otimes\gamma_{2})\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes c_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k}),N})}

\displaystyle\cdot((\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*\phi(G_{r+1}{)}*\ldots*\phi(G_{k}))

\displaystyle{\circ\Phi\Big((\mathrm{Id}_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+i(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes c_{N,i(G_{r+1})+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\cdot(G^{\prime\prime}_{0}*I_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})}

\displaystyle{\circ(\gamma_{1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\cdot(G_{1}*\cdots*G_{r}*I_{p+i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\circ G_{0}\Big)}\quad{\text{by definition of }\Phi}

\displaystyle{=\gamma^{\prime}\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})+N}\otimes\gamma_{2})\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{o(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+o(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes c_{o(G_{r+1})+\cdots+o(G_{k}),N})}

\displaystyle\cdot((\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*\phi(G_{r+1}{)}*\ldots*\phi(G_{k}))

\displaystyle{\circ(\mathrm{Id}_{i(G^{\prime}_{1})+\cdots+i(G^{\prime}_{l})}\otimes c_{N,i(G_{r+1})+\cdots+i(G_{k})})\cdot(\Phi(G^{\prime\prime}_{0})*I_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})}

\displaystyle{\circ(\gamma_{1}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{i(G_{r+1}+\cdots+i(G_{k})})}\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\cdots*\phi(G_{r})*I_{p+i(G_{r+1})+\cdots+i(G_{k})}{\circ}\Phi(G_{0})\quad{\text{by the ind. hyp.}}

\displaystyle=(\gamma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{k})*I_{p})\circ\Phi(G_{0}))\circ(\gamma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{l})*I_{q})\circ\Phi(G^{\prime}_{0}))

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)\circ\Phi(G^{\prime}).

For the second equality, we have also used that the
G_{i}
s are indecomposable and thus that
\Phi(G_{i})=\phi(G_{i})
by definition of
\Phi
. For the third equality we make the same steps than those giving the minimal decomposition of
G^{\prime}\circ G
.

General case
. There exists a permutation
\sigma
, such that if
H^{\prime}=G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{-1}

and
H=\sigma\cdot G
, then the condition of the particular sub-case holds for
(H,H^{\prime})
. Then:

\displaystyle\Phi(G^{\prime}\circ G)

\displaystyle=\Phi((G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{-1}\sigma)\circ G)

\displaystyle=\Phi((G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{-1})\circ(\sigma\cdot G))

\displaystyle=\Phi(G^{\prime}\cdot\sigma^{-1})\circ\Phi(\sigma\cdot G)\quad\text{since the subcase holds}

\displaystyle=(\Phi(G^{\prime})\cdot\sigma^{-1})\circ(\sigma\cdot\Phi(G))

\displaystyle=(\Phi(G^{\prime})\cdot{(\sigma^{-1}\circ\sigma))\circ}\Phi(G)

\displaystyle=\Phi(G^{\prime}){\circ}\Phi(G).

Finally, for any graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
,
\Phi(G\circ G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)\circ\Phi(G^{\prime})
and
\Phi
is a morphism of PROPs.
∎

1.4
The category of TRAPs

TRAPs (TRAces and Permutations) are objects introduced in
[
PCP20
]
in order to sove the issues with PROPs discussed. Their key feature is that they contain “trace maps” that allow to build closed loops. We start by describing the category of TRAPs.

1.4.1
Definition

There exists a categorical definition of TRAPs, but this definition requires the introduction of more sophisticated concept. Indeed, TRAPs cannot (to the best of the author’s knowledge) be described as a category, but instead as an algebra over a monad. Notice that this is true for PROPs as well. However, this definition is not very practical for applications. It was shown in
[
CLS22
]
that the category of unital TRAPs is isomorphic to the category of wheeled PROPs as introduced in
[
Mer06
]
(see also
[
YJ15
]
).

I will not include this result here and focus instead on non unitary TRAPs. Therefore, I will state only the pedestrian definition of TRAPs. This pedestrian definition largely overlaps with the Definition
1.1.9
of PROPs. The main difference is that the vertical concatenations (or compositions) are replaced with partial trace maps. We will show later (in Definition-Proposition
1.7.1
) that one can build these compositions from the partial traces maps.

Definition 1.4.1
.

A
TRAP
(TRAces and Permutations) is a family
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
of vector spaces, equipped with the following structures:

1.

P
is a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module.

2.

P
admits an
horizontal concatenation
, that is to say that for any
(k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
in
\mathbb{N}^{4}

, there is a map

\displaystyle*:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(k,l)\times P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})&\longrightarrow&P(k+k^{\prime},l+l^{\prime})\\
(p,p^{\prime})&\longmapsto&p*p^{\prime},\end{array}\right.

which is associative, commutative, unital and equivariant under the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}
.

3.

For any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
, there is a map

t_{i,j}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}P(k,l)&\longrightarrow&P(k-1,l-1)\\
p&\longmapsto&t_{i,j}(p),\end{array}\right.

(1.14)

called the
partial trace map
, such that:

(a)

(Commutativity). For any
k,l\geqslant 2
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
,
i^{\prime}\in[k-1]
,
j^{\prime}\in[l-1]
,

\displaystyle t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\circ t_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{i-1,j-1}\circ t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}<i,\>j^{\prime}<j,\\
t_{i,j-1}\circ t_{i^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime}}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}\geqslant i,\>j^{\prime}<j,\\
t_{i-1,j}\circ t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}<i,\>j^{\prime}\geqslant j,\\
t_{i,j}\circ t_{i^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime}+1}\mbox{ if }i^{\prime}\geqslant i,\>j^{\prime}\geqslant j.\end{cases}

(b)

(Equivariance). For any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k]
,
j\in[l]
,

\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,

t_{i,j}(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)=l_{j}(\sigma)\cdot(t_{\tau(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}(p))\cdot r_{i}(\tau),

with the following notation: if
\alpha\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
and
q\in[n]
, then

\left(l_{q}(\alpha),r_{q}(\alpha)\right)\in\mathfrak{S}^{2}_{n-1}
are defined by

\displaystyle l_{q}(\alpha)(s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha(s)\mbox{ if }s<\alpha^{-1}(q)\mbox{ and }\alpha(s)<q,\\
\alpha(s)-1\mbox{ if }s<\alpha^{-1}(q)\mbox{ and }\alpha(s)>q,\\
\alpha(s+1)\mbox{ if }s\geqslant\alpha^{-1}(q)\mbox{ and }\alpha(s+1)<q,\\
\alpha(s+1)-1\mbox{ if }s\geqslant\alpha^{-1}(q)\mbox{ and }\alpha(s+1)>q,\end{cases}

\displaystyle r_{q}(\alpha)(s)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha(s)\mbox{ if }s<q\mbox{ and }\alpha(s)<\alpha(q),\\
\alpha(s)-1\mbox{ if }s<q\mbox{ and }\alpha(s)>\alpha(q),\\
\alpha(s+1)\mbox{ if }s\geqslant q\mbox{ and }\alpha(s+1)<\alpha(q),\\
\alpha(s+1)-1\mbox{ if }s\geqslant q\mbox{ and }\alpha(s+1)>\alpha(q).\end{cases}

In other words, if we represent
\alpha
by the word
\alpha(1)\ldots\alpha(n)
, then

l_{q}(\alpha)
is represented by the word obtained by deleting the letter
q
and subtracting
1
to the letters

>q
, whereas
r_{q}(\alpha)
is represented by the word obtained by deleting the letter
\alpha(q)

and substracting
1
to the letters
>\alpha(q)
. Note that
r_{q}(\alpha)=l_{\alpha(q)}(\alpha)
.

(c)

(Compatibility with the horizontal concatenation).
For any
k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, for any
i\in[k+l]
,
j\in[k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}]
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
:

t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})=\begin{cases}t_{i,j}(p)*p^{\prime}\mbox{ if }i\leqslant k,\>j\leqslant l,\\
p*t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime})\mbox{ if }i>k,\>j>l.\end{cases}

The first two points of this definition are precisely the points 1, 2 and 6 of Definition
1.1.9
. Some remarks regarding this definition are in order.

Remark 1.4.2
.

The abuse of notation
t_{i,j}
is legitimate since a full notation such as
t_{i,j}^{k,l}
is not necessary in practice. Indeed the indices
k
and
l
in
t_{i,j}(p)
are entirely determined by the element
p
to which
t_{i,j}
is applied.

Quite a few can be said about the lack of unity in the above definition.

Remark 1.4.3
.

In
[
CLS22
]
, unital TRAPs were defined. Let
P
be a TRAP. A unit of
P
is an element
I\in P(1,1)
such that for any
p\in P
, the image under the partial trace maps of
I*p
and
p*I
is
p
up to the action of a simple permutation. These axioms are actually equivalent to

t_{1,2}(I*p)=p\qquad{\text{and}\quad t_{2,1}(I*p)=p},

(1.15)

for any
k,l\geqslant 1
and any
p\in P(k,l)
.

Here I do not introduce this axiom in more details for two reasons. Firstly, it would require later on more general graphs that the ones if Subsection
1.2.3
. Secondly, the TRAPs appearing when dealing with infinite dimensional vector spaces are typically non unital. Actually, unital TRAPs are useful to relate TRAPs and other structure: PROPs and wheeled PROPs. I will state the relation between TRAPs and PROPs since PROPs were introduced in this thesis before, but for the link between unital TRAPs and wheeled PROPs I will simply refer the reader to
[
CLS22
, Theorem 5.3.1]
.

Notice that one could in principle add a formal unit
I
to any TRAP, but then one also needs to describe
t_{1,1}(I)
and concatenation with this element. This make the TRAP much more challenging to describe, as can be seen in
[
CLS22
]
where the unital version of the TRAP of graphs to be described below requires more complicated objects than the generalised graphs described in Subsection
1.2.3
. Another issue with adding a formal unit is that in some spaces relevant to the theory and application of TRAPs, we do
have
units, just that the partial traces are not defined on them. This happens for example for spaces of operators. The identity is the usual identity operator, but the trace of the identity is not defined in general. So in this case, the identity cannot be an element of the TRAP. Adding another, formal identity seems rather artficial.

Remark 1.4.4
.

One could also point out that we gave only a pedestrian definition of TRAPs, and that a concise definition in the fashion of
1.1.7
is still lacking. However, it was recently pointed out to me that wheeled PROPs are a special case of traced monoidal categories
[
JSV96
]
. Since TRAPs are essentially wheeled PROPs, they can probably be consisely defined via traced monoidal categories, at least in their unital version. Since should be explored further in future work. I am thankful to Vladimir Dotsenko for pointing out traced monoidal categories to me.

Finally, one should notice that TRAPs and PROPs are related concepts.

Remark 1.4.5
.

It should be clear from this definition that one can define a vertical concatenation, or composition, by iterating the partial traces. This is postponed to Subsection
1.7.1
, where we will show that one can build a PROP from a unital (see above) TRAP: see Proposition
1.7.1
and Remark
1.7.2
.

Before introducing morphisms of TRAPs, here is one simple but useful Lemma that allows to simplify the axioms of TRAPs.

Lemma 1.4.6
.

Let
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module, equipped with a horizontal concatenation
*

which is associative, commutative, unital and equivariant under the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}
, and with maps
t_{i,j}
satisfying axioms 3. (a)-(b) of Definition
1.4.1
.

Assuming that for any
k,l,k^{\prime},l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
, for any
p\in P(k,l)
,
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
,

t_{1,1}(p*p^{\prime})=t_{1,1}(p)*p^{\prime},

then Axiom 3.(c) of Definition
1.4.1
is satisfied.

Proof.

Let
p\in P(k,l)
and
p^{\prime}\in P(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
. We take
i
in
[k+l]
,
j
in
[k^{\prime}+l^{\prime}]
and define the transpositions
\sigma=(1,j)
,
\tau=(1,i)
, with the convention
(1,1)=\mathrm{Id}
. We use the notation
\sigma_{j}:=l_{j}(\sigma)
and
\tau_{i}:=r_{i}(\tau)
. Let us consider several cases.

•

If
i\leqslant k
and
j\leqslant l
, then:

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}(\sigma^{2}\cdot(p*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau^{2})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot t_{1,1}(\sigma\cdot(p*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau)\cdot\tau_{i}\quad\text{by equivariance of $t_{i,j}$}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{1,1}((\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)*p^{\prime}))\cdot\tau_{i}\quad\text{since $i\leqslant k$, $j\leqslant l$ and by equivariance of $*$}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{1,1}(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)*p^{\prime})\cdot\tau_{i}\quad\text{by the assumption of the Lemma,}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{j}\cdot(t_{1,1}(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)\cdot\tau_{i}))*p^{\prime}\quad\text{since $i\leqslant k$, $j\leqslant l$ and by equivariance of $*$}

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}(p)*p^{\prime}\quad\text{by equivariance of $t_{i,j}$.}

•

If
i>k
and
j>l
, using
c_{m,n}^{-1}=c_{n,m}
, and as before writing
(c_{l^{\prime},l})_{j}:=l_{j}(c_{l^{\prime},l})
and
(c_{k,k^{\prime}})_{i}:=r_{i}(c_{k,k^{\prime}})
we have

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(p*p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}(c_{l^{\prime},l}\cdot(p^{\prime}*p)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}})\quad\text{by commutativity of $*$}

\displaystyle=(c_{l^{\prime},l})_{j}\cdot t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime}*p)\cdot(c_{k,k^{\prime}})_{i}\quad\text{by equivariance of $t_{i,j}$}

\displaystyle=c_{l^{\prime}-1,l}\cdot(t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime})*p)\cdot c_{k,k^{\prime}-1}\quad\text{By the first point,}

\displaystyle=p*t_{i-k,j-l}(p^{\prime})\quad\text{by commutativity of $*$}.

Thus Axiom
3.(c)
is satisfied.
∎

1.4.2
Morphisms of TRAPs

The definition of morphisms of TRAPs, though somewhat complicated, is quite natural.

Definition 1.4.7
.

Let
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
and
Q=(Q(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be two TRAPs with partial trace maps
(t_{i,j}^{P})_{i,j\geqslant 0}
and
(t_{i,j}^{Q})_{i,j\geqslant 0}

respectively. A
morphism of TRAPs
is a family
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}

of morphisms of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules
\phi(k,l):P(k,l)\longrightarrow Q(k,l)
which are compatible with the horizontal concatenation,
and the partial trace maps. More precisely, for any
(k,l,m,n)\in\mathbb{N}^{4}
:

1.

For any
(\sigma,p,\tau)
in
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times P(k,l)\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
\phi(k,l)(\sigma\cdot p\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\phi(k,l)(p)\cdot\tau
.

2.

\phi(0,0)(I_{0})=I_{0}
.

3.

\forall(p,q)\in P(k,l)\times P(n,m),\penalty\ \phi(k+n,l+m)(p*q)=\phi(k,l)(p)*\phi(n,m)(q)
,

4.

\forall(p,i,j)\in P(k,l)\times[k]\times[l]
,
\phi(k-1,l-1)\circ t^{P}_{i,j}(p)=t^{Q}_{i,j}\circ\phi(k,l)(p)
.

With a slight abuse of notation, we write
\phi(p)
instead of
\phi(k,l)(p)
for
p\in P(k,l)
.
We denote by
\mathbf{TRAP}
the category of TRAPs and TRAPs morphisms.

In the same way that Lemma
1.4.6
simplifies the axioms of a TRAP, we can also simplify the axioms for morphisms of TRAPs.

Lemma 1.4.8
.

Let
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
and
Q=(Q(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be two TRAPs and
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be a family of set maps
\phi(k,l):P(k,l)\longrightarrow Q(k,l)
satisfying Points 1-3 of Definition
1.4.7
. Suppose further that for any
k,l\geqslant 1
, for any
p\in P(k,l)

t_{1,1}\circ\phi(p)=\phi\circ t_{1,1}(p).

Then
\phi
is a map of TRAPs.

Proof.

If
i
,
j
and
p
lies respectively in
[k]
,
[l]
, and
P(k,l)
, then

\displaystyle\phi\circ t_{i,j}(p)

\displaystyle=\phi\circ t_{i,j}((1,j)^{2}\cdot p\cdot(1,i)^{2})

\displaystyle=\phi\left({l_{j}(1,j)}\cdot t_{1,1}((1,j)\cdot p\cdot(1,i))\cdot{r_{i}(1,i)}\right)\quad\text{by equivariance of $t_{i,j}$ (Axiom $3b${ )}}

\displaystyle={l_{j}(1,j)}\cdot\phi\circ t_{1,1}((1,j)\cdot p\cdot(1,i))\cdot{r_{i}(1,i)}\quad\text{by Point 1 of Definition \ref{defn:trap_morphism},}

\displaystyle={l_{j}(1,j)}\cdot t_{1,1}\circ\phi((1,j)\cdot p\cdot(1,i))\cdot{r_{i}(1,i)}\quad\text{by the assumption of the Lemma,}

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}((1,j)\cdot\phi((1,j)\cdot p\cdot(1,i))\cdot(1,i))\quad\text{by equivariance of $t_{i,j}$ }

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}\circ\phi(p)\quad\text{by Point 1 of Definition \ref{defn:trap_morphism},},

with the convention
(1,1)=\mathrm{Id}
. It follows that
\phi
is a morphism of TRAPs. ∎

In particular, to show that a collection of linear maps between two TRAPs preserving
the horizontal concatenation and the actions of the symmetry group is a morphism of TRAPs, it is enough to check
the properties of Lemma
1.4.8
.

Finally, let us point out that the category
\mathbf{TRAP}
admits a natural notion of subobjects (see
[
ML98
, Chapter V.7]
).

Definition 1.4.9
.

We define a
sub-TRAP
of a TRAP
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
to be a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-submodule
Q=(Q(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
of
P
which contains the unit
I_{0}\in P(0,0)
and is stable under the partial trace map of
P
.

1.5
Examples of TRAPs

We give here three examples of TRAPs that are relevant for analysis in infinite dimensions, as a first step toward QFT.

1.5.1
The TRAP of linear morphisms

The most basic example of TRAP is on the same space than the most basic example of PROP which was introduced in Subsection
1.2.1
. Let us recall that for a finite dimensional vector space
V
and its algebraic dual
V^{*}
we consider the family

\Hom_{V}=\left(\Hom_{V}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\geqslant 0}:={(\Hom(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l}))_{k,l\geqslant 0}},

where for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
\Hom(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l})
is the vector space of linear maps from
V^{\otimes k}
to
V^{\otimes l}
.

We shall identify
\Hom_{V}(k,l)
and
{V^{*\otimes k}\otimes V^{\otimes l}}
through the isomorphism

\theta_{k,l}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}{V^{*\otimes k}\otimes V^{\otimes l}}&\longrightarrow&\Hom_{V}(k,l)\\
{f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l}}&\longmapsto&\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}V^{\otimes k}&\longrightarrow&V^{\otimes l}\\
x_{1}\ldots x_{k}&\longmapsto&f_{1}(x_{1})\ldots f_{k}(x_{k})\,v_{1}\ldots v_{l},\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

where with some abuse of notation, we have set
f_{1}\cdots f_{k}:=f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{k}\in V^{*\otimes k}
and

v_{1}\cdots v_{l}:=v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{l}\in V^{\otimes l}
.

As in Subsection
1.2.1

\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\rm op}
-module structure is defined first from the fact that for any vector space
W
, the tensor power
W^{\otimes k}
is a left
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
-module
with the action defined by

\sigma\cdot w_{1}\ldots w_{k}=w_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\ldots w_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}.

Furthermore, via the identification
\theta:=\left(\theta_{k,l}\right)_{k,l\geqslant 0}
, we can equip the family

\Hom_{V}=(\Hom(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l}))_{k,l\geqslant 0}

with the structure
of a
\mathfrak{S}_{l}\times\mathfrak{S}_{k}^{op}
-module by putting, for an
f\in\Hom(V^{\otimes k},V^{\otimes l})
,
for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\times\mathfrak{S}_{l}
:

\forall v_{1}\ldots v_{k}\in V^{\otimes l},\tau\cdot f\cdot\sigma(v_{1}\ldots v_{k})=\tau\cdot f(\sigma\cdot v_{1}\ldots v_{k}).

(1.16)

The horizontal concatenation is the same as the one introduced in Subsection
1.2.1
, i.e. the usual tensor product of linear maps:
if
f\in\Hom_{V}(k,l)
and
g\in\Hom_{V}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
, then

f\otimes g:\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}V^{\otimes(k+k^{\prime})}&\longrightarrow&V^{\otimes(l+l^{\prime})}\\
v_{1}\ldots v_{k+k^{\prime}}&\longmapsto&f(v_{1}\ldots v_{k})\otimes g(v_{k+1}\ldots v_{k+k^{\prime}}).\end{array}\right.

And as before its unit is the unit of the underlying field
I_{0}:=1
.

Finally we define the partial trace maps as

t_{i,j}(\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l}))=f_{i}(v_{j})\,\theta_{k-1,l-1}(f_{1}\ldots f_{i-1}f_{i+1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{j-1}v_{j+1}\ldots v_{l}).

(1.17)

Remark 1.5.1
.

Notice that for
p\in\Hom_{V}(1,1)
,
t_{1,1}(p)
coincides with the usual trace of a linear map on
V
. Proposition
1.7.7
below generalises the notion of trace to any element of
\Hom_{V}
and to any TRAP.

Proposition 1.5.2
.

For a finite dimensional
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
V
, the above construction equips
\Hom_{V}
with a TRAP structure.

Remark 1.5.3
.

\Hom_{V}
is actually a unitary TRAP, with unit given by the identity map
Id_{V}\in\Hom_{V}(1,1)
. This actually shows that
\Hom_{V}
is a wheeled PROP, a fact that was used in the context of invariant theory (see
[
DM19
]
) and algebraic topology (see
[
KV21
]
).

Proof.

Properties 1. and 2. are trivially satisfied, with
I_{0}=1\in\mathbb{K}
.

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(\sigma\cdot\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l})\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=t_{i,j}\circ\theta_{k,l}(f_{\tau(1)}\ldots f_{\tau(k)}\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\ldots v_{\sigma^{-1}(l)})

\displaystyle=f_{\tau(i)}(v_{\sigma^{-1}(j)})\theta_{k-1,l-1}(f_{\tau(1)}\ldots f_{\tau(i-1)}f_{\tau(i+1)}\ldots f_{\tau(k)}

\displaystyle\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\ldots v_{\sigma^{-1}(j-1)}v_{\sigma^{-1}(j+1)}\ldots v_{\sigma^{-1}(l)})

\displaystyle=\sigma_{j}\cdot t_{\tau(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}\theta_{k,l}(f_{1}\ldots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\ldots v_{l})\cdot\tau_{i}.

Property 3.(c) is straightforward from the definition and Lemma
1.4.6
. Thus
\Hom_{V}
is a TRAP.
∎

When
V
is not finite-dimensional,
\theta
is an injective, non surjective map. Its range is the subspace
\Hom_{V}^{fr}
of linear
maps from
V^{\otimes k}
to
V^{\otimes l}
of finite rank. We can equip
\Hom_{V}^{fr}
with a similar TRAP structure:

Proposition 1.5.4
.

With the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
action defined by (
1.16
), the usual tensor product of maps
and the partial trace maps defined by (
1.17
),
\Hom_{V}^{fr}
is a TRAP.

Remark 1.5.5
.

The TRAP
\Hom_{V}^{fr}
is unitary if, and only if,
V
is finite-dimensional. This justifies the previous statement that non unitary TRAPs are the objects adapted to analysis on infinite dimensional spaces.

Let us show that if
\Hom_{V}^{fr}
has a unit then
V
is finite dimensional . Assume
I
is the unit of
P
, i.e. Equation (
1.15
) holds. Then
I
has finite rank, let us fix a basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{k})
of
\mathrm{Im}({I})
. There exist
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k}\in V^{*}
such that for any
v\in V
,

I(v)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(v)e_{i}.

In other words,

I=\theta_{1,1}\left({\sum_{i=1}^{k}e_{i}\otimes\lambda_{i}}\right).

Let
v\in V
, nonzero, and let
\lambda\in V^{*}
such that
\lambda(v)=1
. We consider
f=\theta_{1,1}(v\otimes\lambda)
.
Then
f(v)=\lambda(v)v=v
. Moreover:

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=f(v)=t_{1,2}(I*f)(v)

\displaystyle=t_{1,2}\circ\theta_{2,2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{e_{i}}\,v\otimes\lambda_{i}\lambda\right)(v)

\displaystyle=\theta_{1,1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}{\lambda_{i}}{(v)\,e_{i}}\otimes{\lambda}\right)(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda(v)\lambda_{i}(v)\,{e_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}(v)\,{e_{i}},

We end this paragraph with an example of a TRAP similar to the TRAP
\Hom_{V}
but of a more geometric nature.

Example 1.5.6

(The TRAP of tensors)
.

Given a finite dimensional smooth manifold
M
and a point
x\in M
, we build the
\Hom
-TRAP
\Hom_{T_{x}M}
where
T_{x}M
is the tangent space to
M
at the point
x
.
Given a pair
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
we have, using the musical isomorphisms (see for example
[
Lee97
, Chapter 3]
)

\Hom_{T_{x}M}(p,q)\simeq(T_{x}^{*}M)^{\otimes p}\otimes T_{x}M^{\otimes q},

where we have set
V^{\otimes 0}=\mathbb{R}
. The partial trace maps are built by pairing cotangent and tangent vectors.
We note that if
M
is equipped with a Riemannian metric, thanks to the musical isomorphisms
T_{x}^{*}M\ni\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\sharp}\in T_{x}M
and
T_{x}M\ni v\longmapsto v^{\flat}\in T_{x}^{*}M
between
T_{x}^{*}M
and
T_{x}M
, these dual pairings can be seen as contractions via the metric tensor.

This yields a smooth fibration
\Hom_{TM}:=\{\Hom_{T_{x}M},x\in M\}
of TRAPs parametrised by
M
. For any
(p,q)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
, a smooth section of
\Hom_{TM}(p,q)
defines a smooth
(p,q)
tensor on
M
.

This example suggests an interesting open question: could one define TRAPs on rings and modules? Provided one would need to such structures, it seems that at least some of the properties of TRAPs would still be valid on this more general setting.

1.5.2
The TRAP of continuous morphisms

Still following the same plan as the sections presenting PROPs, we proceed to generalise the TRAP
\Hom_{V}
to infinite dimensional vector spaces by replacing
V^{\otimes k}
in
\Hom_{V}
by Grothendieck’s nuclear spaces
[
Gro52
,
Gro54
]
already introduced in Subsection
1.2.2
.

Recall that the topological dual
E^{\prime}
of a locally convex topological vector space
E
can be endowed with various topologies, one of which is
the
strong topology
, namely the topology of uniform convergence on the bounded domains of
E
. It is generated by the family of semi-norms of
E^{\prime}
defined on any
f\in E^{\prime}
by
||f||_{B}:=\sup_{x\in B}|f(x)|

for any bounded set
B
of
E
(Equation (
1.5
)). The topological dual
E^{\prime}
endowed with this topology is called the
strong dual
.

In the following
E
and
F
are two topological vector spaces and
\Hom^{c}(E,F)
is the set of continuous linear maps from
E
to
F
.

Let us recall Definition
1.2.14
since we use it once again here. For a Fréchet nuclear space
V
we set

\Hom_{V}^{c}(k,l)=\Hom^{c}(V^{\hat{\otimes}k},V^{\hat{\otimes}l})\simeq(V^{\prime})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\widehat{\otimes}V^{\widehat{\otimes}l},

(1.18)

for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
and where
V^{\prime}
stands for the strong topological dual.

Furthermore, for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we define
\theta_{\sigma}
the endomorphism of
V^{\otimes n}
defined by

\theta_{\sigma}(v_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{n})=v_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\ldots\otimes v_{\sigma^{-1}(n)}

which extends to a continuous linear map

\overline{\theta_{\sigma}}
on the closure

V^{\widehat{\otimes}n}
.
For any
f\in\Hom_{V}^{c}(k,l)
,
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
, we had then set:

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot f

\displaystyle=\overline{\theta_{\sigma}}\circ f,

\displaystyle f\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=f\circ\overline{\theta_{\tau}}.

On top of its PROP structure build and proved in Subsection
1.2.2
, the family
\Hom_{V}^{c}
carries a TRAP structure.

Theorem 1.5.7
.

Let
V
be a Fréchet nuclear space.
\Hom_{V}^{c}
, with the action of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\mathrm{op}}
described above, is a TRAP. Its horizontal
concatenation is the usual topological tensor product of maps with
I_{0}:\mathbb{K}\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
given by the
identity map of
\mathbb{K}
, its partial trace maps coincide with those of the TRAP
\Hom_{V}
on elements of
(V^{\prime})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\widehat{\otimes}V^{\widehat{\otimes}l}

t_{i,j}(f_{1}\cdots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\cdots v_{l})=f_{i}(v_{j})f_{1}\cdots f_{i-1}f_{i+1}\cdots f_{k}\otimes v_{1}\cdots v_{j-1}v_{j+1}\cdots v_{l}

with the same notations as in Subsection
1.5.1
.

Remark 1.5.8
.

The TRAP
\Hom_{V}^{c}
is unitary if, and only if,
V
is finite dimensional. This is because the unit would be the identity of
V
, but
t_{1,1}(Id_{V})=\Tr(V)
is only defined when
V
is finite dimensional. Here the issue is with the completion of the tensor product. The identity of
V
is mapped by the isomorphism of Equation
1.7.1
to an infinite series of tensor powers, and the partial trace map
t_{1,1}
is not defined on this object. Once again we see that non unitary TRAP is the right structure when dealing with infinite dimensional spaces.

Proof.

The proof of the TRAP structure of
\Hom^{c}_{V}
goes as in Proposition
1.5.2
. The proof of the unital case is the same as the proof of Proposition
1.5.4
.
∎

Example 1.5.9
.

For a finite dimensional vector space
V
, the TRAP
\Hom_{V}^{c}
coincides with the TRAP
\Hom_{V}
of Subsection
1.5.1
.

Example 1.5.10
.

Let
M
be a smooth finite dimensional manifold.
From Proposition
1.2.13
and Equation (
1.9
), it follows that
the family
({\mathcal{K}}_{M}(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
,
with
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}(k,l)=\left({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(M)\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\,\widehat{\otimes}\,{\mathcal{E}}(M)^{\widehat{\otimes}l}

defines a TRAP.

1.5.3
The TRAP of smoothing pseudo-differential operators

We apply our results on TRAPs to tensor products
of Fréchet spaces
{\mathcal{E}}(M)
of smooth functions on a given smooth finite dimensional orientable closed manifold
M
and
\mu(z)
a volume form on
M
. From now on, we work with vector space over
\mathbb{C}
. Recall from Proposition

1.2.13
that such spaces are stable under tensor products and morphisms in
\mathrm{Hom}^{c}({\mathcal{E}}^{\prime}(M),{\mathcal{E}}(N))
are determined by smoothing kernels in
{\mathcal{E}}(M\times N)
.

We consider smooth kernels
which stabilise
{\mathcal{E}}(M)
and set, for
(k,l)\neq(0,0)
:

{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l):={\mathcal{E}}({M}^{k}\times{M}^{l})\simeq{\mathcal{E}}({M})^{\widehat{\otimes}k}\,\hat{\otimes}\,{\mathcal{E}}({M})^{\widehat{\otimes}l},

(1.19)

whose elements we refer to as smooth generalised kernels. We also set
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(0,0)\simeq\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}:=\left({\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\geqslant 0}
. Notice that
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}
has the structure of a
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\rm op}
-module from the construction of the previous subsection.

Theorem 1.5.11
.

Let
M
be a smooth finite dimensional orientable closed manifold. The family of topological vector spaces
\left({\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\geqslant 0}
can be equipped with the partial trace maps
t_{i,j}:{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\longrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k-1,l-1)

with
t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})
defined by

\displaystyle t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})

\displaystyle(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1}):=

(1.20)

\displaystyle\int_{M}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{{l-1}})\,d\mu(z),

where
d\mu(z)
is a volume form on
M
.

Together with the horizontal concatenation given by the tensor product of maps
(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})*(K^{\prime}_{1}\otimes K^{\prime}_{2})=K_{a}\otimes K_{b}
with
K_{a}:=K_{1}\otimes K^{\prime}_{1}
and
K_{b}:=K_{2}\otimes K^{\prime}_{2}

this defines a TRAP, written
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
, which we call the TRAP of
generalised smooth kernels
on
M
.

Remark 1.5.12
.

Note that the partial trace amounts to what one could call a partial convolution.

Proof.

The unit
I_{0}\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(0,0)\simeq\mathbb{C}\otimes\mathbb{C}
of the horizontal concatenation
*
is the constant map
f:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
defined by
f(x)=1
. It is the unit of
*
by bilinearity of the tensor product. The horizontal concatenation on the
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-module
\left({\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\right)_{k,l\geqslant 0}
satisfies axiom 2. of Definition
1.4.1
by the properties of the tensor product. We want to check that the maps
t_{i,j}
are well-defined and satisfy axioms 3. (a)-(c).

The existence of the integral follows from the smoothness of
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
and the
closedness of
M
. Therefore, by definition of
{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}
, to show that
t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})\in{\mathcal{K}}_{M}^{\infty}(k-1,l-1)
and thus that
t_{i,j}
is a partial trace, is enough to show that the function

t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2}):M^{k-1}\times M^{l-1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is smooth. Since

K_{1}
and
K_{2}
are smooth, the map

(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{{l-1}})\longmapsto K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{{l-1}})

is infinitely differentiable for any
z\in M
. For
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k})\in M^{k}
and
\vec{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{k})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{k}
we use the short-hand notation

\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}:=\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{1}}}{\partial x_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}}\cdots\frac{\partial^{\alpha_{k}}}{\partial x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}.

Then, since
M
is compact, the partial derivatives

\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{{l-1}})

are bounded uniformly in
z
and hence

\displaystyle\int_{M}\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{{l-1}})\,d\mu(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}\int_{M}K_{1}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{i-1},z,x_{i}\cdots,x_{k-1})\,K_{2}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{j-1},z,y_{j}\cdots,y_{{l-1}})\,d\mu(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\vec{x}}^{\vec{\alpha}}\partial_{\vec{y}}^{\vec{\beta}}t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1}).

Therefore the map
t_{i,j}(K_{1}\otimes K_{2})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1})
is smooth.

Axiom 3.(a) follows from the Fubini theorem, which holds in this case since
M
is compact and by definition of the spaces
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)
. Axiom 3.(b) is a consequence of Theorem
1.5.7
.

Finally, to check Axiom 3.(c), by Lemma
1.4.6
it is enough to check the compatibility of the horizontal concatenation with the partial trace to show that
t_{1,1}(p*p^{\prime})=t_{1,1}(p)*p^{\prime}
for any pair
(p,p^{\prime})\in\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)\times\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k^{\prime},l^{\prime})
with
k,k^{\prime},l,l^{\prime}\geqslant 1
. Setting
p=K_{1}\otimes K_{2}
and
p^{\prime}=K^{\prime}_{1}\otimes K^{\prime}_{2}
we have, by definition of the partial trace maps and the horizontal concatenation

\displaystyle t_{1,1}(p*p^{\prime})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l+l^{\prime}-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int K_{1}(z,x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1})K^{\prime}_{1}(x_{k},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1})K_{2}(z,y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1})K^{\prime}_{2}(x_{l},\cdots,x_{l+l^{\prime}-1})d\mu(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\int K_{1}(z,x_{1},\cdots,x_{k-1})K_{2}(z,y_{1},\cdots,y_{l-1})d\mu(z)\right)K^{\prime}_{1}(x_{k},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1})K^{\prime}_{2}(x_{l},\cdots,x_{l+l^{\prime}-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t_{1,1}(p)*p^{\prime})(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k+k^{\prime}-1},y_{1},\cdots,y_{l+l^{\prime}-1}).\qed

Remark 1.5.13
.

Notice that
\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
is non unitary, since the map
f:M\times M\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
which could play the role of a vertical unity is a
\delta
distribution supported on the diagonal. This will further strengthen our claim that non-unitary TRAPs offer an appropriate framework to host infinite dimensional spaces. We expect non-unitary TRAPs to host more general distributions.

1.6
Free TRAPs

1.6.1
Corolla ordered graphs

Recall from Subssection
1.2.3
that we call a
graph
a family

G=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\alpha,\beta)

with
V(G)
the set of vertices,
E(G)
the set of internal edges,
I(G)
the set of incoming edge,
O(G)
the set of outgoing edges and
IO(G)
the set of incoming and outgoing edges. The sets
V(G)
,
E(G)
,
I(G)
,
O(G)
and
IO(G)
are finite. Furthermore
s
and
t
are respectively the source and target maps of the edges and
\alpha
and
\beta
are respectively the indexation of the inputs and outputs of the graph. We refer the reader to Definition
1.2.19
for the details.

To show that graphs are a free TRAP, we will need one more structure on graphs. Furthermore, since we are dealing here with non unitary TRAPs only, the set of input-output edges
IO(G)
will play no role, and on the contrary shall be empty. This justifies the next definition.

Definition 1.6.1
.

For a graph
G=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\alpha,\beta)
, for any
v\in V(G)
,
O(v)
and
I(v)
respectively refer to the set of
outgoing and ingoing edges of the vertex
v
. In other words, for any
v\in V(G)
,

O(v):=\{e\in E(G)\sqcup O(G)|s(e)=v\},\qquad I(v):=\{e\in E(G)\sqcup I(G)|t(e)=v.\}

We denote the cardinals of the sets
O(v)
and
I(v)
as
o(G)
,
i(G)
,
o(v)
and
i(v)
respectively (recall the cardinals of the sets
O(G)
and
I(G)
are denoted as
o(G)
and
i(G)
respectively).

A
corolla ordered graph
is a graph
G
such that for any vertex
v
, the set of incoming edges
I(v)
of
v

and the set of outgoing edges
O(v)
of
v
are totally ordered and we shall denote both order relations by
\leqslant_{v}
.

A (resp. a corolla oriented) graph
G
is
solar
if
IO(G)=\emptyset
.

For a solar graph (that is, such that
IO(G)=\emptyset
), the terminology ’solar’ refers to its radiating aspect with rays around a central body. In
[
YJ15
]
such graphs are called ’ordinary’.

Graphically, if
G
is a corolla ordered graph, we shall represent the orders on the incoming and outgoing edges of a vertex by drawing box-shaped vertices, with the incoming and outgoing edges of any vertex ordered from left to right. For example, we distinguish the following two situations:

We note that any graph
G
can be made into a corolla ordered graph in
\prod_{v\in V(G)}i(v)!o(v)!
ways, by chosing an order on the ingoing and outgoing vertices of the graph. For example the graph of Example
1.2.20
can be made into a corolla ordered graph in
3!1!1!3!=36
ways. Here are three of them:

x
y
1
2
3
1
3
2

x
y
1
2
3
1
3
2

x
y
1
2
3
1
3
2

Recall that for two graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
, a
morphism
of graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a family of maps
f=(f_{V},f_{E},f_{I},f_{O},f_{IO})
where each map sends vertices to vertices, each type of edges of
G
to the corresponding type of edges in
G^{\prime}
and furthermore respects the source, target and indexation maps. We refer the reader to Definition
1.2.21
for the details. Recall also that an
isomorphism
of graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a morphism of graphs
f=(f_{V},f_{E},f_{I},f_{O},f_{IO},f_{L})
from
G
to
G^{\prime}
such that
f_{V},f_{E},f_{I},f_{O},f_{IO}
and
f_{L}
are bijections.

We can now introduce the morphisms relevant to the graphs we will deal with.

Definition 1.6.2
.

Let
G
and
G^{\prime}
be two corolla ordered graphs.

1.

A
morphism
of corolla ordered graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a
morphism
of graphs

f
from
G
to
G^{\prime}
which preserves the order of incoming and outgoing edges that is, for any vertex of
v
:

•

For any incoming edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
of
v
,
e\leqslant_{v}e^{\prime}
in
G
if, and only if,
f(e)\leqslant_{f(v)}f(e^{\prime})
in
G^{\prime}
.

•

For any outgoing edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
of
v
,
e\leqslant_{v}e^{\prime}
in
G
if, and only if,
f(e)\leqslant_{f(v)}f(e^{\prime})
in
G^{\prime}
.

2.

An
isomorphism
of corolla ordered graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
is a morphism of corolla ordered graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
that is also an isomorphism of graphs from
G
to
G^{\prime}
.

3.

Recall that for any
(k,l)
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}

\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
is the quotient space of graphs with
k
input edges and
l
output edges by the equivalence relation given by isomorphism. Similarly, we denote by
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
the set of isoclasses of corolla ordered
graphs
G
such that
i(G)=k
and
o(G)=l
.

4.

The subset of
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
formed by isoclasses of solar graphs is denoted by
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
and the subset of
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
formed by isoclasses of solar corolla ordered graphs is denoted by
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
.

Throughout this thesis,
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
is a family of sets.

Definition 1.6.3
.

A graph decorated by
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
(or
X
-decorated graph, or simply decorated graph) is a couple
(G,d_{G})
with
G
a graph as in Definition
1.2.19
and
\displaystyle d_{G}:V(G)\longrightarrow\bigsqcup_{k,l\geqslant 0}X(k,l)
an arbitrary map, such that for any vertex
v\in V(G)
,
d_{G}(v)\in X(i(v),o(v))
. We denote by
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
the set of graphs decorated by
X
. Similarly, we define
X
-decorated corolla ordered graphs which we denote by
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
.

We further write
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
(respectively,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
and

\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
) the set of graphs (respectively, of corolla ordered graphs, solar graphs, solar corolla ordered graphs)
decorated by
X
with
k
inputs (that is
|I(G)|=k
) and
l
ouputs (that is
|O(G)|=l
).

1.6.2
TRAPs of graphs

The horizontal concatenation and the structure of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\rm op}
-module on
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
and
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
is induced by the one of on
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
. The structure of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{\rm op}
-module is given by Equation
1.10
. Let us recall it here.

\tau\cdot G\cdot\sigma=(V(G),E(G),I(G),O(G),IO(G),s,t,\sigma^{-1}\circ\alpha,\tau\circ\beta).

Let us further recall that for two graphs
G
and
G^{\prime}
the the horizontal concatenation
G*G^{\prime}
is given by the disjoint union of the edges and various types of edges of
G
and
G^{\prime}
. The source, target and indexation maps are induced by the same maps of
G
and
G^{\prime}
. We refer the reader to Subsection
1.2.3
for the precise definitions. Similarly, the orders of the sets
I(v)
and
O(v)
for each
v\in V(G*G^{\prime})=V(G)\sqcup V(G^{\prime})
are induced by the total orders on the sets of
V(G)
and
V(G^{\prime})
.

Let us finally define the partial trace maps. Let us first give an outline of their definition. Let
G\in\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
(thus
IO(G)=\emptyset
),
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant l
. We set
e_{i}=\alpha_{G}^{-1}(i)\in I(G)
,
f_{j}=\beta_{G}^{-1}(j)\in O(G)
and define
t_{i,j}(G)
as the graph obtained by identifying the input of
e_{i}
with the output
j
of
f_{j}
. This creates an edge in
E(G)
. We then reindex in non-decreasing order, the inputs and the outputs of the obtained graph.

Rigorously we define the graph
G^{\prime}=t_{i,j}(G)
in the following way:

\displaystyle V(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=V(G),

\displaystyle E(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=E(G)\sqcup\{(e_{i},f_{j})\},

\displaystyle I(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=I(G)\setminus\{e_{i}\},

\displaystyle O(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=O(G)\setminus\{f_{j}\},

\displaystyle IO(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=IO(G)=\emptyset,

\displaystyle s_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}s_{G}(f_{j})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j}),\\
s_{G}(e)\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle t_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}t_{G}(e_{i})\mbox{ if }e=(e_{i},f_{j}),\\
t_{G}(e)\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}

\displaystyle\alpha_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\alpha_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)<i,\\
\alpha_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\alpha_{G}(e)\geqslant i,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G^{\prime}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\beta_{G}(e)\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)<j,\\
\beta_{G}(e)-1\mbox{ if }\beta_{G}(e)\geqslant j.\end{cases}

Graphically:
G
1
i
k
\ldots
\ldots
1
j
l
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i,j}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k-1
\ldots
\ldots
1
l-1
\ldots
\ldots

As before, if
G
is corolla ordered, or if it is
X
-decorated, then
t_{i,j}(G)
is corolla ordered, or
X
-decorated.

for
i\in\{1,2,3\}
.

Let us know state and show the main result of this Subsection.

Theorem 1.6.5
.

With this data,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
are TRAPs.

Proof.

We provide the proof for
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
. The proof is similar for the three other cases. Properties 1. and 2., as in Subsection
1.2.3
, follow directly from the symmetric group actions and the horizontal concatenation of graphs defined above.
Let us give a graphical interpretation of the proof of Property 3.(a), when
i^{\prime}<i
and
j^{\prime}<j
.

G
1
i^{\prime}
i
k
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
j^{\prime}
j
l
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i,j}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G
1
i^{\prime}
k-1
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
1
j^{\prime}
l-1
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}}{{\longmapsto}}\\
\vskip 113.81102pt\end{subarray}

G

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

G

For Property 3.(b), let us consider a graph
p=G
.
As the input edge indexed by
i
in
\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
is the input edge of
G
indexed by
\tau(i)

and the output edge indexed by
j
in
\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau
is the output edge of
G
indexed by
\sigma^{-1}(j)
,

G_{1}=t_{i,j}(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)
is the graph obtained by gluing together
the input indexed by
\tau(j)
and the output indexed by
\sigma^{-1}(j)
, reindexing the input
and the output edges
we obtain
G_{1}={l_{i}(\sigma)}\cdot t_{\tau(i),\sigma^{-1}(j)}(G)\cdot{r_{j}(\tau)}
.

Let us prove Property 3.(c). By Lemma
1.4.6
, it is enough to prove it for

(p,p^{\prime})=(G,G^{\prime})
a pair of graphs and
(i,j)=(1,1)
. In this case,

e_{i}
and
f_{j}
are both edges of
G
, so
t_{1,1}(G*G^{\prime})=t_{1,1}(G)*G^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 1.6.6
.

Notice that our definition of graphs implies that
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
and their decorated counterparts are
not
TRAPs. Indeed take
I\in\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(1,1)
the graph without vertices and that contain only a edge, an ingoing-outgoing edge. Then
tr_{1,1}(I)
is not defined. One can add a loop component to the definition of graphs such that
tr_{1,1}(I)
is a loop. Then
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
and their decorated counterparts are
unitary
TRAPs, with unit
I
. See
[
PCP20
,
CLS22
]
for a presentation of these unitary TRAPs.

Remark 1.6.7
.

\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
,
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
and
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
admit sub-TRAPs, for example with vertices with only a prescribed number of ingoing and outgoing edges. These sub-TRAPs might be of importance in the question of renormalisability of QFTs, but this question is to be tackled in future work and we should not rigorously introduce these objects here.

1.6.3
Morphisms of TRAPs and free TRAPs

As before,
X=(X(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
is a family of sets. It turns out that
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is the free TRAP generated by
X
. For any
x\in X(k,l)
, we identify
x
with the graph in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)(X)
with one vertex decorated by
x
,
k
incoming edges, totally ordered according to their indices, and
l
outgoing edges, totally ordered according to their indices. For example,
x\in X(3,2)
is identified with the corolla ordered graph

(1.21)

Theorem 1.6.8
.

Let
P
be a TRAP and
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be a map from
X
to
P
that is, for any
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
\phi(k,l):X(k,l)\longrightarrow P(k,l)
is a map.
Then there exists a unique TRAP morphism
\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
, sending
x
to
\phi(x)
for any
x\in X
.

In other words,
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is the free TRAP generated by
X
.

Remark 1.6.9
.

In practice we often have
P=X
and
\phi=\mathrm{Id}
which yields a map

\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow X

(1.22)

from decorated corolla ordered graphs to the space
X
of decorations.

Proof.

We provide here a sketch of the proof, and refer the reader to the Subsection
1.6.4
for a full proof. We define
\Phi(G)
for any graph
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)(X)
by induction on the number
N
of internal edges of
G
.

If
N=0
, then
G
can be written as

G=\sigma\cdot(x_{1}*\ldots*x_{r})\cdot\tau.

where
r
lies in
\mathbb{N}
,
(k_{i},{l_{i}})
lies in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
for any
i
,
x_{i}
in
X{({k_{i},l_{i}})}
and
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{S}_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r}}
,
\tau
in
\mathfrak{S}_{l_{1}+\ldots+l_{r}}
. We then set

\Phi(G)=\sigma\cdot(\phi(x_{1})*\ldots*\phi(x_{k}))\cdot\tau.

(1.23)

We can prove that this does not depend on the choice of the decomposition of
G
, with the help of the TRAP axioms applied to
P
.

Let us now assume that
\Phi(G^{\prime})
is defined for any graph with
N-1
internal edges, for a given
N\geqslant 1
. Let
G
be a graph with
N
internal edges and let
e
be one of these edges. Let
G_{e}
be a graph obtained by cutting this edge in two, such that
G=t_{1,1}(G_{e})
. We then set:

\Phi(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e}).

We can prove that this does not depend on the choice of
e
. It can then be shown that
\Phi
defined as above is compatible with the partial trace maps.
∎

Since the ingoing and outgoing edges of each vertex of a corolla ordered graph are totally ordered, each corolla ordered graph
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
is naturally acted upon by
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
.

Definition 1.6.10
.

For any corolla ordered graph
G\in\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}
and any vertex
v\in V(G)
, there is a natural action of
\mathfrak{S}_{o(v)}\times\mathfrak{S}_{i(v)}^{op}
induced by the action on the totally ordered edges in
O(v)
and
I(v)
. The corolla ordered graph obtained from
G
by the action of
(\sigma,\tau)
on the vertex
v
is denoted by

\sigma\cdot_{v}G\cdot_{v}\tau.

A similar action can be built on a corolla ordered graph
G
decorated by a family of sets
X
:

\sigma\cdot_{v}(G,d_{G})\cdot_{v}\tau:=(\sigma\cdot_{v}G\cdot_{v}\tau,d_{G}).

Let us illustrate the above definition with a simple example.

Example 1.6.11
.

For a graph with two vertices
v
and
w
and only to internal edges, both from
v
to
w
. Then

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle(12)\cdot_{v}\\
\vskip 42.67912pt\end{subarray}\hbox to27.88pt{\vbox to59.75pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip-15.9336pt\lower-9.95847pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundjoin\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
