In these pictures, the labelling of the edges outgoing (respectively, ingoing to) from the vertex
v
(respectively,
w
) are labelled from left to right.

Remark 1.6.12
.

Corolla-ordered graphs were introduced for two reasons. First, they simplify the proof of freeness given next subsection. Second, they are a crucial part to show that the categories of TRAPs and the categories of wheeled PROPs are isomorphic. Since this result is rather distant from the application we have in mind for TRAPs, it was elected to not include it here. This construction is given in details in
[
CLS22
]
.

Note that
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
is obtained from
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
by forgetting the total orders on the edges, which in fact is equivalent to the trivialisation of this action of symmetric groups on incoming and outgoing edges of any vertex. Hence:

Corollary 1.6.13
.

Let
P
be a TRAP and
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
be a map from
X
to
P
. We assume that for any
x\in X(k,l)
, for any
(\sigma,\tau)\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}\otimes\mathfrak{S}_{l}
,

\tau\cdot\phi(x)\cdot\sigma=\phi(x).

There exists a unique TRAP morphism
\Phi:\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
, sending
x
to
\phi(x)
for any
x\in X
.

We end this paragraph with the non corolla ordered counterpart of Remark
1.6.9
:

Remark 1.6.14
.

In practice we often have
P=X
and
\phi=\mathrm{Id}_{P}
which yields a map

\Phi:\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow X

(1.24)

from decorated graphs to the space
X
of decorations.

1.6.4
Proof of freeness of
\mathbf{{CGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)

We end this Section on free TRAP by giving a detailed proof of Theorem
1.6.8
. Let
P
be a TRAP and let

\phi:X\longrightarrow P
be a map.

Let us first prove the existence of
\Phi
. We define
\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
by assigning to any graph
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
an element
\Phi(G)\in P(k,l)
. We proceed by induction on the number
N
of internal edges of
G
. If
N=0
, then
G
can be written (non uniquely) as

\displaystyle G=\sigma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau,

(1.25)

where
r
in
\mathbb{N}
is unique,
(k_{i},l_{i})
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
for any
i
, unique up to a permutation,
\sigma
in
\mathfrak{S}_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r}}
,
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{l_{1}+\ldots+l_{r}}
and
G_{i}\in X(k_{i},l_{i})
for any
i
.
For any graph
H
we set in this case
H^{*0}=I_{0}=\emptyset
, the empty graph. We then put:

\Phi(G)=\sigma\cdot(\phi_{k_{1},l_{1}}(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi_{k_{r},l_{r}}(G_{r}))\cdot\tau,

where as before
x^{*0}=I_{0}
(the unit for horizontal concatenation in the image
P
of
\Phi
) for any
x\in P
.

Let us assume now that
\Phi(G^{\prime})
is defined for any graph with
N-1
internal edges, for a given
N\geqslant 1
.
Let
G
be a graph with
N
internal edges and let
e
be one of these edges.
Let
G_{e}
be a graph obtained by cutting this edge in two:

•

V(G_{e})=V(G)
.

•

E(G_{e})=E(G)\setminus\{e\}
,
I(G_{e})=I(G)\sqcup\{e\}
,
O(G_{e})=O(G)\sqcup\{e\}
.

•

s_{G_{e}}=s_{G}
and
t_{G_{e}}=t_{G}
.

•

For any
e^{\prime}\in I(G_{e})
, for any
f^{\prime}\in O(G_{e})
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{G_{e}}(e^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }e^{\prime}=e,\\
\alpha_{G}(e^{\prime})+1\mbox{ if }e^{\prime}\neq e,\end{cases}

\displaystyle\beta_{G_{e}}(f^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }f^{\prime}=e,\\
\beta_{G}(f^{\prime})+1\mbox{ if }f^{\prime}\neq e.\end{cases}

Notice that since
G
lies in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
(that is
IO(G)=\emptyset
) then
G_{e}
also lies in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
. We have
G=t_{1,1}(G_{e})
and
G_{e}
has
N-1
internal edges. We then put:

\Phi(G):=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e}).

(1.26)

Lemma 1.6.15
.

The map
\Phi
is well-defined. Moreover, for any
\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
with
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
, for any
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,

\Phi(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the number
N
of internal vertices. For
N=0
, we have to show that

\Phi(G)
does not depend on the choice of the decomposition (
1.25
) of
G
.
Such a decomposition is determined modulo a permutation of the vertices
and of the choice of
\sigma
and
\tau
. Thus, we can go from one decomposition of
G
to any other one by means of a finite number of steps among the following two types:

1.

We consider two decompositions of
G
of the form

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{i}*G_{i+1}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{i+1}*G_{i}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau^{\prime},

with

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\mathrm{Id}_{l_{1}+\ldots+l_{i-1}}\otimes c_{l_{i},l_{i+1}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{l_{i+2}+\ldots+l_{r}}),

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{Id}_{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{i-1}}\otimes c_{k_{i+1},k_{i}}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{k_{i+2}+\ldots+k_{r}})\tau.

Then, by commutativity of
*
, and using
c_{l,m}^{-1}=c_{m,l}
:

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*c_{l_{i},l_{i+1}}\cdot(\phi(G_{i+1})*\phi(G_{i}))\cdot c_{k_{i+1},k_{i}}*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{i})*\phi(G_{i+1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau.

2.

We consider two decompositions of
G
of the form

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot({G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{r}})\cdot\tau^{\prime},

with

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma(\sigma_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\sigma_{r}),

\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(\tau_{1}\otimes\ldots\otimes\tau_{r}){\tau},

\displaystyle{G^{\prime}_{i}=\sigma_{i}^{-1}\cdot G_{i}\cdot\tau^{-1}_{i},}

with
\sigma_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k_{i}}
and
\tau_{i}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l_{i}}
if
i\geqslant 1
.
Using the commutativity of
*
and the invariance of the
x_{k,l}
, we find

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\cdot({\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{r})})\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\sigma_{1}\cdot{\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})}\cdot\tau_{1}*\ldots*\sigma_{r}\cdot{\phi(G^{\prime}_{r})}\cdot\tau_{r})\cdot\tau

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau.

Hence,
\Phi(G)
is well-defined for
G
without internal vertices. Moreover, for any
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
,
\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
, a decomposition of
G
of the form

G=\sigma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau,

give rise to a decomposition of
G^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot G\cdot\tau^{\prime}
given by

\sigma^{\prime}\sigma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau\tau^{\prime},

and, by definition of
\Phi(G^{\prime})
:

\displaystyle\Phi(G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\sigma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(\sigma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau)\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau^{\prime}

since
\phi
is a morphism of
\mathfrak{S}\times\mathfrak{S}^{op}
-modules.

Let us assume the result at rank
N-1
and let
G
be a graph with
N
internal edges. Let us prove that

\Phi(G)
defined in (
1.26
) does not depend on the choice of
e
. If
e^{\prime}
is another internal edge of
G
,
then:

(G_{e})_{e^{\prime}}=(12)\cdot(G_{e^{\prime}})_{e}\cdot(12),

which implies, by definition of
\Phi(G_{e})
and
\Phi(G_{e^{\prime}})
:

\displaystyle t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((G_{e})_{e^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{1,1}\circ((12)\cdot\Phi((G_{e^{\prime}})_{e})\cdot(12))\quad\text{{ by the induction hypothesis}}

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{2,2}\circ\Phi((G_{e^{\prime}})_{e})\quad{\text{by equivariance of the }t_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((G_{e^{\prime}})_{e})\quad{\text{by commutativity of the }t_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e^{\prime}}).

So
\Phi(G)
is well-defined. Let
\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{k}
and
\tau\in\mathfrak{S}_{l}
. Then:

(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)_{e}=((1)\otimes\sigma)\cdot(G_{e})\cdot((1)\otimes\tau),

so:

\displaystyle\Phi(\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((\sigma\cdot G\cdot\tau)_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\left(((1)\otimes\sigma)\cdot\Phi(G_{e})\cdot((1)\otimes\tau)\right)\quad{\text{by the induction hypothesis}}

\displaystyle={l_{1}((1)\otimes\sigma)}\cdot t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})\cdot{r_{1}((1)\otimes\tau)}\quad{\text{by equivariance of the }t_{i,j}}

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot\Phi(G)\cdot\tau

by definition of
l_{i}
and
r_{j}
.
∎

We have therefore defined a map
\Phi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
in the non-unitary case,
compatible with the action of the symmetric groups. It remains to prove that
\Phi
is compatible with the horizontal concatenation

*
and with the partial trace maps.

Lemma 1.6.16
.

For any graphs
G
,
G^{\prime}
,

\Phi(G*G^{\prime})=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

Proof.

We proceed by induction on the number
N
of internal edges of
G*G^{\prime}
. If
N=0
, we put:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=\sigma\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{r})\cdot\tau,

\displaystyle G^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}{\cdot}(G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\cdot\tau^{\prime}.

We obtain:

G*G^{\prime}=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(G_{1}*\ldots*G_{r}*G^{\prime}_{1}*\ldots*G^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}})\cdot(\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime}),

which gives, by equivarience of
*
:

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\sigma\otimes\sigma^{\prime})\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r})*\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}))\cdot(\tau\otimes\tau^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(\sigma\cdot(\phi(G_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G_{r}))\cdot\tau)*\sigma^{\prime}\cdot(\phi(G^{\prime}_{1})*\ldots*\phi(G^{\prime}_{r^{\prime}}))\cdot\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

If
N\geqslant 1
, let us assume that the result holds at rank
N-1
and take an internal edge
e
of
G*G^{\prime}
. If
e
is an internal edge of
G
, then

(G*G^{\prime})_{e}=G_{e}*G^{\prime}
, and:

\displaystyle\Phi(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi((G*G^{\prime})_{e})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e}*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle=t_{1,1}(\Phi(G_{e})*\Phi(G^{\prime}))\quad\text{by the induction hypothesis}

\displaystyle=\left(t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})\right)*\Phi(G^{\prime})\quad\text{by Axiom 3.(c) of Definition \ref{def:Trap}}

\displaystyle=\Phi(G)*\Phi(G^{\prime}).

If
e
is an internal edge of
G^{\prime}
, we obtain similarly that
\Phi(G^{\prime}*G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})*\Phi(G)
.
The result then
follows from the commutativity of
*
.
∎

We still need to prove the compatibility of
\Phi
with the partial trace maps.

Lemma 1.6.17
.

Let
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(k,l)
with
(k,l)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
,
i\in[k]
and
j\in[l]
. Then:

t_{i,j}\circ\Phi(G)=\Phi\circ t_{i,j}(G).

Proof.

By Lemma
1.4.8
, it is enough to prove that
\Phi
is compatible with
t_{1,1}
. Let
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)(k,l)
,
e_{1}=\alpha^{-1}(1)\in I(G)
and
f_{1}=\beta^{-1}(1)\in O(G)
. We set
G^{\prime}=t_{1,1}(G)
and
e=\{e_{1},f_{1}\}
to be the edge of
G^{\prime}
created in the process. Notice that since
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
we have
G^{\prime}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
. Then
e\in E(G^{\prime})
and
G^{\prime}_{e}=G
. By definition of
G^{\prime}
and construction of
\Phi(G^{\prime})
:

\Phi\circ t_{1,1}(G)=\Phi(G^{\prime})=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G^{\prime}_{e})=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G).

So
\Phi
is compatible with the partial trace maps, both in the unitary and non-unitary cases.
∎

We have proved the existence of
\Phi
. It remains to prove the unicity. Let
\Psi:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)\longrightarrow P
be a TRAP morphism extending
\phi
. We prove
\Psi=\Phi
by induction on the number of internal edges. First,
\Psi
and
\Phi
must coincide on graphs with no internal vertices since they are both extentions of
\phi
and morphisms of TRAPs.

Assume now that
\Psi
and
\Phi
coincide on solar corolla oriented graphs with
N
internal edges or less. Let
G
be such a graph with
N+1
internal edges. Then, for
e\in E(G)
.

\Phi(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\Phi(G_{e})=t_{1,1}\circ\Psi(G_{e})=\Psi\circ t_{1,1}(G_{e})=\Psi(G).

We have used the definition of
\Phi
, the induction hypothesis, the fact that
\Psi
is a morphism of TRAPs and the definition of
G_{e}
. Thus
\Psi=\Phi
and
\Phi
is unique.

This ends the proof of Theorem
1.6.8
.

1.7
Compositions, generalised trace and convolution

We will now see how the definition of TRAPs and the universal property of the TRAP of graphs imply that they carry more structures.

1.7.1
Vertical concatenation in a TRAP

In Subsection
1.2.3
, we showed that
\mathbf{Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
admits a PROP structure. It is clear that
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
is a sub-PROP and that the corolla ordered graphs as well as the decorated graphs inherit this PROP structure, up to the lack of unity. We therefore have both PROP and TRAP structures for (unitary) TRAP.

The vertical concatenation on
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}
can be described in terms of the horizontal concatenation and of the partial trace maps:
If
G
is a solar graph with
k
inputs and
l
outputs, and
G^{\prime}
a solar graph with
l
inputs and
m
outputs, then:

\displaystyle t_{k+1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k+l-1,l-1}\circ t_{k+l,l}(G*G^{\prime})

\displaystyle={G^{\prime}\circ G},

or, graphically:
G
1
k
\ldots
\ldots
G^{\prime}
1
m
\ldots
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle=\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
1
k
\ldots
\ldots
G^{\prime}
1
m
\ldots

This construction can be generalised from TRAPs of graphs to an arbitrary TRAP.

Definition-Proposition 1.7.1
.

Let
P
be a TRAP. We define a vertical concatenation
3
3

3

When there is a risk of confusion, we will write
\circ_{P}
for the vertical concatenation of a given TRAP
P
.

\circ
in the following way:

\displaystyle\forall(k,l,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{3},\>\forall q\in P(l,m),\>\forall p\in P(k,l),

\displaystyle p\circ q

\displaystyle:=t_{k+1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k+l-1,l-1}\circ t_{k+l,l}(p*q).

This operation is associative: for any
(k,l,m,n)
in
\mathbb{N}^{4}
, for any
(p,q,r)
in
P(k,l)\times P(l,m)\times P(n,k)
,

r\circ(q\circ p)=(r\circ q)\circ p.

(1.27)

Remark 1.7.2
.

We therefore have that non-unitary TRAPs carry a non-unitary version of the PROP structure. In the unitary case we obtain a full-fledge PROP; see
[
CLS22
, Definition-Proposition 4.1.1]
.

Proof.

Recall that in Subsection
1.6.3
we identified any element
p
of the decorating set and the solar graph with one vertex decorated by
p
(see Equation (
1.21
)).
Let
\alpha:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow P
be the unique TRAP morphism such that
\alpha(p)=p
for any
p\in P
whose existence follows from Theorem
1.6.8
and more specifically from the case detailed in Remark
1.6.9
.
This is therefore a surjective TRAP morphism. As
\alpha
respects the horizontal concatenation and the partial trace maps,
for any graphs
G,G^{\prime}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
such that
G\circ G^{\prime}
is well-defined,
\alpha(G)\circ\alpha(G^{\prime})
is also well-defined and

\alpha(G)\circ\alpha(G^{\prime})=\alpha(G\circ G^{\prime}).

Since the vertical concatenation is clearly associative in
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
, the vertical concatenation is associative in
P
.
∎

Remark 1.7.3
.

In
[
YJ15
, Paragraph 3.3.3]
, the authors also define
partial vertical concatenations
, which could also be built in this framework by gluing only a subset of the outputs to the inputs with the partial trace maps. I do not pursue further this course since such partial vertical concatenations will play no role here.

Let us illustrate the link between TRAPs and (non-unitary) PROPs in a example built from the TRAP of Proposition
1.5.4
.

Example 1.7.4
.

Let
V
be a vector space and let
f=\theta(v_{1}\ldots v_{l}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})\in\Hom_{V}^{fr}(k,l)
,

g=\theta(w_{1}\ldots w_{m}\otimes g_{1}\ldots g_{l})\in\Hom_{V}^{fr}(l,m)
. Then, denoting by
\bullet
the vertical
concatenation of
\Hom_{V}^{fr}
:

\displaystyle g\bullet f

\displaystyle=g_{1}(v_{1})\ldots g_{l}(v_{l})\theta(w_{1}\ldots w_{m}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})

\displaystyle=\theta(w_{1}\ldots w_{m}\otimes g_{1}\ldots g_{l})\circ\theta(v_{1}\ldots v_{l}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})

\displaystyle=g\circ f.

Hence, the vertical concatenation induced by the TRAP structure is the usual composition of linear maps.
If
V
is not finite-dimensional, this composition does not have a unit, as
\mathrm{Id}_{V}
is not of finite rank.

We end this subsection with a simple yet important proposition.

Proposition 1.7.5
.

For any two TRAPs
P=(P(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
and
Q
, any TRAP morphism
\phi=(\phi(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}:P\longrightarrow Q
is compatible with the vertical concatenations of
P
and
Q
.

Proof.

We need to show that for any TRAPs
P
and
Q
and any TRAP morphism
\phi:P\longrightarrow Q
as in the statement of the proposition, for any
(k,l,m)
in
\mathbb{N}^{3}
,
p_{1}
in
P(k,l)
and
p_{2}
in
P(l,m)
we have

\phi(k,m)(p_{2}\circ_{P}p_{1})=\phi(k,l)(p_{1})\circ_{Q}\phi(l,m)(p_{2}).

Using the definition of the vertical concatenation in the TRAP
P
and the fourth property of the Definition
1.4.7
of morphisms of TRAPs we have

\phi(k,m)(p_{2}\circ_{P}p_{1})=t_{k+1,1}^{Q}\circ\cdots\circ t_{k+l,l}^{Q}[\phi(k+l,m+l)(p_{1}*p_{2})]

with
t_{i,j}^{Q}
the partial trace maps of the TRAP
Q
. Then using the third property of Definition
1.4.7

we obtain:

\phi(k,m)(p_{2}\circ_{P}p_{1})=t_{k+1,1}^{Q}\circ\cdots\circ t_{k+l,l}^{Q}[\phi(k,l)(p_{1})*\phi(l,m)(p_{2})]=\phi(k,l)(p_{1})\circ_{Q}\phi(l,m)(p_{2}).

∎

1.7.2
The generalised trace on a TRAP

If
G
is a solar graph with the same number of inputs and outputs, we define its generalised trace by, roughly speaking, grafting
any of its input to the output with the same index:
G
1
k
\ldots
1
k
\ldots

\begin{subarray}{c}\displaystyle\longmapsto\\
\vskip 85.35826pt\end{subarray}

G
\ldots
\ldots

This construction preserve corolla ordered graphs and
X
-decorated graphs.
Moreover, we can describe this construction in terms of the partial trace maps: if
G\in{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(X)(k,k)
, then
its generalized trace is

t_{1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k,k}(G)=t_{1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{1,1}(G).

This formulas have a meaning for any TRAP:

Definition 1.7.6
.

Let
P
be a TRAP. For any
p
in
P(k,k)
, with
k
in
\mathbb{N}
, the generalised trace on
P
is defined as:

\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p):=t_{1,1}\circ\ldots\circ t_{k,k}(p)\in P(0,0).

In the case of the TRAPs
\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)
, we shall simply write
\mathrm{Tr}
instead of
\mathrm{Tr}_{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(X)}
.

Let us now state some properties of these generalised traces.

Proposition 1.7.7
.

Let
P
be a TRAP.

1.

For any
(k,l)
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any
(p,q)
in
P(k,l)\times P(l,k)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p\circ q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(q\circ p),

which justifies the terminology "trace".

2.

For any
(k,l)
in
\mathbb{N}^{2}
, for any
(p,q)
in
P(k,k)\times P(l,l)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p*q)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p)*\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(q).

Proof.

Let
\alpha:{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)\longrightarrow P
be, as before in the proof of Definition-Proposition
1.7.1
, the unique TRAP morphism which extends the identity map on
P
.
Since
\alpha
respects the partial trace maps, for any graph
G\in{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}}(P)(k,k)
,

\alpha\circ\mathrm{Tr}(G)=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}\circ\alpha(G).

Let
p,q\in P(k,k)
. In
\mathbf{{sol}Gr^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,
\mathrm{Tr}(q\circ p)
and
\mathrm{Tr}(p\circ q)
are represented respectively by the graphs

p
\ldots
\ldots
q
\ldots

q
\ldots
\ldots
p
\ldots

which are the same. Applying
\alpha
, we obtain
\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p\circ q)=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(q\circ p)
.
Moreover, the graph
\mathrm{Tr}(p*q)
is represented by

p
\ldots
\ldots
q
\ldots
\ldots

which is also a graphical representation of
\mathrm{Tr}(p)*\mathrm{Tr}(q)
. Applying
\alpha
,
we obtain
\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p*q)=\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(p)*\mathrm{Tr}_{P}(q)
.
∎

Let us check that our generalised traces indeed generalise the usual traces of endomorphisms of finite dimensional vector spaces.

Example 1.7.8
.

Let
V
be a finite dimensional vector space and
f=\theta(v_{1}\ldots v_{k}\otimes f_{1}\ldots f_{k})\in\Hom_{V}^{fr}(k,k)
.
Identifying
\Hom_{V}(0,0)
with
\mathbb{R}
, we obtain that

\mathrm{Tr}_{\Hom_{V}}(f)=f_{1}(v_{1})\ldots f_{k}(v_{k}),

which is the usual trace of linear endomorphisms of a finite-dimensional vector space.
If
V
is not finite-dimensional,
\mathrm{Tr}_{\Hom_{V}^{fr}}
is a direct generalisation of this trace for linear endomorphisms
of finite rank.

1.7.3
Amplitudes and generalised convolutions

By Theorem
1.6.8
applied to
\phi=\mathrm{Id}_{P}
,
we know that for any TRAP
P
there exists a canonical TRAP map
\Phi_{P}:\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\longrightarrow P
(see Remark
1.6.9
).

Definition 1.7.9
.

Let
G
be a graph decorated by a TRAP
P
.
The
P
-amplitude
associated to
G
is the image of
G
under
\Phi_{P}
.

When
P=\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}
is the TRAP of smooth generalised kernels over a smooth finite dimensional closed Riemannian manifold
M
of Subsection
1.5.3
(that is if
P(k,l)=\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty}(k,l)
with the r.h.s defined in (
1.19
)),
we simply write
\Phi
for
\Phi_{P}
and call
\Phi(G)
the
smooth amplitudes

associated to
G\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(\mathcal{K}_{M}^{\infty})
.

Remark 1.7.10
.

The terminology
P
-amplitude is justified in so far as it associates to a graph an expression in
P
depending on the ingoing and outgoing edges of the graph in a similar way as an amplitude associated to a Feynman diagram depends on the external ingoing and outgoing momenta. So these
P
-amplitudes are a step toward to our final goal of building amplitudes of Feynman graphs.

The case of a path graph relates amplitudes to convolutions.

Remark 1.7.11
.

Let
G
be a path graph decorated by
X=(\mathcal{K}_{X}^{\infty}(k,l))_{k,l\geqslant 0}
, that is to say a solar graph such that
I(G)=O(G)=[1]
,
V(G)=\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}\}
,
E(G)=\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-1}\}
and the source and target maps defined by
s_{G}(1)=v_{n}
,
t_{G}(1)=v_{1}
and

\forall i\in[n-1],\penalty\ s_{G}(e_{i})=v_{i},\qquad t_{G}(e_{i})=v_{i+1}.

Here is a graphical representation of this graph:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v_{1}}
\textstyle{\ldots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{v_{n}}
\textstyle{1}

Let
P_{i},i=1,\cdots,n
be smoothing pseudo-differential operators each of which is defined by the kernel
K_{i}
that decorates the vertex
v_{i}
. Then the generalised convolution associated to the graph
G
is the convolution
K_{1}\star\cdots\star K_{n}
of the kernels
K_{1},\cdots,K_{n}
, which is the
kernel of the smoothing pseudo-differential operator
P_{1}\circ\cdots\circ P_{n}
. In this sense,
P
-amplitudes can be seen as generalisations of the convolution of multiple smooth kernels.

The generalised amplitude of a TRAP
P
respects the horizontal concatenation of
P
but also the vertical concatenation of
P
built from the partial traces of
P
in Subsection
1.7.1
.

Proposition 1.7.12
.

For any TRAP
P
, the
P
-amplitude associated to a horizontal concatenation of graphs is the horizontal concatenation of their
P
-amplitudes: for any
G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
,

\Phi_{P}(G_{1}*G_{2})=\Phi_{P}(G_{1})*\Phi_{P}(G_{2}),

and the same holds for the vertical concatenation: if
G_{1}\circ G_{2}
exists, then

\Phi_{P}(G_{1}\circ G_{2})=\Phi_{P}(G_{1})\circ_{P}\Phi_{P}(G_{2})

with
\circ_{P}
the vertical concatenation of
P
.

Proof.

This follows directly from the fact that
\Phi_{P}
is a TRAP morphism and from Proposition
1.7.5
.
∎

For any TRAP
P
, let
\iota_{P}:P\hookrightarrow\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)
be the canonical embedding of
P
into the TRAP of
P
-decorated graphs that is,
\iota_{P}(p)
is the solar graph with only one vertex decorated by
p
. We have the following simple Proposition, which basically states that every TRAP computations can be performed via graphs.

Lemma 1.7.13
.

For any TRAP
P
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{P\times_{\circ}P\penalty\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ_{P}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\times\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{P}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{{solCGr}^{\circlearrowright}}(P)}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{P}}

with
\circ_{p}
the vertical concatenation of the TRAP
P
, the top arrow given by
\iota_{P}\times\iota_{P}
and
P\times_{\circ}P\subseteq P\times P
is the domain of the vertical concatenation of the TRAP
P
, as defined in Definition
1.7.6
.

In words, the vertical concatenation of two elements
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
of
P
is the
P
-amplitude associated with the graph given by the vertical concatenation of two graphs with exactly one vertex, each decorated by one
p_{i}
.
Graphically, if
p\in P(k,l)
and
q\in P(l,m)
:

\Phi_{P}\left(\begin{subarray}{c}\hskip 14.22636pt\\

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.38373pt}{75.82516pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
