Figure 2.2
:
Universal property of forests.

for every
\omega
in
\Omega
.

Remark 2.2.13
.

This Theorem is proved constructively, by recursively defining the map
\Phi
as a morphism of operated algebras. Its uniqueness is then shown indirectly. In particular, this proof still holds in the non commutative case, but we will not need this level of generality here.

We use these universal properties in a special case, where the decorating set
\Omega
has an algebra structure to lift maps on the decorating sets to maps on forests. We do this by using the original map to define an operation. This can be carried out in various ways, and we introduce here two that will be used later on. Such branchings were introduced in
[
CGPZ18
]
and further used in
[
CGPZ20a
]
.

Definition 2.2.14
.

Let
\Omega
be a commutative algebra and
\phi:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
be a map. Let
\beta_{\phi}:\Omega\times\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
be the
operation of
\Omega
on itself defined by

\beta_{\phi}(\omega_{1},\omega_{2}):=\phi(\omega_{1}.\omega_{2})
. The
branched
\phi
-map
(or branching of
\phi
) is the morphism of
commutative
\Omega
-operated algebras

\widehat{\phi}:(\mathcal{F}_{\Omega},B_{+})\longrightarrow(\Omega,\beta_{\phi})
whose existence and uniqueness is given by Theorem
2.2.12
.

Notice that the map
\widehat{\phi}
is entirely determined by the relations

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=1_{\Omega},

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(F_{1}F_{2})

\displaystyle=\widehat{\phi}(F_{1})\widehat{\phi}(F_{2}),

(2.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(B_{+}^{\omega}(F))

\displaystyle=\phi\left(\omega\widehat{\phi}(F)\right).

Before moving on, and since branching maps are a crucial element of the rest of the paper, let me work out some of their actions on small trees.

Example 2.2.15
.

Let
\Omega
and
\phi
be as in the above Definition. Then

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(\emptyset)=1_{\Omega};\qquad

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny$\omega$}\end{picture})=\phi(\omega);\qquad\widehat{\phi}\left(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny$\omega_{1}$}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny$\omega_{2}$}\end{picture}\right)=\phi\left(\omega_{1}\phi(\omega_{2})\right);

\displaystyle\widehat{\phi}\left(\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny$\omega_{1}$}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny$\omega_{2}$}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny\penalty\ $\omega_{3}$}\end{picture}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi\left(\omega_{1}\phi(\omega_{2})\phi(\omega_{3})\right).

As already stated, there are other possibilities to lift maps from an algebra to rooted forests decorated by this algebra. Here is an other one that will play a role later on.

Definition 2.2.16
.

Let
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}
be two commutative algebras and
\phi:\Omega_{1}\longrightarrow\Omega_{2}
be a map. Let

\tilde{\beta}_{\phi}:\Omega_{1}\times\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{2}}
be the operation of
\Omega_{1}
on

\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{2}}
defined by
\tilde{\beta}_{\phi}(\omega_{1},F):=B_{+}(\phi(\omega_{1}),F)
. The
lifted
\phi
-map
(or lifting of
\phi
)
is the morphism of commutative
\Omega_{1}
-operated algebras

\phi^{\sharp}:(\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{1}},B_{+})\longrightarrow(\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{2}},\tilde{\beta}_{\phi})
whose existence and uniqueness is given by Theorem

2.2.12
.

As before, let me give examples of the action of a lifted map on small trees.

Example 2.2.17
.

Let
\Omega_{1},\Omega_{2}
and
\phi
be as in the above Definition. Then:

\displaystyle\phi^{\sharp}(\emptyset)=\emptyset;\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{\sharp}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny$\omega$}\end{picture})=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny$\phi(\omega)$}\end{picture}\hskip 11.38092pt;\qquad\phi^{\sharp}\left(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny$\omega_{1}$}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny$\omega_{2}$}\end{picture}\right)=\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny$\phi(\omega_{1})$}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny$\phi(\omega_{2})$}\end{picture}\hskip 14.22636pt;

\displaystyle\phi^{\sharp}\left(\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny$a$}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny$b$}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny\penalty\ $c$}\end{picture}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny$\phi(a)$}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny$\phi(b)$}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny$\phi(c)\hskip 7.3612pt$}\end{picture}\hskip 17.07182pt.

2.2.3
Shuffle of rooted forests

We will see later that branchings are related through Rota-Baxter maps to flattenings and shuffles of rooted forests. Let us start by introducing the latter. They are a non-associative generalisation of the shuffle products of words. As far as I am aware, these products were introduced in my work
[
Cla20
]
, however a different product with a similar construction was introduced earlier in
[
Guo12
]
.

Definition 2.2.18

(
[
Cla20
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a set (resp.
(\Omega,\bullet)
be a commutative semigroup and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
). The
shuffle product on trees
,
\shuffle
, (resp. the
\lambda
-shuffle product on trees
,
\shuffle_{\lambda}
,) of two forests
F
and

F^{\prime}
is defined recursively on
|F|+|F^{\prime}|
.

If
|F|+|F^{\prime}|=0
(and thus
F=F^{\prime}=\emptyset
), we set
\emptyset\shuffle\emptyset=\emptyset\shuffle_{\lambda}\emptyset=\emptyset
.

For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, assume the shuffle (resp.
\lambda
-shuffle) products of forests has been defined on every forests
f,f^{\prime}
such that
|f|+|f^{\prime}|\leqslant N
. Then for any two forests
F,F^{\prime}
such that
|F|+|F^{\prime}|=N+1
;

•

(Unit) if
F^{\prime}=\emptyset
, set
\emptyset\shuffle F=F\shuffle\emptyset=F
; and
F\shuffle_{\lambda}\emptyset=\emptyset\shuffle_{\lambda}F=F
.

•

(Compatibility with the concatenation product) if
F
or
F^{\prime}
is not a tree, then we can write
F
and
f
uniquely as a concatenation of trees:
F=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
and
F^{\prime}=t_{1}\cdots t_{n}
with the
T_{i}
s and
t_{j}
s
nonempty,
k+n\geqslant 3
and set

F\shuffle F^{\prime}=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((T_{i}\shuffle t_{j})T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{k}\right)

(resp.

F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime}=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((T_{i}\shuffle_{\lambda}t_{j})T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{k}\right)\penalty\ ),

where
T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}
stands for the concatenation of the trees
T_{1},\cdots,T_{n}
without the tree
T_{i}
.

•

(Compatibility with the grafting) if
F=T=B_{+}^{a}(f)
and
F^{\prime}=T^{\prime}=B_{+}^{a^{\prime}}(f^{\prime})
are two nonempty trees, we set

T\shuffle T^{\prime}=B_{+}^{a}(f\shuffle T^{\prime})+B_{+}^{a^{\prime}}(T\shuffle f^{\prime})

(resp.

T\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime}=B_{+}^{a}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime})+B_{+}^{a^{\prime}}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime})+\lambda B_{+}^{a\bullet a^{\prime}}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime})\penalty\ ).

We extend these products by linearity to obtain products on the vector space
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}
.

Remark 2.2.19
.

The well-definedness of the products
\shuffle
,
\shuffle_{\lambda}
follows from the fact that any forest can be uniquely written (up to permutation) as an iteration of concatenations and graftings.

Notice also that I use the same symbols for shuffles on trees and shuffles on words, as whether we are working with words or with trees will be clear from context. So, as for words, we write
{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}:=\shuffle_{1}
the

stuffle product on trees
and
\shuffle_{-1}
the
anti-stuffle product on trees
.

As for words,
\shuffle_{0}=\shuffle
. I nevertheless make a distinction between the cases
\lambda=0
and
\lambda\neq 0
as in the former case,
\Omega
is not
required to have a semigroup structure. I will however sometimes treat the
\shuffle
product as a special case of
\shuffle_{\lambda}
, keeping in mind that when dealing with

\lambda=0
(so with the shuffle product), I will always implicitly allow the decoration set to have no semigroup structure.

Example 2.2.20
.

Here are examples of stuffle of trees with
(\Omega,.)=(\mathbb{N},+)
:

Based on these intermediate computations, one can compute more involved shuffles of trees. However due to their length, we will not write down the result here. For example the shuffle

\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny r}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny m}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny n}\end{picture}\shuffle_{\lambda}\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny s}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny q}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny p}\end{picture}
is a sum of forty-four trees, of which twenty have six vertices, twenty have five vertices, and four have four vertices.

We have a simple but important result about the structures these shuffles endow spaces of rooted forests with.

Proposition 2.2.21
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{*}
and
(\Omega,\bullet)
be a commutative semigroup; or
\lambda=0
and
\Omega
be a set. Then
(\mathcal{F},\shuffle_{\lambda},\emptyset)
is an nonassociative, commutative, unital algebra.

Proof.

The case
\lambda=0
is a consequence of the more general case as every undefined product in
\Omega
disappears if
\lambda
is set to
0
. Therefore we will only explicitly work out the case
\lambda\neq 0
.

Let
(\Omega,.)
be a commutative semigroup and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.

1.

By definition of
\shuffle_{\lambda}
,
\emptyset\shuffle_{\lambda}F=F\shuffle_{\lambda}\emptyset=F
for any
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}
. Therefore
\emptyset
is the unit for
\shuffle_{\lambda}
as claimed.

2.

We prove the commutativity of
\shuffle_{\lambda}
by induction on
|F|+|F^{\prime}|
. If
|F|+|F^{\prime}|=0
then
F=F^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime}=\emptyset=F^{\prime}\shuffle_{\lambda}F
by definition. Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
and assume that, for any
pair of forest
f,f^{\prime}
such that
|f|+|f^{\prime}|\leqslant N
we have
f\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\shuffle_{\lambda}f
. Let
F,F^{\prime}
be two forests such that
|F|+|F^{\prime}|=N+1
. We then distinguish three cases:

•

If
F=\emptyset
or
F^{\prime}=\emptyset
, then
F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime}=F^{\prime}\shuffle_{\lambda}F
since
\emptyset
is the unit of
\shuffle_{\lambda}
.

•

If
F=T=B_{+}^{a}(f)
and
F^{\prime}=T^{\prime}=B_{+}^{a^{\prime}}(f^{\prime})
are two nonempty trees, then

T\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime}-T^{\prime}\shuffle_{\lambda}T=\lambda B_{+}^{a\bullet a^{\prime}}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime})-\lambda B_{+}^{a^{\prime}\bullet a}(f^{\prime}\shuffle_{\lambda}f).

The R.H.S. vanishes by commutativity of
(\Omega,\bullet)
and the induction hypothesis.

•

If
F
or
F^{\prime}
is not a tree, we write
F=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
and
F^{\prime}=t_{1}\cdots t_{n}
with
k+n\geqslant 3
. Then

\displaystyle F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((T_{i}\shuffle_{\lambda}t_{j})T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((t_{j}\shuffle_{\lambda}T_{i})t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{k}T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}\right)

by the induction hypothesis and the commutativity of the concatenation product of trees. Exchanging the order of the summations we indeed find
F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime}=F^{\prime}\shuffle_{\lambda}F
.

We have treated the three possible cases. Thus
\shuffle_{\lambda}
is indeed commutative.

∎

Remark 2.2.22
.

We will focus here on the applications of these new shuffle products of trees and their nonassociativity to the study of AZVs. Linked questions, such that the existence of a coproduct associated to these shuffles and the
eventual existence of a comodule-bialgebra structure
[
EFFM17
]
are interesting questions, but away from the scope of the present work. As such, they are left for further investigations.

Remark 2.2.23
.

One can see
10
10

10

I thank Dominique Manchon for his very useful comments on this point.
that the coefficient
1/kn
arising in the compatibility with the concatenation product of trees equation of the
definition of the shuffle products on trees will prevent associativity. However, one could also define “unweighted” shuffle products of rooted forests without this factor. It turns out that these unweighted shuffle products are still not associative. Indeed, if one computes
((T_{1}\dots T_{n})\shuffle_{\lambda}T)\shuffle_{\lambda}(t_{1}\cdots t_{m})
, we will see
appear terms containing the forests
(T_{i}\shuffle_{\lambda}T)(T_{j}\shuffle_{\lambda}t_{k})
. Such terms will not be present in products
(T_{1}\dots T_{n})\shuffle_{\lambda}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}(t_{1}\cdots t_{m}))
.

Let us end this subsection by an illustration of the nonassocativity of
\shuffle_{\lambda}
, with
\lambda
set to
0
in order to have simpler
expressions to handle.

Counterexample 2.2.24
.

Let
\Omega
be a set. Then an easy computation gives, for any
(a,b,c,d)\in\Omega^{4}

\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny b}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ \begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny c}\end{picture}\right)\shuffle\penalty\ \begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny d}\end{picture}=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny b}\end{picture}\shuffle\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny c}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ \begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny d}\end{picture}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny d}\end{picture}+\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny d}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny a}\end{picture}\right)\left(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny b}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny c}\end{picture}+\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny c}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny b}\end{picture}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny b}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny d}\end{picture}+\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny d}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny b}\end{picture}\right)\left(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny c}\end{picture}+\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny c}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny a}\end{picture}\right).

2.2.4
Back to words

I now introduce the flattening maps. They have many different names. In
[
Man13
]
, they are called the arborifications (simple and contracting), following Ecalle in
[
Eca92
]
(see also
[
EV04
]
). They also appear in
[
Yam20
]
. I choose here to follow
[
CGPZ20b
]
and
[
Cla20
]
and to define these flattening maps through the universal properties of rooted forests given by Theorem
2.2.12
.

Definition 2.2.25
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
(\Omega,\bullet)
be a commutative semigroup (resp.
\lambda=0
and
\Omega
a set). We define an operation of
\Omega
on the commutative algebra
(\mathcal{W},\emptyset,\shuffle_{\lambda})
as:

\displaystyle C_{+}:\penalty\ \Omega

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{W}_{\Omega}\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}_{\Omega}

(2.9)

\displaystyle(\omega

\displaystyle,w)\mapsto C_{+}^{\omega}(w):=(\omega)\sqcup w.

Then the
flattening map of weight
\lambda
is the morphism of

\Omega
-operated algebras
fl_{\lambda}:(\mathcal{F}_{\Omega},B_{+})\mapsto(\mathcal{W}_{\Omega},C_{+})
whose
existence and uniqueness is given by Theorem
2.2.12
.

Notice that these flattening maps are entirely determined by the following relations:

\displaystyle fl_{\lambda}(\emptyset)

\displaystyle=\emptyset

\displaystyle fl_{\lambda}(F_{1}F_{2})

\displaystyle=fl_{\lambda}(F_{1})\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(F_{2})

\displaystyle fl_{\lambda}(B_{+}^{\omega}(F))

\displaystyle=(\omega)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(F).

The following Lemma will be useful to prove the main result of this Section.

Lemma 2.2.26
.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
and
(\Omega,\bullet)
be a commutative semi-group (resp.
\lambda=0
and
\Omega
a set). Then
fl_{\lambda}
is a algebra morphism for the
\lambda
-shuffle products of rooted forests and words: for any rooted forests
F
and
F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}

fl_{\lambda}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})=fl_{\lambda}(F)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(F^{\prime})

with
\shuffle_{\lambda}
the
\lambda
-shuffle of rooted forests in the LHS and the
\lambda
-shuffle of words in the RHS.

Proof.

We prove this result for
(\Omega,\bullet)
a commutative semi-group since the case where
\Omega
is a set can be deduced from it by setting
\lambda=0
. The proof is by induction on
N=|F|+|F^{\prime}|
. If
N=0
then
F=F^{\prime}=\emptyset
and the result trivially holds. For
N\in\mathbb{N}
, let us assume that it holds for any rooted forests
f
and
f^{\prime}
such that
|f|+|f^{\prime}|\leqslant N
. Let
F
and
F^{\prime}
be two rooted forests such that
|F|+|F^{\prime}|=N+1
. We have three cases to consider:

•

If
F=\emptyset
or
F^{\prime}=\emptyset
the result trivially holds.

•

If
F=T=B_{+}^{a}(f)
and
F^{\prime}=T^{\prime}=B_{+}^{b}(f^{\prime})
are two rooted trees since
fl_{\lambda}
is a map of
\Omega
-operated algebras we have by definition of
\omega_{\lambda}
, the
\lambda
-shuffle of rooted forests we have

\displaystyle fl_{\lambda}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})

\displaystyle=fl_{\lambda}(B_{+}^{a}(f)\shuffle_{\lambda}B_{+}^{b}(f^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=fl_{\lambda}(B_{+}^{a}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime}))+fl_{\lambda}(B_{+}^{b}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime}))+\lambda fl_{\lambda}(B_{+}^{a\bullet b}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=(a)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime})+(b)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime})+\lambda(a\bullet b)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(f\shuffle_{\lambda}f^{\prime}).

Since
|f|+|T^{\prime}|=|T|+|f^{\prime}|=N
and
|f|+|f^{\prime}|=N-1
we can use the induction hypothesis and we obtain

\displaystyle fl_{\lambda}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(a)\sqcup(fl_{\lambda}(f)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(T^{\prime}))+(b)\sqcup(fl_{\lambda}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(f^{\prime}))+\lambda(a\bullet b)\sqcup(fl_{\lambda}(f)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(f^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=((a)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(f))\shuffle_{\lambda}((b)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(f^{\prime}))\qquad\text{by definition \ref{def:stuffle}}

\displaystyle=fl_{\lambda}(T)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(T^{\prime})

by definition of
fl_{\lambda}
. In this case the result also holds.

•

If
F=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
and
F^{\prime}=t_{1}\cdots t_{k}
with
n+k\geqslant 3
we have by definition of the
\lambda
-shuffle of rooted forests

\displaystyle fl_{\lambda}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}fl_{\lambda}\left((T_{i}\shuffle_{\lambda}t_{j})T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}fl_{\lambda}\left((T_{i}\shuffle_{\lambda}t_{j})\right)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}\left(T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{n}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{k}\right).

Since
n+k\geqslant 3
we have
|T_{i}|+|t_{j}|\leqslant N
and we can use the induction hypothesis to each of these pair of trees. Then using that
fl_{\lambda}(TT^{\prime})=fl_{\lambda}(T)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(T^{\prime})
we obtain

fl_{\lambda}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}fl_{\lambda}(t_{1}\cdots t_{k})\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(t_{1}\cdots t_{k})=fl_{\lambda}(F)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(F^{\prime})

which concludes the proof.

∎

Before we move on, I state a simple yet important property of
flattening maps.

Proposition 2.2.27
.

Let
(\Omega,\bullet)
be a commutative semigroup and
\lambda
a rational (resp. integer) number. For any finite forest
F
in

\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}
,
fl_{\lambda}(F)
is a
finite sum of finite words with rational (resp. integer) coefficients.

If
\Omega
is a set, then for any finite forest
F
in

\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}
,
fl_{0}(F)
is again a
finite sum of finite words with integer coefficients.

Proof.

This result is easily shown by induction on the number of vertices of
F
. It follows from the fact that the flattening of the empty
forest is trivially a finite sum of finite words with integer coefficients; that (provided
\lambda
is rational; resp. integer) the

\lambda
-shuffle
of two finite sums of finite words with rational (resp. integer) coefficients is a finite sum of finite words with rational (resp. integer)
coefficients since

\mathbb{Q}
(resp.
\mathbb{Z}
) is stable under multiplication and addition; and
finally that the concatenation by one letter of a finite sum of finite words with rational (resp. integer) coefficients is a finite sum of
finite words
with rational (resp. integer) coefficients.

The case where
\Omega
is a set follows from the case
\lambda=0
.
∎

As in
[
CGPZ20a
]
, one can build counterparts for words of Definitions
2.2.14
and
2.2.16
.

Definition 2.2.28
.

Let
\Omega
be a commutative algebra,
\Omega_{1}
and
\Omega_{2}
be two sets. Let
\iota_{\Omega}

(resp.
\iota_{\Omega_{1}}
,
\iota_{\Omega_{2}}
) be the canonical inclusion of words into trees, which to any word associate a
ladder tree. Let
\phi:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
and
\psi:\Omega_{1}\longrightarrow\Omega_{2}
be two maps. The
W-branched
\phi
map

\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}_{\Omega}\longrightarrow\Omega
is defined by

\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}:=\widehat{\phi}\circ\iota_{\Omega}
; and the
W-lifted
\phi
map

\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}_{\Omega_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{W}_{\Omega_{2}}
by

\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}:=\iota_{\Omega_{2}}^{-1}\circ\phi^{\sharp}\circ\iota_{\Omega_{1}}
.

Remark 2.2.29
.

•

The W-lifted
\phi
map is well defined since
\phi^{\sharp}
maps ladder trees (introduced in Remark
2.2.9
) to ladder trees, thus the image of

\phi^{\sharp}\circ\iota_{\Omega_{1}}
is included in the image of
\iota_{\Omega_{2}}
.

•

The maps
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}
and
\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}
can be recursively defined by the relations:

\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}(\emptyset):=1_{\Omega},\qquad\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}\left((\omega)\sqcup w\right):=\phi\left(\omega\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\right)

for
\widehat{\phi}_{\mathcal{W}}
and

\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}(\emptyset):=\emptyset,\qquad\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}\left((\omega)\sqcup w\right):=\phi(\omega)\sqcup\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)

for
\phi^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}
.

2.2.5
Rota-Baxter maps and an important Theorem

The various structures defined above (branching, flattening, shuffles of words and rooted forests) are related through the notion of Rota-Baxter maps. I recall their definition now but will not enter more into this rich and fascinating topic. I refer instead the reader to
[
Guo12
]
for a rich and gentle introduction to the subject.

Definition 2.2.30
.

Let
\Omega
be a commutative algebra and
\lambda
a real number. A map
P:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
is a
Rota-Baxter map of weight

\lambda
if

P(a)P(b)=P(aP(b))+P(P(a)b)+\lambda P(ab)

(2.10)

for any
a
and
b
in
\Omega
.

Let me give standard examples of Rota-Baxter maps, which will be of importance in the next Sections.

Example 2.2.31
.

1.

Let
X\subseteq L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R})
be an algebra of locally integrable functions on
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the pointwise product of
functions stable under the integration map
f\mapsto\left(x\mapsto\int_{a}^{x}f(x)dx\right)
. Then for any

a
in
\mathbb{R}
, the integration map is a Rota-Baxter operator of weight
0
.

2.

Let
l(\mathbb{R})
be the algebra of sequences on
\mathbb{R}
equipped with the pointwise product of sequences. The summation operator

\Sigma:l(\mathbb{R})\longrightarrow l(\mathbb{R})
defined by
\Sigma(u_{n})(N):=\sum_{n=0}^{N}u_{n}
is a Rota-Baxter map of weight
-1
.

3.

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}_{-1}:\mathbb{N}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}
be the translation map by
-1
and
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{-1}
be its pull-back to
l(\mathbb{R})
. Then
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{-1}\Sigma
is
a Rota-Baxter map of weight
+1
.

The next result is the main Theorem of this long Section. The equivalences of the first three items were first shown in
[
CGPZ20b
]
in the locality framework and a partially new proof was given in
[
Cla20
]
. In the same paper, the proof of the equivalence of the fourth item was shown. Here, I will give the proof of
[
Cla20
]
.

Theorem 2.2.32
.

[
CGPZ18
, Theorem 2.13]
,
[
Cla20
, Theorems 2.20 and 5.8]
.

Let
\Omega
be a commutative algebra and
P:\Omega\longrightarrow\Omega
a linear map. For any real number
\lambda
, the following statements are equivalent:

1.

P
is a Rota-Baxter map of weight
\lambda
.

2.

The
P
-branched map factorises through words:
\widehat{P}=\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}\circ fl_{\lambda}
.

3.

\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}
is an algebra morphism for the
\lambda
-shuffle product, namely

\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w\shuffle_{\lambda}w^{\prime})=\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w^{\prime})
for any
w
and
w^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{W}_{\Omega}
.

4.

\widehat{P}
is an algebra morphism for the
\lambda
-shuffle product of rooted forests, namely, for any
F
and
F^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\Omega}
:

\widehat{P}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})=\widehat{P}(F)\widehat{P}(F^{\prime}).

Proof.

We prove the implications
(1)\Rightarrow(3)\Rightarrow(2)\Rightarrow(4)\Rightarrow(1)
.

•

(1)\Rightarrow(3)
: Assuming that
(1)
holds, we prove this result by induction on
p=|w|+|w^{\prime}|
. Let
P
be a Rota-Baxter operator of weight
\lambda
.

–

If
p=0
, then
w=w^{\prime}=\emptyset
and the result trivially holds as both sides are equal to
1
.

–

For
p
a natural number, let us assume that

\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w\shuffle_{\lambda}w^{\prime})=\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w^{\prime})
for any words
w
and
w^{\prime}
such that
|w|+|w^{\prime}|\leqslant p
. Let

w
and
w^{\prime}
be two words such that
|w|+|w^{\prime}|=p+1
. If
w=\emptyset
or
w^{\prime}=\emptyset
the result once again trivially holds. Otherwise
there
exist two words
\tilde{w}
and
\tilde{w}^{\prime}
(eventually empty) and
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
in
\Omega
such that
w=(\omega)\sqcup\tilde{w}
and

w^{\prime}=(\omega^{\prime})\sqcup\tilde{w}^{\prime}
. Then by definition of
\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}
we have

\displaystyle\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\widehat{P}_{\mathcal{W}}(w^{\prime})

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w})\right)P\left(\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w})P\left(\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)\right)+P\left(P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w})\right)\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\quad+\lambda P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w})\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w^{\prime})\right)+P\left({\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)+\lambda P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w})\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

were we have used that
P
is a Rota-Baxter map of weight
\lambda
.

On the other hand we have by definition of
\shuffle_{\lambda}
and from the definition of
{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}
:

\displaystyle{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w\shuffle_{\lambda}w^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left((\omega)\sqcup(\tilde{w}\shuffle_{\lambda}w^{\prime})\right)+{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left((\omega^{\prime})\sqcup(w\shuffle_{\lambda}\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)+\lambda{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left((\omega\omega^{\prime})\sqcup(\tilde{w}\shuffle_{\lambda}\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}\shuffle_{\lambda}w^{\prime})\right)+P\left(\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w\shuffle_{\lambda}\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)+\lambda P\left(\omega\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}\shuffle_{\lambda}\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w^{\prime})\right)+P\left(\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)+\lambda P\left(\omega\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

by the induction hypothesis

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w^{\prime})\right)+P\left({\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(w)\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)+\lambda P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w})\omega^{\prime}{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(\tilde{w}^{\prime})\right)

by commutativity of
\Omega
. This concludes this part of the proof.

•

(3)\Rightarrow(2)
: Assuming that
(3)
holds, we once again prove this result by induction, this time on
p=|F|
.

–

If
p=0
then
F=\emptyset
and the result trivially holds.

–

For any
p
a natural number, let us assume that
{\widehat{P}}(F)={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(fl_{\lambda}(F))
for any forest
F
such that
|F|\leqslant p
. Let
F
be a
forest
with exactly
p+1
vertices. Therefore
F
is nonempty and we have either
F=T=B_{+}^{\omega}(\tilde{F})
for some (eventually empty) forest

\tilde{F}
or
F=F_{1}F_{2}
with
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
nonempty forests. In the first case we have

\displaystyle{\widehat{P}}(F=T=B_{+}^{\omega}(\tilde{F}))

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}(\tilde{F})\right)\quad\text{by definition of }{\widehat{P}}

\displaystyle=P\left(\omega{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(fl_{\lambda}(\tilde{F}))\right)\quad\text{by the induction hypothesis}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left((\omega)\sqcup fl_{\lambda}(\tilde{F})\right)\quad\text{by definition of }{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F)\right)\quad\text{by definition of }fl_{\lambda}.

In the second case we have

\displaystyle{\widehat{P}}(F=F_{1}F_{2})

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}(F_{1}){\widehat{P}}(F_{2})\quad\text{by definition of }{\widehat{P}}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F_{1})\right){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F_{2})\right)\quad\text{by the induction hypothesis}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F_{1})\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(F_{2})\right)\quad\text{since }(iii)\text{ holds by assumption}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F_{1}F_{2})\right)\quad\text{by definition of }fl_{\lambda}.

This concludes the induction.

•

(2)\Rightarrow(4)
: Assuming that
(2)
holds we have for any rooted forests
F
and
F^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle{\widehat{P}}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}(fl_{\lambda}(F\shuffle_{\lambda}F^{\prime}))

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F)\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}F^{\prime})\right)\qquad\text{by Lemma \ref{lem:fl_shuffle}}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F)\right){\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(fl_{\lambda}(F)\right)\qquad\text{by definition of }{\widehat{P}}_{\mathcal{W}}

\displaystyle={\widehat{P}}(F){\widehat{P}}(F^{\prime})

since
(2)
holds.

•

(4)\Rightarrow(1)
: For any
a
and
b
in
\Omega
, writing
{\widehat{P}}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime})={\widehat{P}}(T){\widehat{P}}(T^{\prime})
with
T=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\end{picture}
and
T^{\prime}=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny b}\end{picture}
gives

{\widehat{P}}(T){\widehat{P}}(T^{\prime})=P(a)P(b)

one the one hand and

{\widehat{P}}(T\shuffle_{\lambda}T^{\prime})={\widehat{P}}(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny b}\end{picture})+{\widehat{P}}(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny b}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny a}\end{picture})+\lambda{\widehat{P}}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny a b}\end{picture}\hskip 5.69046pt)=P(aP(b))+P(bP(a))+\lambda P(ab)

thus
P
is a Rota-Baxter operator of weight
\lambda
.

Hence we have proven the four implications, thus the theorem.
∎

2.3
Arborified zeta values: series

2.3.1
Log-polyhomogeneous symbols

I start by recalling the basic definition of analytical objects that will play an essential role for the definition of arborified zeta values (AZVs) as iterated series. The presentation here follows closely
[
MP10
,
Pay12
]
.
Our goal is to use the universal property of rooted forests (Theorem
2.2.12
) for forests decorated by a suitable analytical space to define AZVs, and deduce their properties directly from their construction. Log-polyhomogeneous symbols are the suitable analytical space.

Definition 2.3.1
.

1.

Let
r\in\mathbb{R}
. A smooth function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}\to\mathbb{C}
is a
symbol
(with constant coefficients) of order
r
on

\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
if

\forall k\in\mathbb{N},\penalty\ \exists C_{k}:\penalty\ \forall x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1},\ |\partial_{x}^{k}\sigma(x)|\leqslant C_{k}x^{r-k}.

(2.11)

The set of such symbols of order
r
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
(with constant coefficients) is written
\mathcal{S}^{r}

2.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. A symbol
\sigma:\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a
classical symbol
also called
poly-homogeneous symbol
on

\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
of order
\alpha
if it lies in
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}
and if there exists a sequence

(a_{\alpha-j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
of real numbers such that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}

\sigma_{(N)}(x):=\sigma(x)-\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}a_{\alpha-j}x^{\alpha-j}

(2.12)

lies in
\mathcal{S}^{\alpha-N}
. When this is the case, I will use the following classical short hand notation

\sigma\sim\sum\sigma_{\alpha-j}

where
\sigma_{\alpha-j}
is the function defined by
\sigma_{\alpha-j}(x)=a_{\alpha-j}x^{\alpha-j}
.
I denote by
CS^{\alpha}
the set of classical symbols on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
of order
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and I set

CS:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}CS^{\alpha},\qquad CS^{\leqslant\beta}:=\sum_{\alpha\leqslant\beta}CS^{\alpha}

the linear span of classical symbols of all orders resp. of order less or equal to
\beta
).

3.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. A
log-polyhomogeneous symbol of order
(\alpha,k)
is a function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

such that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}

f(x)=\sum_{l=0}^{k}f_{l}(x)\log^{l}(x)

(2.13)

with
f_{l}\in CS^{\alpha}
. I denote by
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}
the real vector space spanned by log-polyhomogeneous symbol of
order

(\alpha,k)
and also define

\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;*}:=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k};\quad\mathcal{P}^{*;*}:=\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathcal{P}^{*;k}.

with
\mathcal{P}^{*;k}
the linear span over
\mathbb{R}
of all
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 2.3.2
.

In general, linear combination of symbols (resp. classical symbols, log-polyhomogeneous symbols) of same orders can give a symbols (resp. classical symbol, log-polyhomogeneous symbol) of lower degree. However, it is clear from the definition that
\mathcal{S}^{r}
is a vector space since
r\leqslant r^{\prime}\penalty\ \Longrightarrow S^{r}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1})\subseteq S^{r^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1})
. The same is true for
CS^{\leqslant\alpha}
.

I choose to restrict our discussion to the case
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Symbols (resp. classical, log-polyhomogeneous) of complex orders can be introduced in a similar fashion, however we will not require this level of generality for the construction and study of stuffle arborified zeta values. For the same reason I choose to define these objects on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
rather than on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. I did not indicate that these functions are defined on
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
on the notations
\mathcal{S}^{r}
,
CS^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha,k}
for the sake of readability.

Remark 2.3.3
.

Besides these restrictions, the above definition of log-polyhomogeneous symbols differs slightly to the one of
[
MP10
]
. I require the functions
f_{l}
to be classical symbols rather than symbols. This is because these objects appear more naturally in the subsequent developments.

For any connected subset
I
of
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
let
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha,k}(I)
be the sets of log-polyhomogeneous symbols over
I
. These objects have the same definition than
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1})
with
x\in\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
replaced by
x\in I
. Notice that
for two connected subsets
I
,
J
of
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
,
I\subseteq J\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\alpha,k}(J)\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\alpha,k}(I)
.

The following Lemma is a direct consequence of the definition of log-polyhomogeneous symbols and standard results of real analysis:

Lemma 2.3.4
.

Let
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}(I)
be a log-polyhomogeneous symbol over
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
with
I
unbounded. If
\alpha<0
, then
\sigma
admits a finite
limit in
+\infty
for any
k
.
If
\alpha=0
then
\sigma
admits a finite limit in
+\infty
if and only if
k=0
.

In the following Sections, the case of classical symbols with integer orders will play an important role. In particular, we will need to integrate them. This justifies the introduction of log-polyhomogeneous symbols since the latter, contrarily to classical symbols with integer orders, are stable under integration.

Before moving on to the the Euler-MacLaurin formula, I should recall some important properties of log-polyhomogeneous symbols. Although, as pointed out before, the definition used here of log-polyhomogeneous symbols differs slightly from the one of
[
Pay12
]
, it is simple enough to check that the proofs of
[
Pay12
]
still work.

Proposition 2.3.5
.

(
[
Pay12
, Proposition 2.55]
)
Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for any real number
a<0
, the pull-back translation map
\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{a}
stabilises
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}
i.e.
for any
\sigma\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}

\mathfrak{t}^{*}_{a}\sigma:=\left(x\longmapsto\sigma(x+a)\right)

is an element of
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}
.

The last useful property of log-polyhomogeneous symbols is very simple to prove, see for example
[
Pay12
, Exercice 2.18]
. It reads:

Proposition 2.3.6
.

The space
\mathcal{P}^{*;*}
is a bifiltrated, i.e. for any
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
and
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\displaystyle\alpha\leqslant\beta\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ \alpha-\beta\in\mathbb{Z}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\beta;k};

\displaystyle k\leqslant l\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;l};

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}.\mathcal{P}^{\beta;l}\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\alpha+\beta;k+l}.

2.3.2
Euler-MacLaurin formula

Following the ideas developed in
[
CGPZ20b
]
, the goal is to define AZVs by iterating a summation operator on log-polyhomogeneous symbols:

S(\sigma)(N):=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sigma(n).

Of course, the operator
S
does not stabilise the spaces of classical symbols nor log-polyhomogeneous symbols. So we use the
Euler-MacLaurin formula
to interpolate
S
at non-integer values. Recall that the Euler-MacLaurin formula reads

\displaystyle S(\sigma)(N)

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{N}\sigma(x)dx+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sigma(N)+\sigma(1)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{K}\frac{B_{k}}{k!}\,\left(\sigma^{(k-1)}(N)-\sigma^{(k-1)}(1)\right)+\frac{(-1)^{K+1}}{K!}\int_{1}^{N}\overline{B_{K}}(x)\,\sigma^{(K)}(x)\,dx

(2.14)

where
\overline{B_{k}}(x)=B_{k}\left(x-\lfloor x\rfloor\right)
with
\lfloor x\rfloor
the integer part of the real number
x
, and
B_{k}(x)
the
k
-th Bernoulli polynomial. Notice that (
2.3.2
) holds for any
K\geqslant 2
.

Following once more
[
MP10
]
and
[
CGPZ20a
]
we define an operator
P
acting on smooth functions by

\displaystyle P(\sigma)(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{x}\sigma(t)dt+\frac{1}{2}\left(\sigma(t)+\sigma(1)\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=2}^{K}\frac{B_{k}}{k!}\,\left(\sigma^{(k-1)}(t)-\sigma^{(k-1)}(1)\right)+\frac{(-1)^{K+1}}{K!}\int_{1}^{x}\overline{B_{K}}(t)\,\sigma^{(K)}(t)\,dt.

(2.15)

Notice that for any
N\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
we have
P(\sigma)(N)=S(\sigma)(N)
:
P
interpolates the discrete sum
S(\sigma)
.

Lemma 2.3.7
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
we have

\int:\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;l}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\alpha+1;l}+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}.

Furthermore

\int:\mathcal{P}^{-1;l}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{0;l+1}+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}.

In both cases, the operator
\int
is defined by
(\int f)(x):=\int_{1}^{x}\sigma(t)dt
.

Proof.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
we have for
\sigma
in
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;l}

\int_{1}^{x}\sigma(t)dt=\sum_{l=0}^{k}\left[\sigma_{j=0}^{N_{l}}a_{\alpha-j}^{(l)}\int_{1}^{x}t^{\alpha-j}\log^{l}(t)dt+\int_{1}^{x}\sigma_{(N_{l}),l}(t)\log^{l}(t)dt\right]

where we used (
2.12
) with obvious notations.

•

Using that, if
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
then
\alpha-j\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we prove by induction
on
l
that

x\mapsto\int_{1}^{x}t^{\alpha}\log^{l}(t)dt\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha+1;l}+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}.

The case
l=0
is shown by direct integration.

Assuming that this result holds for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
, we have, by integration by parts

\int_{1}^{x}t^{\alpha}\log^{l+1}(t)dt=\frac{x^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\log^{l}(x)+\frac{l}{\alpha+1}\int_{1}^{x}t^{\alpha}\log^{l}(t)dt.

By the induction hypothesis, we have
\left(x\mapsto\int_{1}^{x}t^{\alpha}\log^{l}(t)dt\right)\in\mathcal{P}^{\alpha+1;l}+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}
; then Proposition

2.3.6
allows to conclude this induction.

•

To end this proof, it is now enough to show that, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
and
l\in\mathbb{N}
, if

\tau\in{}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1})
then it exists
\rho\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1})
and
K\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\int_{1}^{x}\tau(t)\log^{l}(t)dt=\rho(x)\log^{l}(x)+K.

Clearly,
\int
maps smooth functions of
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
to smooth functions of
\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1}
. Moreover, using the
bound (
2.11
) we have

\left|\int_{1}^{x}\tau(t)\log^{l}(t)dt\right|\leqslant C\int_{1}^{x}t^{\alpha}\log^{l}(t)dt

For some
C\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the induction of the previous point allows us to conclude.

In the case of
\mathcal{P}^{-1;l}
the same arguments hold but the integration (resp. integration by parts) will add one logarithm.
∎

We further recall a classical Lemma of the theory of log-polyhomogeneous symbols

Lemma 2.3.8
.

The differentiation operator
\partial:\sigma\mapsto\sigma^{\prime}
sends
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;l}
to
\mathcal{P}^{\alpha-1;l}
. We also have, for any

\sigma\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1})
that
\partial(\sigma\log^{l})=\tau\log^{l}
for some
\tau\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha-1}
.

We can now state and prove the main Proposition of this subsection. This result is a refinement of
[
MP10
, Proposition 8]
.

Proposition 2.3.9
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have for the operator
P
of Equation (
2.3.2
)

\displaystyle P:\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\alpha+1;k}+\mathcal{P}^{0;0};

\displaystyle P:\mathcal{P}^{-1;k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{0;k+1}+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}.

Proof.

1.

Let us start with the case
\alpha\notin\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 1}
. Applying Lemmas
2.3.7
and
2.3.8
in the Euler-MacLaurin
formula
(
2.3.2
) we see that its is enough to show that it exists
\tau\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha+1-N}
so that, for a big enough
K

\int_{1}^{x}\overline{B_{K}}(t)\,\sigma^{(K)}(t)\,dt=\tau(x)\log^{k}(x).

Using that
\overline{B_{K}}(t)
is bounded and twice Lemma
2.3.8
and Lemma
2.3.7
we obtain that such a

\tau\in\mathcal{S}^{\alpha-K+1}
exists. Therefore we obtain the desired bound by taking
K\geqslant N
.

2.

The case
\alpha=-1
is exactly similar, using the second part of Lemma
2.3.7
.

∎

2.3.3
Arborified zeta values as series

Definition 2.3.10
.

Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathbb{N}^{*}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{*;*}
be the map defined by

\mathcal{R}(\alpha):=\left(x\mapsto x^{-\alpha}\right)

for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

We lift
\mathcal{R}
as detailed in Definition
2.2.16
to obtain the lifted
\mathcal{R}
-map
\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{*;*}}
.

Definition 2.3.11
.

For
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
, we define the operator
{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}
as
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}_{\lambda}P
. We further define
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}:=\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{\lambda}\circ\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{*;*}

The simple, yet important subsequent Lemma is a consequence of Propositions
2.3.9
and
2.3.5

Lemma 2.3.12
.

For any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant-1}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}:\mathcal{P}^{\alpha;k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\alpha+1;k}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda})+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda})

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}:\mathcal{P}^{-1;k}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{0;k+1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda})+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda}).

Before stating the main analytic property of
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}
, let us recall some useful notations for forests decorated by
\mathbb{N}^{*}
:
for any
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated forest
(F,d)
, we write

1.

|F|:=|V(F)|
as usual,

2.

\sharp_{n}F
the number of vertices of
F
decorated by
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
:

\sharp_{n}F:=|\{v\in V(F):d(v)=n\}|.

Proposition 2.3.13
.

For any nonempty
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated tree
(T,d)
of root
r
we have

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(T)\in\mathcal{P}^{1-d(r),\sharp_{1}T}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|}).

For any nonempty
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated tree
(F,d)
with
F=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
, with
T_{i}
nonempty and of root
r_{i}
for any
i\in\{1,\cdots k\}
,
we have

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F)\in\mathcal{P}^{1-r_{\rm min}(F),\sharp_{1}F}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})

with
r_{\rm min}(F):=\min_{i=1,\cdots,k}d(r_{i})
.

Proof.

We prove this result by induction on the number
n=|F|
of vertices of the forest.

For the case
n=1
we have, for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{*}

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny$\alpha$}\end{picture})={\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}(t\mapsto t^{-\alpha}).

Since
(t\mapsto t^{-\alpha})
lies in
\mathcal{P}^{-\alpha,0}
, the result follows from Lemma
2.3.12
.

Let us assume this result to hold for any nonempty forests of
n
or fewer vertices. Let
F
be a forest of
n+1
vertices. We distinguish
two cases.

•

If
F=F_{1}F_{2}
with
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
non empty then we can write

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F)=\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F_{1})\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F_{2}).

Indeed
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}
being the composition of two algebra morphisms, it is an algebra morphism. Then, using the induction hypothesis, the filtration properties
of Proposition
2.3.6
(which can be applied since
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F_{i})
has integer order) and the remark that
I\subseteq J\Rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\alpha,k}(J)\subseteq\mathcal{P}^{\alpha,k}(I)
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F)

\displaystyle\in\mathcal{P}^{2-r_{\rm min}(F_{1})-r_{\rm min}(F_{2}),\sharp_{1}F}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})+\mathcal{P}^{1-r_{\rm min}(F_{1}),\sharp_{1}F_{1}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})

\displaystyle+\mathcal{P}^{1-r_{\rm min}(F_{2}),\sharp_{1}F_{2}}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|F|})

which, by Proposition
2.3.6
implies the result for
F
since
1-r_{\rm min}(F_{1})\leqslant 0
;
1-r_{\rm min}(F_{2})\leqslant 0
and
since each of the orders are integers.

•

In the case
F=T
a tree, there exists a nonempty forest
\tilde{F}
and a positive natural number
\alpha
such that

T=B_{+}^{\alpha}(\tilde{F})
since
|T|=n+1\geqslant 2
. We then have

\mathcal{Z}(T)={\mathfrak{S}}\left(t\mapsto t^{-\alpha}\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{F})(t)\right).

Using the induction hypothesis on
\tilde{F}
and the bifiltration property we then obtain

t\mapsto t^{-\alpha}\mathcal{Z}(\tilde{F})(t)\in\mathcal{P}^{1-d_{\rm min}-\alpha,\sharp_{1}\tilde{F}}(\mathbb{R}_{1-\lambda n})+\mathcal{P}^{-\alpha,0}(\mathbb{R}_{1-\lambda n}).

In the case
\alpha\geqslant 2
, from Lemma
2.3.12
and the observation that
1-d_{\rm min}\leqslant 0
, we obtain the desired result, once again
using Proposition
2.3.6
(since in this case
\sharp_{1}T=\sharp_{1}\tilde{F}
), which can be used for the first and the second indices as well.

In the case
\alpha=1
, the same argument holds if one notices that
\sharp_{1}T=\sharp_{1}\tilde{F}+1
.

These two cases allow to conclude the proof of the Theorem.

∎

We now introduce the set of rooted forests on which arborified zeta values will be defined.

Definition 2.3.14
.

A
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated tree
(T,d)
is called
convergent
if it is empty or if it has a root
r
and
d(r)\geqslant 2
, i.e. if the decoration of its root is strictly greater than one. A
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated forest

(F=T_{1}\cdots T_{k},d)
is called
convergent
if
T_{i}
is convergent for each
i\in\{1,\cdots,k\}
. Let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
be the
subalgebra of convergent forests.

It is clear that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
that is a subalgebra of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
for the concatenation of rooted forests since by definition
\emptyset\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
is stable by concatenation of forests.

We can finally define arborified zeta values. This definition is illustrated by diagram
2.3
below.

Definition-Proposition 2.3.15
.

For any convergent
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated forest
F
and
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F)(x)
admits a finite limit as
x
goes to infinity. We define the maps

\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup},\zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(F):=\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{-1}(F)(x),\qquad\zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(F):=\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{0}(F)(x)

for any
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
.

Proof.

For any convergent forest
F
we have
1-d_{\rm min}\leqslant-1
. Therefore by Lemma
2.3.4
and Proposition

2.3.13
the limits at
+\infty
of
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}(F)
are well-defined and
finite for
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
provided that
F
is convergent.
∎

Let us notice that the arborified zeta values defined here coincide with the branched zeta values of
[
CGPZ18
]
in the convergent case where the renormalisation scheme reduces to a simple evaluation at
0
of the regularisation
parameters. We could therefore have defined AZVs through these branched zeta values. We have opted for this chapter to not contain the divergent counterparts of AZVs since these were introduced as a testing ground for the multivariate renormalisation scheme that is the main topic of the next chapter.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
\mathbb{R}
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{*;*}}
\mathcal{P}^{*;*}
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup},\penalty\ \zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}
\widehat{{\mathfrak{S}}}_{\lambda}
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}
{\rm ev}_{\infty}

Figure 2.3:
Definition of arborified zeta values.

Using the fact that
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda}
and
{\rm ev}_{\infty}
are both algebra morphisms, we obtain the simple, yet important subsequent Proposition

Proposition 2.3.16
.

The maps
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
and
\zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
are algebra morphisms for the concatenation product of trees.

Remark 2.3.17
.

Let us notice that the techniques used above can also be used to define branched Euler sums which correspond (in the words case) to convergent MZVs with some negative arguments. These objects are an active area of
research, see for example
[
XW20
]
.

2.3.4
Applying the main theorem

The construction of the previous subsection can be adapted to build multiple zeta values instead of arborified zetas simply by replacing

\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}
by
\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}
by
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda,\mathcal{W}}
. This gives an alternative construction of MZVs which will allow us to derive algebraic proofs of classical results.

Definition 2.3.18
.

For
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
, let
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda,\mathcal{W}}:\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}^{*;*}
be the operator defined by

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda,\mathcal{W}}:=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{W}}^{\sharp}\circ\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{W}}
.

We have an important analytic result which is proven in exactly the same fashion as Proposition
2.3.13
, with only the case
w=C_{+}^{\omega}(\tilde{w})
to be
considered.

Proposition 2.3.19
.

For any nonempty word
w=(\omega_{1}\cdots\omega_{n})
, we have

\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda,\mathcal{W}}(w)\in\mathcal{P}^{1-\omega_{1},\sharp_{1}w}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|w|})+\mathcal{P}^{0;0}(\mathbb{R}_{\geqslant 1-\lambda|w|}).

Recall (Definition
2.1.11
) that a word
w
written in the alphabet
\mathbb{N}^{*}
is called convergent if it is empty or if its first letter on the left is
greater or equal to
2
and that
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
is the
linear span of convergent words.

As the terminology suggests, convergent words are the convergence domain of MZVs, for which we now give an alternative definition.

Definition-Proposition 2.3.20
.

For any convergent word written in the alphabet
\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
,
\mathcal{Z}_{\lambda,\mathcal{W}}(w)(x)
admits a finite limit as
x

goes to infinity. We define the maps

\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup},\zeta^{\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}:\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
by

\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(w):=\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{-1,\mathcal{W}}(w)(x),\qquad\zeta^{\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(w):=\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{0,\mathcal{W}}(w)(x)

for any
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
.

Proof.

The proof of the statement is similar to the proof of Definition-Proposition
2.3.15
.
∎

We can now start to deduce the algebraic proprties of MZVs from this definition. Before this, we need a very classical result.

Lemma 2.3.21
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
,
(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv},\emptyset,\shuffle_{\lambda})
is a subalgebra of
(\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}},\emptyset,\shuffle_{\lambda})
.

Proof.

By definition
\emptyset\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
. The rest of the proof is easily carried out by induction on
|w|+|w^{\prime}|
for two convergent words

w
and
w^{\prime}
, using Definition
2.1.7
of the product
\shuffle_{\lambda}
.
∎

Proposition 2.3.22
.

The map
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
is a algebra morphism for the stuffle product
{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}=\shuffle_{1}
. Furthermore the map
\zeta^{\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
is
an algebra morphism for the anti-stuffle product
\shuffle_{-1}
.

Proof.

By Examples
2.2.31
,
3
(resp.
2.2.31
,
2
) we know that
{\mathfrak{S}}_{-1}
(resp.
{\mathfrak{S}}_{0}
), when restricted
to
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, is a Rota-Baxter map of weight
+1
(resp
-1
). One can then apply Theorem
2.2.32

3
to get that

N\mapsto\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{-1,\mathcal{W}}(N)
(resp.
N\mapsto\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{0,\mathcal{W}}(N)
), is an algebra morphism for the stuffle
(resp. anti-stuffle) product. Taking the limit
N\to\infty
gives to the result.
∎

We can now look at properties of AZVs and in particular relate them to MZVs but before we will need a simple Lemma.

Lemma 2.3.23
.

For any
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, the flatting map
fl_{\lambda}
maps convergent forests to convergent words. Furthermore,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
is a subalgebra for the
\lambda
-shuffle of rooted forests
\shuffle^{T}_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

One can perform an easy proof by induction on the number of vertices of the convergent forest. For the empty forest, we have

fl_{\lambda}(\emptyset)\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
. For a nonempty convergent tree
T=B_{+}^{n\geqslant 2}(F)
for some forest
F
. Then

fl_{\lambda}(T)=C_{+}^{n\geqslant 2}(fl_{\lambda}(F))\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
. Finally for the case
F=F_{1}F_{2}
, we have that
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are
convergent if
F
is and therefore
fl_{\lambda}(F)=fl_{\lambda}(F_{1})\shuffle_{\lambda}fl_{\lambda}(F_{2})\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
by the
induction hypothesis and Lemma
2.3.21
.

Similarly, if
T
and
T^{\prime}
(reps.
F
and
F^{\prime}
) are convergent trees (resp. forest), it is clear from the definition of the shuffle of rooted forests that
T\shuffle^{T}_{\lambda}T^{\prime}
(resp.
F\shuffle^{T}_{\lambda}F^{\prime}
) is a linear combination of convergent trees (resp. forests).
∎

We can now state our main result.

Theorem 2.3.24
.

For any convergent forest
F
, the convergent arborified zeta value
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(F)
(resp.
\zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(F)
) is a finite
linear combination of convergent multiple zeta values
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(w)
(resp.
\zeta^{\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(w)
) with rational coefficients and can be written as a finite
linear combination of multiple zeta values with integer coefficients given by the
1
-flattening (resp. the
-1
-flattening):

\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T}=\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\circ fl_{1}\qquad\text{(resp. }\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T,\star}=\zeta^{\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\circ fl_{-1}\text{)}.

Futhermore, the map
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
(resp.
\zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
) is an algebra morphism for the stuffle product of rooted forest
{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T}:=\shuffle^{T}_{1}
(resp. for the product
\shuffle^{T}_{-1}
).

Proof.

Let
F
be any convergent forest.

Once again, by Examples
2.2.31
,
3
(resp.
2.2.31
,
2
) we know that
{\mathfrak{S}}_{-1}
(resp.

{\mathfrak{S}}_{0}
), when restricted to
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, is a Rota-Baxter map of weight
+1
(resp
-1
). Applying Theorem
2.2.32

2
we
get that
N\mapsto\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{-1}(F)(N)
(resp.
N\mapsto\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{0}(F)(N)
), when restricted to
\mathbb{N}^{*}
, factorises through words, i.e.
that
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}(F)(N)=\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda,\mathcal{W}}\circ fl_{\lambda}(F)(N)
for any
N
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant|F|+1}
and

\lambda\in\{0,-1\}
.

Then by Lemma
2.3.23
we can take the limit
N\to\infty
in both sides which gives the result.
We obtain a finite sum with integer coefficients thanks to Proposition
2.2.27
.

Finally, the fact that
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
(resp.
\zeta^{\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
) is an algebra morphism for the stuffle product of rooted forest
{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T}
(resp. for the product
\shuffle^{T}_{-1}
) follows from the fact that it is a composition of algebra morphisms for
{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T}
(resp.
\shuffle^{T}_{-1}
). First, once again, by Examples
2.2.31
and
3
we can use Theorem
2.2.32

4
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\lambda}
. Furthermore,
\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}
is an algebra morphism of the shuffle products of rooted forests as well since for any algebras morphism
P:\Omega_{1}\longrightarrow\Omega_{2}
between two commutative algebras, the lifted map

P^{\sharp}:\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{1}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\Omega_{2}}
is an algebra morphism for the
\lambda
-shuffles of trees, for any value of

\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. This clearly holds from the definition of
P^{\sharp}
and the fact that
P
is an algebra morphism. However, it is can also be easily proven, once again by induction on the number of vertices of forests, using the fact that

P^{\sharp}
is a morphism of operated algebras.

Notice that the result makes sense by the stability property of Lemma
2.3.23
. In any case,
composing these algebra morphisms we obtain another algebra morphism, which is no other than
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
(resp.
\zeta^{T,\star}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
).
∎

The results on AZVs obtained so far, the definitions and relations between branched zetas and multiple zetas are summarized as the
commutativity of diagram
2.5
below. Notice that the left-most arrow should be
fl_{1}
for the un-starred AZVs and MZVs, and
fl_{-1}
for the starred AZVs and MZVs.

\mathcal{W}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
\mathcal{W}_{\mathcal{P}^{*;*}}
\mathcal{P}^{*;*}
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{P}^{*;*}}
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
\mathbb{R}
\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}_{\mathcal{W}}
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}_{\mathcal{W}}
\lim_{+\infty}
\mathcal{R}^{\sharp}
\widehat{\mathfrak{S}}
\iota_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
\iota_{\mathcal{P}^{*;*}}
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup},\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{\star}
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup},\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T;\star}
fl_{\pm 1}

Figure 2.4:
Multiple zetas and arborified zetas.

2.4
Shuffle arborified zeta values

2.4.1
Chen integrals and arborification

In
[
Che77
]
iterated integrals are recursively defined. One way to define them is as a map
Ch:\mathcal{W}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}(I)
; where
I=[a,b]
is a
closed interval,
\mathcal{I}(I)
is the set of continuous, integrable functions over
I
and
X=\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{N}\}
is a finite subset of
\mathcal{I}(I)
.
In
[
Che77
]
this recursive definition goes as follows:

Definition-Proposition 2.4.1
.

Let
I=[a,b]
be a closed interval in
\mathbb{R}
and
X=\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{N}\}
be a finite set with
f_{i}:I\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
smooth, continuous functions over

I
.
Ch:\mathcal{W}_{X}\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}(I)
is the linear map, whose action on the basis elements of
\mathcal{W}_{X}
is recursively defined by

\displaystyle Ch(\emptyset)

\displaystyle:=\left(x\mapsto 1\penalty\ \forall x\in I\right)=:{\bf 1},

\displaystyle Ch((f_{i})\sqcup w)

\displaystyle:=\left(x\mapsto\int_{a}^{x}f_{i}(t)Ch(w)(t)dt\penalty\ \forall x\in I\right)

for any
f_{i}
in
X
and
w
in
\mathcal{W}_{X}
.

Proof.

In order to prove that this definition is consistent, one has to prove that
Ch(w)
lies in
\mathcal{I}(I)
for any word
w
in
\mathcal{W}_{X}
.
We show by induction that it exists
M\in\mathbb{R}
such that for any
x
in
I
and
w
in
\mathcal{W}_{X}
, one has

|Ch(w)(x)|\leqslant\left(M|b-a|\right)^{|w|}.

This can easily be done by induction on the length of the word
w
. For a word of length
0
, it is trivially true.
Now, since each
f_{i}
in
X
is continuous over the closed interval
I
,
f_{i}
is bounded by some constant
M_{i}
. Let

M:=\max_{i=1,\cdots,n}M_{i}
. If
|Ch(w)(x)|\leqslant\left(M|b-a|\right)^{|w|}
holds for every word
w
of length
n\geqslant 1
, then one has for any

f_{i}
in
X

|Ch((f_{i})\sqcup w)(x)|\leqslant\int_{a}^{x}|f_{i}(t)Ch(w)(t)|dt\leqslant\left(M|b-a|\right)^{|w|+1}.

This shows that
|Ch(w)(x)|\leqslant\left(M|b-a|\right)^{|w|}
for any
x
in
I
and
w
in
\mathcal{W}_{X}
. Continuity of
Ch(w)
follows from the
standard theorems of integration theory.
∎

We denote by
Ch_{X}(I)
the algebra (for the pointwise product) freely generated by the image of
Ch
. It admits
{\bf 1}=Ch(\emptyset)
as a unit.

This approach shows that Chen integrals are the elements of the image of a map
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}_{\mathcal{W}}
going from
\mathcal{W}_{X}
to a subset of

\mathcal{I}(I)
, where
\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}
is the integration map defined by
\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}(f)(x):=\int_{a}^{x}f(t)dt
for
x
in
[a,b]
. Indeed, standard results of integration theory
states that, since the interval
I
is closed, the image of
\mathcal{I}(I)
under the map
\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}
lies in
\mathcal{I}(I)
. This suggest a natural
generalisation of Chen integrals to arborified Chen integrals. Notice that this type of generalisation exists in the related context of rough paths, where one can build branched rough paths, see for example
[
HK15
]
.

Definition 2.4.2
.

Let
I=[a,b]
and
\mathcal{I}(I)
be as above. Let
\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}:\mathcal{I}(I)\longrightarrow\mathcal{I}(I)
be
the integration map defined as above by
\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}(f)(x):=\int_{a}^{x}f(t)dt
. Let
X=\{f_{1},\cdots,f_{N}\}
is a finite subset of
\mathcal{I}(I)
.

arborified Chen integrals
are elements of the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}:\mathcal{F}_{X}\mapsto\mathcal{I}(I)
.

Remark 2.4.3
.

One could recursively prove that
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}
is well-defined as in the case of words, that is that the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}

is indeed in
\mathcal{I}(I)
. However by Definition
2.2.14
we already have this result since
\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}(\mathcal{I}(I))\subseteq\mathcal{I}(I)
.

Proposition 2.4.4
.

Any arborified Chen integral is a finite sum of Chen iterated integrals with rational coefficients.

Proof.

This follows from the observation above that Chen iterated integrals are elements of the image of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}_{\mathcal{W}}
. Then by Example

2.2.31

1
we can apply Theorem
2.2.32
to
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}
which gives

\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}=\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{a}^{b}}_{\mathcal{W}}\circ fl_{0}
. For any finite forest
F
,
fl_{0}(F)
is a finite sum of words with rational coefficients by
Proposition
2.2.27
, thus
we obtain the result.
∎

2.4.2
Arborified polylogarithms

We specialise the construction of the previous subsection to the case
X=\{\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}\}
with
\sigma_{x}(t):=1/t
and

\sigma_{y}(t):=1/(1-t)
. Furthermore the above construction is carried out on
I=[\epsilon,z]
with
0<\epsilon<z<1
.

However arborified polylogarithms (resp. arborified shuffle zeta values) should be defined (up to convergence issues) as a map

Li^{T}:\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}\longrightarrow\Omega
(resp.
\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}:\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
) for a suitable space
\Omega
of functions. In order
to build such a map, we follow the same strategy as for stuffle zeta values. Let
\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}:\{x,y\}\longrightarrow\{\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y}\}
defined
by
\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}(\varepsilon)=\sigma_{\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon
in
\{x,y\}
.

Definition 2.4.5
.

A forest
F
in
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
is called
semi-convergent
if each of its leaves and branching vertices are decorated by
y
and
convergent
if it is
semi-convergent and each of its roots are decorated by
x
. The linear span of semi-convergent forests is denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm semi}_{\{x,y\}}

and the linear span of convergent forests is denoted by
\mathcal{F}^{\rm conv}_{\{x,y\}}
.

Similarly, a word
w
in
\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}
is called
semi-convergent
if it is empty or if it ends by
y
and
convergent
if it is empty or if it ends by
y
and starts by
x
.
We write
\mathcal{W}^{\rm semi}_{\{x,y\}}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\rm conv}_{\{x,y\}}
the linear span of semi-convergent and convergent words respectively.

This various notions are stable under natural product.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

\mathcal{F}^{\rm semi}_{\{x,y\}}
and
\mathcal{F}^{\rm conv}_{\{x,y\}}
are subalgebras of
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
for the concatenation of forests;
\mathcal{W}^{\rm semi}_{\{x,y\}}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\rm conv}_{\{x,y\}}

are subalgebras of
\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}
for the shuffle product
\shuffle
.

Proof.

As before, the result trivially holds for forests. For words the result follows from the fact that for any two words
w
and
w^{\prime}
, then we can write
w\shuffle w^{\prime}=\sum_{i}w_{i}
and the first (resp. last) letter
of each
w_{i}
is the first (resp. last) letter of
w
or
w^{\prime}
.
∎

In order to state an important result of this Section, let us recall the definition of multiple polylogarithms.

Definition 2.4.7
.

Let
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}
be a word either empty or whose last letter is
y
. The
single variable multiple polylogarithm
(shortened in multiple polylogarithm in what follows) attached to
w
is defined by

\displaystyle Li_{w}(z):=\begin{cases}&0\quad{\rm for}\quad z=0,\\
&\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp,\mathcal{W}}(w)\right)\quad{\rm for}\quad z\in]0,1[.\end{cases}

We write
Li:\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,1[)
the map which, to such a word, associates the map
z\mapsto Li_{w}(z)
.

The existence of the limit for semi-convergent words and the fact that
Li_{w}
is a smooth map are well-known results of polylogarithms theory, see for example
[
Bro09
]
.

Definition-Proposition 2.4.8
.

For any
z\in]0,1[
and semi-convergent
F
the limit

Li^{T}_{F}(z):=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp}(F)\right)

exists. Setting
Li^{T}_{F}(0)=0
we obtain a map

\displaystyle Li^{T}_{F}:[0,1[

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\longmapsto Li^{T}_{F}(z)

which we call the
arborified polylogarithm
associated to the semi-convergent forest
F
. The
arborified polylogarithm map
is defined
by its action
Li^{T}:F\longrightarrow Li^{T}_{F}
semi-convergent forests.

Proof.

One needs to prove the existence of the limit for any
z\in[0,1[
. It follows from Example
2.2.31
,
1
that we can
apply Theorem
2.2.32
with
\lambda=0
. Furthermore, one easily shows by induction that the image of a semi-convergent forest under
the map
fl_{0}
is a finite sum of words, each ending with
y
. As stated above, it is a well-known fact (see for example
[
Bro09
]
) that for such a word
w
,
the limit

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}_{\mathcal{W}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp,\mathcal{W}}(w)\right)

exists. The result then follows.
∎

We have a simple but important compatibility Lemma whose proof is the same than the one of Lemma
2.3.23
.

Lemma 2.4.9
.

The flattening map of weight
0
maps
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}
(resp.
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
) to
\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}
(resp.
\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
).
Furthermore,
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
are subalgebras for the
0
-shuffle of rooted forests
\shuffle^{T}_{0}
.

We then have our main theorem on arborified polylogarithms:

Theorem 2.4.10
.

For any semi-convergent forest
F
, the arborified polylogarithm associated to
F
enjoys the following properties:

1.

It is a finite sum of multiple polylogarithms with rational coefficients that can be written as a finite
linear combination of multiple polylogarithms with integer coefficients;

2.

It is a smooth map on
[0,1[
;

3.

The arborified polylogarithm map
Li^{T}:F\longrightarrow Li^{T}_{F}
is a algebra morphism for the concatenation of trees and the pointwise product of functions.

4.

The arborified polylogarithm map
Li^{T}:F\longrightarrow Li^{T}_{F}
is a algebra morphism for the shuffle product
\shuffle^{T}:=\shuffle^{T}_{0}
of rooted forests and the pointwise product of functions.

Proof.

1.

The proof of this result is the same as the proof of Theorem
2.3.24
, using Example
2.2.31
,
1
. The limits in the definition of
Li_{w}
are well-defined
by Lemma
2.4.9
.

2.

The second point follows from the first, since multiple polylogarithms are smooth maps of
[0,1[
and by the existance of the limit in the definition of
Li^{T}
.

3.

This follows from the fact that
Li^{T}
is the composition of
\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}_{\mathcal{W}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp,\mathcal{W}}
, which are both algebra morphisms.
Furthermore, if
FF^{\prime}
is a semi-convergent forest, then
F
and
F^{\prime}
are two semi-convergent forests. Then
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp}(F)\right)

and
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp}(F^{\prime})\right)
exist and their product is equal to

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left[\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp}(F)\right)\widehat{\mathcal{I}_{\epsilon}^{z}}\left(\mathcal{R}_{\{x,y\}}^{\sharp}(F)\right)\right]

Thus the limit in the definition of
Li^{T}
is also an algebra morphism and
Li^{T}
is an algebra morphism as stated.

4.

Once again the proof of this result is as the proof of Theorem
2.3.24
. It is an application of Theorem
2.2.32

4
together with Example
2.2.31
. The result make sense by the stability property of Lemma
2.4.9
.

∎

As in the case of the stuffle branched zeta values, one can use this framework to provide a new proof that multiple polylogarithms are algebra morphisms for the shuffle product.

Proposition 2.4.11
.

The map
Li:\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{\infty}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
is an algebra morphism for the shuffle product.

Proof.

The proof follows the same steps as the proof of Proposition
2.3.22
.
∎

2.4.3
Arborified zeta values as integrals

It is well-known (see for example
[
Bro09
]
or
[
Wal11
]
