) that for a convergent word
w

the limit
\lim_{z\to 1}Li_{w}(z)
exists. This allows the following definition, which clearly is another way to define shuffle MZVs.

Definition 2.4.12
.

Let
w\in\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}
be a word starting with
x
and ending with
y
, then the
shuffle multiple zeta value
associated to
w
is the real number

\zeta_{\shuffle}(w):=\lim_{z\to 1}Li_{w}(z).

We write
\zeta_{\shuffle}
the map defined by

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}:\{\emptyset\}\bigcup\left((x)\sqcup\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}\sqcup(y)\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle w\longmapsto\zeta_{\shuffle}(w

\displaystyle).

This definition can easily be generalised to trees, thanks to the following Lemma.

Lemma 2.4.13
.

For any convergent forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
, the limit
\lim_{z\to 1}Li^{T}_{F}(z)
exists.

Proof.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
. If
F=\emptyset
, then
fl_{0}(F)=\emptyset
and the result trivially holds. Otherwise
fl_{0}(F)\in(x)\sqcup\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}\sqcup(y)
. Indeed, we can write
fl_{0}(F)=\sum_{i\in I}w_{i}
for some finite set
I
. Then each
w_{i}
has the decoration of a root of
F
(thus a
x
)
as its first letter; and the decoration of a leaf of
F
(thus a
y
) as its last letter. This is shown ad absurdum: if we have
w_{i}=(y)\sqcup\tilde{w}
, then a vertex decorated by
y
was not above all roots of
F
, since
every roots of
F
is decorated by
x
. This is a contradiction. The same argument shows that no
w_{i}
cannot end with an
x
. The result then follows from Theorem
2.4.10
and the observation above that

\lim_{z\to 1}Li_{w}(z)
exists for any

w\in(x)\sqcup\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}\sqcup(y)
.
∎

This allows the following definition.

Definition 2.4.14
.

For any convergent forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
the corresponding
shuffle arborified zeta value
is defined as

\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F):=\lim_{z\to 1}Li_{F}^{T}(z).

We write
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}
the map defined by

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}:\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle F\longmapsto\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}

\displaystyle(F).

Shuffle arborified zeta values enjoy the following properties.

Theorem 2.4.15
.

For any convergent forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
, the shuffle arborified zeta values
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)
is a finite sum of multiple zeta values with rational coefficients that can be written as a finite
sum of multiple zeta values with integer coefficients given by the
0
-flattening:

\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}=\zeta_{\shuffle}\circ fl_{0}.

Furthermore the map
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}:\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}

is an algebra morphism for the concatenation product of trees as well as for the shuffle product
\shuffle^{T}
of rooted forests.

Proof.

This Theorem is a consequence of Theorem
2.4.10
applied to convergent forests, for which one can take the limit
z\to 1
according to Lemma
2.4.13
.
∎

Remark 2.4.16
.

One could prove this theorem along the steps of the proof of Theorem
2.3.24
. This is true in many occurrences throughout this section. Hence Sections
2.3
and

2.4
present two different (however equivalent) ways of building branched objects.

We conclude this section by pointing out that, as a consequence of our previous results, we also have shown that multiple zetas as iterated integrals are algebra morphism for the shuffle product.

Proposition 2.4.17
.

The map
\zeta_{\shuffle}:\{\emptyset\}\bigcup\left((x)\sqcup\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}\sqcup(y)\right)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is an algebra morphism for the shuffle product.

Proof.

The proof follows from Proposition
2.4.11
on words in
\{\emptyset\}\bigcup\left((x)\sqcup\mathcal{W}_{\{x,y\}}\sqcup(y)\right)
and taking the limit
z\to 1
.
∎

2.5
Attempting to relate AZVs

Now that we have two versions of AZVs :
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T}
and
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}
. The former generalises
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
since it is defined on rooted trees and coincides with MZVs on linear trees which are the image of words under the canonical embedding of words into trees. Similarly,
\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}
generalises
\zeta_{\shuffle}
. Therefore
we would like to relate
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}^{T}
and
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}
by a generalisation of Kontsevich’s relation (
2.5
).

2.5.1
Branched binarisation map

We aim to generalise the map (
2.4
) to trees, that is to build a map

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
which coincide with
\iota_{\{x,y\}}\circ{\mathfrak{s}}
when restricted to ladder trees.
In order to do so, once again we use the universal property of trees given by Theorem
2.2.12
.

Definition 2.5.1
.

Let
\beta:\mathbb{N}^{*}\times\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
defined by

\displaystyle\beta(1,F):=B_{+}^{y}(F)

\displaystyle\beta(n,F):=\left(B_{+}^{x}\right)^{n-1}\left(B_{+}^{y}(F)\right)

for any
n\geqslant 2
. The
branched binarisation map
is the morphism of operated algebras

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
whose existence and uniqueness is given by Theorem
2.2.12
.

Example 2.5.2
.

Here are some example of the action of the binarisation map:

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\qquad{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture})=\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny$x$}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny$y$}\end{picture}\qquad{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny 1}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\right)=\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(0.5,14.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(0.5,6.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny$y$}\put(9.0,5.0){\tiny$y$}\put(-5.0,5.0){\tiny$x$}\put(-5.0,12.0){\tiny$y$}\end{picture}\qquad{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny$2$}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny$1$}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny$2$}\end{picture}\right)=\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-1.5,6.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,12.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,11.5){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(-0.65,11.5){\line{0}{1}{7.0}}\put(-0.65,19.0){\circle*{2.0}}\par\put(5.0,0.0){\tiny$x$}\put(5.0,5.0){\tiny$y$}\put(-6.0,12.0){\tiny$x$}\put(0.0,21.0){\tiny$y$}\put(7.0,11.0){\tiny$y$}\end{picture}.

Now we state a simple lemma relating convergent forests in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
and in
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
.

Lemma 2.5.3
.

The branched binarisation map maps convergent forests to convergent forests:

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}\right)=\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}.

Furthermore,
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
is a bijection.

Proof.

By definition of the operation
\beta
, if
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
is in the image of
\beta
, then
F
is semi-convergent. Therefore
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm semi}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
.
Thus we only need to prove that the image of a convergent forest has its roots decorated by
x
s only.

Let
T\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
be a convergent tree. If
T=\emptyset
then
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\emptyset)=\emptyset\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
by definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
.

If
T\neq\emptyset
then it exists a forest
F
such that
T=B_{+}^{p}(F)
with
p\geqslant 2
. Then by definition of
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
we have

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T)=\left(B_{+}^{x}\right)^{p-1}\left(B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F))\right)

which lies in
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
since
p-1\geqslant 1
.

Let
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
be a convergent forest. Then we have
F=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
with
T_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
by definition
of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
. Then

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)={\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{1})\cdots{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{1})\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}

by definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
. Therefore
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
.

The bijectivity of
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
is also shown by induction, using
|{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)|=||F||
. The same argument on
({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}
allows to show that

({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\left(\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}\right)\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
; concluding the proof.
∎

Recall that a branching vertex is a vertex that has strictly more than one direct successor. This concept will be
of importance when trying to relate shuffle and stuffle arborified zeta values through the branched binarisation map.

We now have a result which is a direct consequence of a result of the next Section (see Remark
2.6.4
). We give here only the proof for the ladder forests. We nonetheless state it here since it gives a negative answer to the question we asked.

Theorem 2.5.4
.

For any convergent forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
we have

\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F))\leqslant\zeta^{T}_{{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}}(F).

Furthermore, the inequality is an equality if, and only if,
F
has no branching vertex (i.e.
F
is the empty tree or
F=l_{1}\cdots l_{k}
with

l_{i}
being ladder trees).

Partial proof.

•

If
F=\emptyset
, then
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)=\emptyset
and the result holds by construction.

•

If
F=l_{1}\cdots l_{k}
with
l_{i}
being ladder trees, then the result follows from the classical property (
2.5
) of the binarisation map together with the
facts that
\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}
and
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}
are algebra morphisms for the concatenation product of trees,
\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(w)=\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(\iota_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}(w))
and

\zeta_{\shuffle}(w)=\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}(\iota_{\{x,y\}}(w))
.

2.5.2
Hoffman’s relations for branched zetas

In order to find the branched equivalent of Hoffman’s regularisation relations, it is useful to recall that the concatenation product of trees can be seen as lift to trees of both the shuffle and stuffle products.
The latter only differ in the way one goes back to words, that is to say by a choice of the
flattening map. Therefore the most naive candidate for this relations is that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F)-{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}){\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)
is convergent
for any convergent forest
F
and lies in the kernel of
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}
.

However, this statement is trivially true and does not impose any new relation on the algebra spanned by branched zetas. Indeed, since
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
is an
algebra morphism we have
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F)-{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}){\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)=0
11
11

11

notice that
0
and
\emptyset
are distinct elements of our algebra
.
This observation is a consequence of the fact that the distinction
between the stuffle and shuffle products can only be made at the level of words, reached through flattening maps. Consequently, a more relevant quantity
to study is

{\mathfrak{s}}\left(fl_{1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)-fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)

for convergent forests
F
. However, the following result states that this does not generalise Hoffman’s relations.

Proposition 2.5.5
.

For any convergent forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
,

{\mathfrak{s}}\left(fl_{1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)-fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)

lies in the algebra of convergent words if, and only if,
F
is empty or
F=l_{1}\cdots l_{k}
with the
l_{i}
ladder trees. In this case

{\mathfrak{s}}\left(fl_{1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}l_{1}\cdots l_{k}\right)\right)-fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}l_{1}\cdots l_{k}\right)\right)\in{\rm Ker}(\zeta_{\shuffle}).

Proof.

•

If
F
is empty, then
{\mathfrak{s}}\left(fl_{1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)-fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)=0
and the
result trivially holds.

•

If
F=l_{1}\cdots l_{k}
with the
l_{i}
ladder trees then, by definition of the flattening maps we have

{\mathfrak{s}}\left(fl_{1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)-fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)={\mathfrak{s}}\left((y){\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}w_{1}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\cdots{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}w_{k}\right)-(y)\shuffle{\mathfrak{s}}(w_{1})\shuffle\cdots\shuffle{\mathfrak{s}}(w_{k})

with
w_{i}:=\iota_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{-1}(l_{i})
. Then the result holds by Hoffman’s regularisation relations (
2.7
).

•

Let
T
be a non ladder tree, and
v
a branching vertex of
T
with decoration
p_{1}
. Let
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
be two direct successors of
V
with decorations
p_{2}
and
p_{3}
respectively. Then it exists two
eventually empty words
w
and
w^{\prime}
such that

fl_{1}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}T)=(1)\sqcup w\sqcup(p_{1}[p_{2}+p_{3}])\sqcup w^{\prime}+X

with
X
a finite linear combination of words written in the alphabet
\mathbb{N}^{*}
.

By definition of
fl_{0}
,
{\mathfrak{s}}\left((1)\sqcup w\sqcup(p_{1}[p_{2}+p_{3}])\sqcup w^{\prime}\right)
will not show up in
fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)
. Thus we obtain

{\mathfrak{s}}\left(fl_{1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)-fl_{0}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}F\right)\right)={\mathfrak{s}}\left((1)\sqcup w\sqcup(p_{1}[p_{2}+p_{3}])\sqcup w^{\prime}\right)+Y

with
Y
a finite linear combination of words written in the alphabet
\{x,y\}
without the divergent tree

{\mathfrak{s}}\left((1)\sqcup w\sqcup(p_{1}[p_{2}+p_{3}])\sqcup w^{\prime}\right)
. This implies the result for any non-ladder tree.

For a forest with at least one branching point, the result follows from the fact that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
and the flattening maps are algebras morphisms and the previous discussion on non ladder trees.

∎

This result was derived from the picture that the shuffle and stuffle products of words are lifted to trees to the concatenation of trees. We
have also derived a generalisation to rooted forests of the shuffle and stuffle products of words. This leads us to an alternative possible
generalisation of Hoffman’s relations (
2.7
); namely that

\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ T-({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T)\right)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ T\right)-\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T)

lie in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}

for any convergent tree
T\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
. About this quantity one finds

Proposition 2.5.6
.

For any convergent forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv}
, one has

\displaystyle\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ F-({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)\right)\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}^{\rm conv};

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ F\right)-\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}.

Proof.

First, notice that by Lemma
2.5.3
the two statements are equivalent. We therefore only prove the second one.

For a convergent forest
F=T_{1}\cdots T_{k}
, we have
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)={\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{1})\cdots{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{k})
. Then we have, with obvious
notations:

\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ F=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(B_{+}^{1}(T_{i})+X_{i,F}\right)F\setminus T_{i}

for some finite sum of trees
X_{i,F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}{\rm conv}
. Therefore we have

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ F\right)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{i}))+{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(X_{i,F})\right){\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F\setminus T_{i}).

On the other hand we have

\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{i}))+Y_{i,F}\right){\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F\setminus T_{i})

for some
Y_{i,F}\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{\rm conv}
. Taking the difference of these two quantities, one obtains the result, since by
Lemma
2.5.3
the branched binarisation map
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
maps convergent forests to convergent forests.
∎

While this result might give us hope, one should expect Theorem
2.5.4
to prevent the quantities

{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ F\right)-\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)
from lying in the kernel of
\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}
. And indeed, one
finds, after a long yet straightforward computation, that

\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}\left({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny 1}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}\right)-\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny$y$}\end{picture}\shuffle\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,5.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,12.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(0.0,12.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(6.0,-3.0){\tiny$x$}\put(6.0,4.0){\tiny$y$}\put(9.0,12.0){\tiny$y$}\put(-5.0,12.0){\tiny$y$}\end{picture}\right)=\zeta(2,3)+\zeta(3,2)>0.

(2.16)

The precise characterisation of quantities of the form
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\left(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}\penalty\ F\right)-\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny y}\end{picture}\shuffle\penalty\ {\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)
as well as their image under
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}
could be of interest for future investigations.

To sum up our work so far, let us compare the properties of MZVs and AZVs in Table
2.1
below.

Table 2.1:
Properties of MZVs and AZVs.

The conclusion of this Section could be that branching vertices, at least with
the current generalisation of MZVs, induce an important change when lifting their properties to rooted forests.

Another possible point of view is that some of the properties of MZVs are lost when one generalises them to rooted forests and that our initial goal is out of reach. In front of this observation, we have a choice: to despair or not to despair. As a young naive person I chose the latter. Instead, we will now see that shuffle AVZs
can
be written as multiple sums generalising the MZVs. This motivates the introduction of a new generalisation of MZVs to rooted forests.

2.6
Series representation of AZVs

In order to prove the existence of a series representation for AZVs, we need to introduce a few more notions. Before, let us recall from Definition

2.2.1
that a
path
in a rooted forest
F
is a finite sequence of elements of
V(F)
:
p=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n})
that is either a singleton (in which case
p=(v)
is said to be a path between
v
and itself) or such that for all
i\in[[1,n-1]]
,
(v_{i},v_{i+1})
is an edge of
F
. The same notion generalises to decorated rooted forests.

Definition 2.6.1
.

A
segment
of a rooted forest
(F,d_{F})\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
is a non-empty path
s_{v}=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}=v)
such that
d_{F}(v_{n})=y
,
d_{F}(v_{i})=x
for any
i
in
\{1,\cdots,n-1\}
and
d(a(v_{1}))=y
, with
a(v_{1})
the direct ancestor of
v_{1}
. We call the number
n
the
length
of the segment
s_{v}
and write it
|s_{v}|
.

We write
S(F):=\{s_{v}|v\in V_{y}(F)\}
the set of segments of a rooted forest
F
.

In words: a segment
s_{v}
of a rooted forest is a path in this rooted forest, which ends at the vertex
v
decorated by
y
and starts just above the first ancestor of
v
being also decorated by
y
.

The set
S(F)
inherits a poset structure from the poset structure of
V(F)
. We also denote this partial order relation by
\preceq
:
s_{v}\preceq s_{v^{\prime}}:\Longleftrightarrow v\preceq v^{\prime}
. This allows us to define the depth of a segment.

Definition 2.6.2
.

The
depths
of the segments of a decorated rooted forest
F
decorated by
\{x,y\}
are recursively defined by:

•

depth
(s_{v})=0
if
v
is a leaf of
F
,

•

depth
(s_{v})=\max\{{\rm depth}(s_{v^{\prime}})|v^{\prime}\neq v\wedge s_{v}\preceq s_{v^{\prime}}\}+1
.

We also set
N_{F}:=\max\{{\rm depth}(s_{v})|s_{v}\in S(F)\}
the maximal depth of a segment of
F
. For any
n\in\{0,\cdots,N_{F}\}
we set

S_{n}(F):=\{s_{v}\in S(F)|{\rm depth}(s_{v})=n\},

we further set:

S^{n}(F):=\bigcup_{i=0}^{n}S_{i}(F)

for any
n
in
\{0,\cdots,N_{F}\}
. For any such
n
we also write

||S_{n}(F)||:=\sum_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}|s_{v}|\quad\text{and}\quad||S^{n}(F)||:=\sum_{s_{v}\in S^{n}(F)}|s_{v}|.

Notice that depth of segments of a rooted forest are well-defined because we have taken our forests to be finite.

We are now able to prove the following

Theorem 2.6.3
.

For any convergent forest
F
the corresponding arborified zeta values admits the following series representation:

\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}\geqslant 1\\
v\in V_{y}(F)\end{subarray}}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succeq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{-|s_{v}|}

where
s_{v}=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{n}=v)
is a the segment of
F
ending at
v
and
v\in V_{y}(F)
in the first sum means that this series has a summation variable for each
v\in V_{y}(F)
.

Proof.

Let
F
be a convergent forest. Since the map
F\mapsto\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)
is an algebra morphism for the concatenation product of trees, it is enough to show that the theorem holds for
F
a rooted tree. Thus we can assume without loss of generality that
F
is a rooted tree.

Let
N_{F}
be the maximal depth of the segments of this tree. If
N_{F}=0
, the theorem reduces to the usual series representation of a Riemann zeta.

If
N_{F}\geqslant 1
, since we are working with convergent integrals we can use Fubini’s theorem to regroup integrations of the segment. This mean that we can write the arborified zeta associated to
F
as

\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)=\int_{\Delta_{F}}\prod_{n=0}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}

(2.17)

where, for
s_{v}=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{p})\in S_{n}(F)
we have set

d\omega_{s_{v}}:=\frac{dz_{w_{1}}}{z_{w_{1}}}\cdots\frac{dz_{w_{p-1}}}{z_{w_{p-1}}}\frac{dz_{w_{p}}}{1-z_{w_{p}}}

(with an obvious abuse of notation if
p=1
). Now recall that for any
A\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
p\geqslant 1
and any
Z\in[0,1]
(
Z\neq 1
if
p=1
) we have

\int_{0\leqslant z_{p}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant z_{1}\leqslant Z}\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\cdots\frac{dz_{p-1}}{z_{p-1}}\frac{dz_{p}}{1-z_{p}}(z_{p})^{A}=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\frac{Z^{n+A}}{(n+A)^{p}}

(2.18)

(again with obvious abuses of notations when
p=1
). This classical result follows from the theorem of dominated convergence and the Taylor expansion of the function
x\mapsto(1-x)^{-1}
.

We can now use (
2.18
) with
A=0
in (
2.17
) to integrate all the variable attached to vertices belonging to a segment of depth zero. We obtain

\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}=1,\\
v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{0}(F)\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{0}(F)}(n_{v})^{-|s_{v}|}\int_{\Delta_{F}\setminus S_{0}(F)}\prod_{n=1}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{1}(F)}(z_{v})^{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succ v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}}

(2.19)

where
v^{\prime}\succ v
means that
v^{\prime}
is a descendant of
v
that is distinct from
v

and where we have set

\displaystyle[0,1]^{|V(F)|-||S_{0}(F)||}\ni(z_{v_{1}},\cdots,z_{v_{p}})\in\Delta_{F}\setminus S_{0}(F)

\displaystyle:\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left(\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{p}\}=V(F)\setminus\{v^{\prime}\in s_{v}|s_{v}\in S_{0}(F)\}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ (v_{i}\preceq v_{j}\Leftrightarrow z_{v_{j}}\leqslant z_{v_{i}})\right).

Now for any
k\in\{0,\cdots,N_{F}-1\}
we set

\displaystyle[0,1]^{|V(F)|-||S^{k}(F)||}\ni(z_{v_{1}},\cdots,z_{v_{p}})\in\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{k}(F)

\displaystyle:\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left(\{v_{1},\cdots,v_{p}\}=V(F)\setminus\{v^{\prime}\in s_{v}|s_{v}\in S^{k}(F)\}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ (v_{i}\preceq v_{j}\Leftrightarrow z_{v_{j}}\leqslant z_{v_{i}})\right)

(notice that we replaced
S_{0}(F)
by
S^{k}(F)
). Let us prove by induction over
k
that for any
k\in\{0,\cdots,N_{F}-1\}
we have

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}=1,\\
v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S^{k}(F)\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S^{k}(F)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in S^{k}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succeq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{-|s_{v}|}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\times\underbrace{\int_{\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{k}(F)}\prod_{n=k+1}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)}(z_{v})^{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succ v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}}}_{=:I_{F,k}}.

First, since
S_{0}(F)=S^{0}(F)
and
\Delta_{F}\setminus S_{0}(F)=\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{0}(F)
, Equation (
2.19
) is exactly Equation (
2.20
) for
k=0
. Then if
N_{F}=1
, we have proven Equation (
2.20
) in every case of interest. If
N_{F}\geqslant 2
then let us assume that Equation (
2.20
) holds for
k\in\{0,\cdots,N_{F}-2\}
. We then have, once again from the Taylor expansion of the function
x\mapsto(1-x)^{-1}
and the dominated convergence theorem

\displaystyle I_{F,k}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{k}(F)}\left(\prod_{n=k+2}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\right)\left(\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\right)\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)}(z_{v})^{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succ v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}=0,\\
v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}\int_{\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{k}(F)}\left(\prod_{n=k+2}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\right)

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V_{y}(F)|\\
s_{v}=(\vec{\alpha},v)\in S_{k+1}(F)\end{subarray}}\frac{d\vec{z}_{\vec{\alpha}}}{\vec{z}_{\vec{\alpha}}}dz_{v}(z_{v})^{n_{v}}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)}(z_{v})^{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succ v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}}

with the obvious notation that
\frac{d\vec{z}_{\vec{\alpha}}}{\vec{z}_{\vec{\alpha}}}
is a product of
dz/z
. We can now merge the two last products to obtain

I_{F,k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}=0,\\
v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}\int_{\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{k}(F)}\left(\prod_{n=k+2}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in V_{y}(F)|\\
s_{v}=(\vec{\alpha},v)\in S_{k+1}(F)\end{subarray}}\frac{d\vec{z}_{\vec{\alpha}}}{\vec{z}_{\vec{\alpha}}}dz_{v}(z_{v})^{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succeq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}}.

Finally integrating the variable attached to vertices belonging to segments of depth
k
and switching each of the summation variable by one we obtain

I_{F,k}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}=1,\\
v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}\prod_{v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S_{k+1}(F)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succeq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{-|s_{v}|}\underbrace{\int_{\Delta_{F}\setminus S^{k+1}(F)}\left(\prod_{n=k+2}^{N_{F}}\prod_{s_{v}\in S_{n}(F)}d\omega_{s_{v}}\right)}_{I_{F,k+1}}.

Notice that this whole computation consisted essentially of using Formula (
2.18
) for each of the segments of depth exactly
k
. In any case, plugging this expression for
I_{F,k}
back into (
2.20
) we obtain exactly the same equation with
k
replaced by
k+1
. So, by a finite induction we have proven Equation (
2.20
) for any
k\in\{0,\cdots,N_{F}\}
for any value of
N_{F}\geqslant 1
.

Since we have assumed
F
to be a connected rooted forest (i.e. a rooted tree),
F
has exactly one segment
s_{v}
of maximal depth
N_{F}
. Furthermore, since
F
is convergent, we have
l:=|s_{v}|\geqslant 2
. Thus, after a relabelling of
s_{v}
:

s_{v}=(1,\cdots,l=v),

Equation (
2.20
) with
k=N_{F}-1
reads

\displaystyle\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(F)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}=1,\\
v\in V_{y}(F)|s_{v}\in S^{N_{F}-1}(F)\end{subarray}}^{+\infty}\prod_{v\in s_{v}|s_{v}\in S^{N_{F}-1}(F)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in S^{N_{F}-1}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succeq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{-|s_{v}|}

\displaystyle\times\int_{0\leqslant z_{1}<\cdots<z_{l}\leqslant 1}\frac{dz_{1}}{z_{1}}\cdots\frac{dz_{l-1}}{z_{l-1}}\frac{dz_{l}}{1-z_{l}}\left(z_{l}\right)^{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\neq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}}

Using once again Formula (
2.18
) with
Z=1
,
p=l\geqslant 2
and
A=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V_{y}(F)\\
v^{\prime}\neq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}
we obtain the statement of the theorem once we write all the sums together.
∎

Remark 2.6.4
.

This series representation of AZVs is
not
the stuffle AZVs defined earlier.
Instead, this series representation of AZVs defined by iterated integrals should be seen as a new generalisation of MZVs defined as multiple series.

Notice however that this result implies Theorem
2.5.4
. Indeed the series expression of
F={\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f)
given by Theorem
2.6.3
contains less terms that
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(f)
, and strictly less if
f
has at least one branching vertices. To see this, let us take
f=\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny a}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny c}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny b}\end{picture}
. Then

\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(f)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}>0\\
n_{1}>n_{2},n_{3}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n_{1}^{a}n_{2}^{b}n_{3}^{b}},\qquad\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}({\mathfrak{s}}(f))=\sum_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}>0}\frac{1}{(m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3})^{a}m_{2}^{b}m_{3}^{b}}.

If we change the varibles in
\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}({\mathfrak{s}}(f))
to
n_{3}=m_{3}
,
n_{2}=m_{2}
and
n_{1}=m_{1}+m_{2}+m_{3}
, the summand is the same that the one of
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(f)
, but the condition is
n_{1}>n_{2}+n_{3}
. For example, terms like
(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})=(3,2,1)
appears in
\zeta^{T}_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(f)
but not in
\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}({\mathfrak{s}}(f))
. This is true for any rooted forest with at least one branching vertex: we have precisely Theorem
2.5.4
.

2.7
Tree zeta values

Theorem
2.6.3
motivates a new generalisation of MZVs to trees. This section is devoted to studying this generalisation.

2.7.1
Definition and first properties

Let us first state the definition of these new iterated sums without taking care of their convergence.

Definition 2.7.1
.

For an
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated rooted forest
F
, whenever it exists, let

\zeta^{t}(F):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n_{v}\geqslant 1\\
v\in V(F)\end{subarray}}\prod_{v\in V(F)}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in V(F)\\
v^{\prime}\succeq v\end{subarray}}n_{v^{\prime}}\right)^{-\alpha_{v}}

(2.21)

(with
\alpha_{v}=d_{F}(v)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
the decoration of the vertex
v
and, as before,
v\in V(F)
in the first sum means that this series has a summation variable for each
v\in V(F)
) be the
tree zeta value
(TZV) associated to
F
.
\zeta^{t}
is then extended by linearity to a linear map defined on a subset of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
.

Remark 2.7.2
.

In a private communication, F. Zerbini suggested that tree zeta values could be of interest, and in particular that they were likely to be MZVs. I am thankful for this input.

Here are some simple examples of TZVs, one of which was discussed in Remark
2.6.4
:

\zeta^{t}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny n}\end{picture})=\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(n),\qquad\zeta^{t}(\begin{picture}(-5.0,-1.0)\put(3.5,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(-0.65,0.0){$\vee$}\put(6.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(1.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(5.0,-2.0){\tiny$2$}\put(7.5,5.0){\tiny$1$}\put(-5.0,8.0){\tiny$2$}\end{picture})=\sum_{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{(n_{1}+n_{2}+n_{3})^{2}n_{2}(n_{3})^{2}}.

Remark 2.7.3
.

As already pointed out, a simple change of summing variables in the interated series of Equation (
2.1
) allows to rewrite stuffle MZVs as

\zeta_{\sqcup\mathchoice{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-12.5mu}{\mkern-8.7mu}{\mkern-8.5mu}-\mathchoice{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-14.5mu}{\mkern-10.7mu}{\mkern-10.5mu}\sqcup}(n_{1}\cdots n_{k})=\sum_{m_{1},\cdots,m_{k}\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{(m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k})^{n_{1}}(m_{2}+\cdots+m_{k})^{n_{2}}\cdots(m_{k})^{n_{k}}}.

This is the expression of a tree zeta value associated to a ladder tree decorated by
n_{1},\cdots,n_{k}
. Therefore, TZVs are indeed a new generalisation of MZVs to rooted forests.

Recall from Definition
2.5.1
that the branched binarisation map
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}
is the the unique morphism of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-operated algebras given by the universal property of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
. Roughly speaking, it can be understood as mapping a vertex decorated by
n
to a segment of length
n
(in the sense of Definition
2.6.1
).
We then have a simple but important result which is the justification of the definition of TZVs:

Proposition 2.7.4
.

For any convergent
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated rooted forest
F
, the tree zeta value
\zeta^{t}(F)
associated to
F
is convergent and is equal to the arborified zeta values associated to the convergent
\{x,y\}
-decorated rooted forest
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)
:

\zeta^{t}(F)=\zeta^{T}_{\shuffle}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F)).

Proof.

The result follows from the simple observation that, for any convergent
\{x,y\}
-decorated rooted forest
f
the series representation of
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(f)
given by Theorem
2.6.3
is precisely the tree zeta value
\zeta^{t}(({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}(f))
. The convergence of
\zeta^{t}(F)
for any convergent
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated rooted forest
F
then follows from the facts that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
is a one to one map between the two sets of convergent rooted forests and that the arborified zeta value
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}(f)
converges for any convergent
\{x,y\}
-decorated rooted forest
f
.
∎

From this Proposition, one easily derives important properties of tree zeta values from the properties of branched zeta values.

Theorem 2.7.5
.

•

The map
\zeta^{t}:F\mapsto\zeta^{t}(F)
is an algebra morphism from convergent
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated rooted forests to
\mathbb{R}
for the concatenation product of forests.

•

For any convergent
\mathbb{N}^{*}
-decorated rooted forests
F
, the tree zeta value
\zeta^{t}(F)
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination of MZVs, given by

\zeta^{t}(F)=(\zeta_{\shuffle}\circ fl_{0}\circ{\mathfrak{s}}^{T})(F).

Proof.

Both points follow directly from Proposition
2.7.4

•

The first point follows from the fact that
\zeta^{t}=\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}\circ{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
together with the fact that both
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}
and
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
are algebra morphisms for the concatenation product of rooted forests.

•

The second point follows from the same relation
\zeta^{t}=\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}\circ{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
together with Theorem
2.4.15
which states that
\zeta_{\shuffle}^{T}=\zeta_{\shuffle}\circ fl_{0}
.

∎

Remark 2.7.6
.

Finite sums similar to TZVs were studied in
[
Ono16
]
in the context of finite MZVs. In particular,
[
Ono16
, Theorem 1.4]
is the equivalent to Theorem
2.7.5
in the context of finite sums.

Notice that
\zeta^{t}
is
not
an algebra morphism for the shuffle product of trees, since
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
is not. It can also be checked on computations that it neither is an algebra morphism for any of the stuffle products of rooted forests. The rest of this Section is dedicated to the study of the algebraic properties of TZVs.

2.7.2
The Upsilon product

The
\Upsilon
product is an alternative product which will have interesting applications to TZVs as well as their applications to CZVs.

Definition 2.7.7
.

The
\Upsilon
product (read ‘‘Upsilon’’)
12
12

12

which was the rune “yew” in the paper
[
Cla20
]
, but the rune package seems to bring more issues than it is worth for long documents, and I have chosen to modify it here.
is a product
\Upsilon:\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{N^{*}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
defined by

\Upsilon=({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\circ\shuffle^{T}\circ({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\otimes{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}).

Remark 2.7.8
.

In some applications, we will use the
\Upsilon
product on words: for two words
w
and
w^{\prime}
we will write
w\Upsilon w^{\prime}
instead of
\iota^{-1}(\iota(w)\Upsilon\iota(w^{\prime}))
(with
\iota
the canonical injection of words into rooted forests). We allow ourselves to make this small abuse of notation as it should not give rise to any confusion and will allow to greatly lighten the notations.

We start by a simple but important property of the
\Upsilon
product.

Proposition 2.7.9
.

The
\Upsilon
product is commutative and unital, but not associative.

Proof.

The commutativity directly follows from the commutativity of
\shuffle^{T}
. The non associativity as well and can be check on examples: take
F_{1}=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}
,
F_{2}=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}
and
F_{3}=\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}
. After some computations we obtain:

\displaystyle(F_{1}\Upsilon F_{2})\Upsilon F_{3}

\displaystyle-F_{1}\Upsilon(F_{2}\Upsilon F_{3})=2\times\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}+8\times\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+8\times\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}

\displaystyle-\left[3\times\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}+6\times\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}+6\times\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+12\times\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+18\times\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 4}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}\right].

The fact that
\Upsilon
is unital follows directly from the facts that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
and that
\emptyset
is the neutral element for
\shuffle^{T}
.
∎

We will now work out an explicit formula for the
\Upsilon
product. We start with two technical Lemmas about the shuffle product of rooted forests decorated by
\{x,y\}
.

Lemma 2.7.10
.

For any rooted forests
(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{2}
and any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have:

\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}B_{+}^{y}(g)=(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}\big[f\shuffle^{T}B_{+}^{y}(g)\big]+\sum_{j=0}^{n}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ j}\circ B_{+}^{y}\Big[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-j)}B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\Big].

Proof.

We prove this result by induction on
n
. For
n=0
, the lemma holds by definition of the shuffle product
\shuffle^{T}
. Let us assume it holds for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We then have

\displaystyle\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+1)}\circ B_{+}^{x}y(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}B_{+}^{y}(g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{+}^{x}\circ\Big[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}B_{+}^{y}(g)\Big]+B_{+}^{y}\Big[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{+}^{x}\bigg((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}\big[f\shuffle^{T}B_{+}^{y}(g)\big]+\sum_{j=0}^{a}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ j}\circ B_{+}^{y}\Big[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\qquad+B_{+}^{y}\Big[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\Big]\text{ by the induction hypothesis}

\displaystyle=(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\big[f\shuffle^{T}B_{+}^{y}(g)\big]+\sum_{j=0}^{n+1}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ j}\circ B_{+}^{y}\Big[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+1-j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\Big]

which conclude the induction and the proof.
∎

We generalise this result:

Lemma 2.7.11
.

For any rooted forests
(f,g)\in\mathcal{F}_{\{x,y\}}^{2}
and any
(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
we have:

\displaystyle\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ m}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n+i}{i}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[f\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{m+j}{j}(B_{+}^{x})^{m+j}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\right].

Proof.

We prove this result by induction on
n+m
. If
n+m=0
, then it holds by definition of the shuffle product of rooted trees. Assume that the result hold for some
N=n+m
. Then for any
(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that
n+m=N+1
, if
n=0
or
m=0
the result reduces to the previous Lemma
2.7.10
(eventually using the commutativity of
\shuffle^{T}
). For
n\neq 0
and
m\neq 0
we have

\displaystyle\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ m}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)

\displaystyle=B_{+}^{x}\circ\Big(\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ m}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle B_{+}^{x}\circ\Big(\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\Big).

Using the induction hypothesis we obtain:

\displaystyle B_{+}^{x}\circ\Big(\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ m}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B_{+}^{x}\Bigg(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n-1+i}{i}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1+i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[f\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{m+j}{j}(B_{+}^{x})^{m+i}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1-j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\right]\Bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\binom{n-1+i}{i}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[f\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\binom{m-1+j}{j-1}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m+j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{n-j}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\right].

Similarly we obtain:

\displaystyle B_{+}^{x}\circ\Big(\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\binom{n-1+i}{i-1}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n+i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[f\shuffle^{T}\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-i)}B_{+}^{y}(g)\big)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt+\sum_{j=0}^{n}\binom{m-1+j}{j}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m+j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[\big((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)\big)\shuffle^{T}g\right].

Summing these two expressions and using Pascal’s triangle (and the fact that
\binom{p-1}{0}=\binom{p}{0}
) gives the result, which concludes the induction and the proof.
∎

Remark 2.7.12
.

This result can be understood in a purely combinatorial way. Each tree in this shuffle has to have at least min
\{n,m\}

x
’s decorating its root and its descendants. These decorations can come either from the term
(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ n}\circ B_{+}^{y}(f)
or from the term
(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ m}\circ B_{+}^{y}(g)
. The binomial coefficients come from all the possibilities the
x
’s have to be chosen.

We can now prove the main result of this Subsection, which gives an inductive formula to directly compute the
\Upsilon
product of two rooted forests, without references to the shuffle
\shuffle^{T}
nor the binarisation map
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}
.

Theorem 2.7.13
.

The
\Upsilon
product admits the following inductive description:

•

For any forest
F\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathbb{N}^{*}}
,
F\Upsilon\emptyset=\emptyset\Upsilon F=F
.

•

For any rooted trees
T_{1}=B_{+}^{n}(f_{1})
and
T_{2}=B_{+}^{m}(f_{2})
:

T_{1}\Upsilon T_{2}=\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{n-1+i}{i}B_{+}^{n+i}\left[f_{1}\Upsilon B_{+}^{m-i}(f_{2})\right]+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{m-1+j}{j}B_{+}^{m+j}\left[B_{+}^{n-j}(f_{1})\Upsilon f_{2}\right].

•

For any rooted forests
F_{1}=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
and
F_{2}=t_{1}\cdots t_{k}
:

F_{1}\Upsilon F_{2}=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((T_{i}\Upsilon t_{j})T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{k}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{n}\right).

Proof.

•

The first point follows directly from the facts that
{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
and that
\emptyset
is the unique neutral element for the shuffle product
\shuffle^{T}
.

•

For the second point, let
T_{1}=B_{+}^{n}(f_{1})
and
T_{2}=B_{+}^{m}(f_{2})
be as in the Theorem. Then

\displaystyle T_{1}\Upsilon T_{2}=({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{1})\shuffle^{T}{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{2}))\qquad\text{by definition of }\Upsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\left((B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{1}))\shuffle^{T}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1)}\circ B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{2}))\right)\qquad\text{by definition of }{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\Bigg(\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{n-1+i}{i}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1+i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{1})\shuffle^{T}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-1-i)}\circ B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{2}))\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{m-1+j}{j}(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(m-1+j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}\left[(B_{+}^{x})^{\circ(n-1-j)}\circ B_{+}^{y}({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{1}))\shuffle^{T}{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{2})\right]\Bigg)\penalty\ \text{by Lemma \ref{lem:yew_inter}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{n-1+i}{i}B_{+}^{n+i}\left(({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\left[{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{1})\shuffle^{T}{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(B_{+}^{m-i}f_{2})\right]\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{m-1+j}{j}B_{+}^{m+j}\left(({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\left[{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(B_{+}^{n-j}f_{1})\shuffle^{T}{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(f_{2})\right]\right)\qquad\text{by definition of }({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{n-1+i}{i}B_{+}^{n+i}\left[f_{1}\Upsilon B_{+}^{m-i}(f_{2})\right]+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{m-1+j}{j}B_{+}^{m+j}\left[B_{n-j}(f_{1})\Upsilon f_{2}\right]

by definition of
\Upsilon
.

•

Finally, let
F_{1}=T_{1}\cdots T_{n}
and
F_{2}=t_{1}\cdots t_{k}
be as in the Theorem. Then

\displaystyle F_{1}\Upsilon F_{2}=({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}(s^{T}(F_{1})\shuffle^{T}{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(F_{2}))\qquad\text{by definition of }\Upsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\mathfrak{s}}^{T})^{-1}\Bigg(\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(({\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{i})\shuffle^{T}{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(t_{j})){\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{1})\cdots\widehat{{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{i})}\cdots{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(T_{k}){\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(t_{1})\cdots\widehat{{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(t_{j})}\cdots{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}(t_{n})\Big)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\llcorner\text{since }{\mathfrak{s}}^{T}\text{ is an algebra morphism and by definition of }\shuffle^{T}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{kn}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left((T_{i}\Upsilon t_{j})T_{1}\cdots\widehat{T_{i}}\cdots T_{k}t_{1}\cdots\widehat{t_{j}}\cdots t_{n}\right).

∎

This result allows reasonably fast computations of
\Upsilon
products of rooted forests, especially in cases with few branchings. Let us illustrate this with some examples.

Example 2.7.14
.

We start with an explicit computation:

\displaystyle\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\Upsilon\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}

\displaystyle=B_{+}^{2}(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})+2.B_{+}^{3}(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})+3.B_{+}^{4}(\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})+B_{+}^{3}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}\Upsilon\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})+3.B_{+}^{4}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}\Upsilon\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})

\displaystyle=\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+2.\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+3.\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 4}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+B_{+}^{3}\left[B_{+}^{2}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})+B_{+}^{1}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture})+B_{+}^{2}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})\right]+3.B_{+}^{4}\left[B_{+}^{1}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})+B_{+}^{1}(\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture})\right]

\displaystyle=\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+4.\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 2}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+9.\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 4}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 1}\end{picture}+\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny 3}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny 1}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny 2}\end{picture}

The same computation can be carried out with arbitrary coefficients. We obtain

\displaystyle\begin{picture}(-2.0,-1.0)\put(0.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(2.0,-2.0){\tiny m}\end{picture}\Upsilon\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny n}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny k}\end{picture}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\binom{n-1+i}{i}\left[\sum_{i^{\prime}=0}^{k-1}\binom{m-i-1+i^{\prime}}{i^{\prime}}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny n+i}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny m-i+i'}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny k-i'}\end{picture}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{m-i-1}\binom{k-1+j^{\prime}}{j^{\prime}}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny n+i}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny k+j'}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny m-i-j'}\end{picture}\right]+\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{m-1+j}{j}\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,10.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny m+j}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny n-j}\put(4.0,9.0){\tiny k}\end{picture}.

We can carry further the computation. For example, one finds

\displaystyle\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny n}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny k}\end{picture}\Upsilon\begin{picture}(0.0,-1.0)\put(3.0,0.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{5.0}}\put(3.0,5.0){\circle*{2.0}}\put(4.0,-2.0){\tiny m}\put(4.0,4.0){\tiny l}\end{picture}
