\top
which is compatible with the linear structure on
V
in the sense that, for any subset
X
of
V
,
X^{\top}
is a linear subspace of
V
.

Remark 3.1.8
.

For a locality vector space
(V,\top)
, since
V^{\top}
is a linear subspace of
V
, we have
\{0\}\times V\subset\top,
or equivalently
0\in V^{\top}.
Note that there are no locality restrictions for the vector space structure (addition and scalar multiplication) on
V
, that is, the addition and scalar multiplication are everywhere defined.

An important example is when the locality structure on a vector space comes from a locality on a basis. It is a special case of locality basis.

Example 3.1.9
.

From a locality set
(X,\top)
let
(KX,\top)
be the locality vector space whose defining relation (denoted by the same symbol
\top
) is the linear extension of that on
X
. More precisely for
u,v\in KX
,
(u,v)\in\top
if the basis elements from
X
appearing in
u
are related via
\top
to the basis elements appearing in
v
.
Thus

KX\times_{\top_{KX}}KX=\bigcup_{U,V\subseteq X,(U,V)\subseteq\top}KU\times KV.

In order to have the category of locality vector spaces, let us define their morphisms.

Definition 3.1.10
.

Let
\left(U,\top_{U}\right)
and
(V,\top_{V})
be locality vector spaces, a linear map
\phi:\left(U,\top_{U}\right)\to\left(V,\top_{V}\right)
is called a
locality linear map
if it is a locality map.

We write
\mathbf{LocVect}
the category of locality vector spaces with locality linear maps.

There are so far no clear way to turn
\mathbf{LocVect}
into a monoidal category with a monoidal structure relevant for locality (see Conjecture
3.6.1
at the end of this Chapter and the discussion after). However, the set of locality linear maps from a locality vector space to another can itself be endowed with the structure of a locality vector space. Below
we only prove the case that is of importance for us.

Proposition 3.1.11
.

Let
(U,\top_{U}),(V,\top_{V})
be locality vector spaces and
\phi,\psi:(U,\top_{U})\longrightarrow(V,\top_{V})
be independent locality linear maps. Any two linear combinations of
\phi
and
\psi
are also independent. In particular, any linear combination
\lambda\phi+\mu\psi
with
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{K}
is a locality linear map.

Proof.

Let
u_{1},u_{2}
be in
\top_{U}
. Since
\phi
and
\psi
are independent locality linear maps, we have
\phi(u_{1})\top\phi(u_{2})
and
\phi(u_{1})\top\psi(u_{2})
. Since
\phi(u_{1})^{\top}
is a vector subspace of
V
this implies

\phi(u_{1})\top\left(\lambda\phi(u_{2})+\mu\psi(u_{2})\right).

Similarly, we also have
\psi(u_{1})\top\left(\lambda\phi(u_{2})+\mu\psi(u_{2})\right)
and hence, using once more the axiom of locality vector spaces we obtain

\left(\lambda\phi(u_{1})+\mu\psi(u_{1})\right)\top_{V}\left(\lambda\phi(u_{2})+\mu\psi(u_{2})\right).

∎

Locality algebras are defined through locality semigroups, so let us introduce this notion now. First, for a locality set
(X,T)
and an integer
k\geqslant 2
, denote

X^{{}_{\top}k}:=\underbrace{X\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}X}_{k\text{ factors}}:=\left\{(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k})\in X^{k}\,\left|\,(x_{i},x_{j})\in\top,1\leqslant i\neq j\leqslant k\right.\right\}.

(3.1)

Definition 3.1.12
.

1.

An
locality semigroup
1
1

1

As a special case of partial algebras
[
Grä08
]
, the terminology “partial semigroup” is used for a set equipped with a partial associative product defined only for certain pairs of elements in the set. The condition for a locality semigroup is more restrictive than that of a partial semigroup in that the former requires that the pairs for which the partial product is defined stem from a symmetric relation and that
the partial product should be compatible with the locality relation in the sense of Equation (
3.2
).

is a locality set
(G,\top)
together with a partial product law defined on
\top
:

m_{G}:G\times_{\top}G\longrightarrow G

for which the product is compatible with the locality relation on
G
, namely

\forall U\subseteq G,\quad m_{G}((U^{\top}\times U^{\top})\cap\top)\subset U^{\top}

(3.2)

and such that the following
locality associativity property
holds:

(x\cdot y)\cdot z=x\cdot(y\cdot z)\text{ for all }(x,y,z)\in G\times_{\top}G\times_{\top}G.

(3.3)

Note that, because of the condition (
3.2
), both sides of Eq. (
3.3
) are well-defined for any triple in the given subset.

2.

A locality semigroup is
commutative
if
m_{G}(x,y)=m_{G}(y,x)
for
(x,y)\in\top
, noting that both sides of the equations are defined since
\top
is symmetric.

3.

A
locality monoid
is a locality semigroup
(G,\top,m_{G})
together with a
unit element

1_{G}\in G
given by the defining property

\{1_{G}\}^{\top}=G\quad\text{ and }\quad m_{G}(x,1_{G})=m_{G}(1_{G},x)=x\quad\forall x\in G.

We denote the locality monoid by
(G,\top,m_{G},1_{G})
.

4.

A
locality group
is a locality monoid
(G,\top,m_{G},1_{G})
equipped with a morphism
\iota:G\to G
of locality sets, called the
inverse map
, such that
(\iota(g),g)\in\top
and
m_{G}(\iota(g),g)=m_{G}(g,\iota(g))=1_{G}
for any
g\in G
.

5.

A
sub-locality semigroup
of a locality semigroup
(G,\top,m_{G})
is a locality semigroup
(G^{\prime},\top^{\prime},m_{G^{\prime}})
with
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
,
\top^{\prime}=(G^{\prime}\times G^{\prime})\cap\top
and
m_{G^{\prime}}=m_{G}|_{\top^{\prime}}
, that is, for
x,y\in G^{\prime}
and
(x,y)\in\top
,
m_{G}(x,y)
is in
G^{\prime}
.
A
sub-locality monoid
of a locality monoid is a sub-locality semigroup of the corresponding locality semigroup which share the same unit. A
sub-locality group
of a locality group is a sub-locality monoid of the corresponding locality monoid which
is also a locality group.

For notational convenience, we usually abbreviate
m_{G}(x,y)
by
x\cdot y
or simply
xy
.

Remark 3.1.13
.

One easily checks that on a locality monoid
(G,\top,m_{G},1_{G})
if
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
is in
G^{{}_{\top}4}
, then
(x_{1}x_{2},y_{1},y_{2})
and
(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1}y_{2})
are in
G^{{}_{\top}3}
and hence
(x_{1}x_{2},y_{1}y_{2})\in\top.

Locality semigroup is our first structure where the philosophy of locality is made clear. In this structure, one can only multiply objects that are independent. This is one of the basic ideas behind locality structures. It is quite easy to exhibit examples of locality semigroup. Instead, we present a simple counter example of locality semigroup.

Counterexample 3.1.14
.

The set
G
of linear subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a locality set with respect to the following relation
\top_{G}
on linear subspaces of
G
:
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
are called transverse if they intersect trivially, namely if

U\cap V=\{0\}
. The set
G
equipped with linear sums
+
is a monoid. But the corresponding
(G,\top_{G},+)
is not a locality monoid. Indeed, for the standard basis
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the subspaces
\mathbb{R}e_{1}
and
\mathbb{R}e_{2}
both intersect
\mathbb{R}(e_{1}+e_{2})
trivially, but
\mathbb{R}e_{1}+\mathbb{R}e_{2}
does not.

Before defining locality algebras, we need first need a preliminary notion.

Definition 3.1.15
.

Let
V
and
W
be vector spaces and let
\top:=V\times_{\top}W\subseteq V\times W
. A map
f:V\times_{\top}W\to U
to a vector space
U
is called
locality bilinear
(with respect to
\top
) if

f(v_{1}+v_{2},w_{1})=f(v_{1},w_{1})+f(v_{2},w_{1}),\quad f(v_{1},w_{1}+w_{2})=f(v_{1},w_{1})+f(v_{1},w_{2}),

f(kv_{1},w_{1})=kf(v_{1},w_{1}),\quad f(v_{1},kw_{1})=kf(v_{1},w_{1})

for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in V
,
w_{1},w_{2}\in W
and
k\in\mathbb{K}
such that all the pairs arising in the above expressions are in
V\times_{\top}W
.

In most of our applications, the set
V\times_{\top}W\subseteq V\times W
will be clear from context, and therefore we will not write that a map is bilinear
with respect to
V\times_{\top}W
except is absolutely necessary.

Definition 3.1.16
.

1.

A
nonunitary locality algebra
over
\mathbb{K}
is a locality vector space
(A,\top)
over
K
together with a locality bilinear map

m_{A}:A\times_{\top}A\to A

such that

(A,\top,m_{A})
is a locality semi-group in the sense of Definition
3.1.12
.(
1
).

2.

A
locality algebra
is a nonunitary locality algebra
(A,\top,m_{A})
together with a
unit

1_{A}:\mathbb{K}\to A
in the sense that

(A,\top,m_{A},1_{A})
is a locality monoid defined in Definition
3.1.12
. (
3
). We shall omit explicitly mentioning the unit
1_{A}
and the product
m_{A}
unless this generates an ambiguity.

3.

A linear subspace
B
of a locality algebra
\left(A,\top,m_{A}\right)
is called a
sub-locality algebra
of
A
if

B
is a sub-locality semigroup of
A
in the sense of Definition
3.1.12
.(
5
).

4.

A sub-locality algebra
I
of a locality commutative algebra
\left(A,\top,m_{A}\right)
is called a
locality ideal
of
A
if for any
b\in I
we have

b^{\top}\cdot b\subseteq I\penalty\ \forall b^{\top}\in\{b\}^{\top}
.

5.

A locality-linear map
f:(A,\top_{A},\cdot_{A})\to(B,\top_{B},\cdot_{B})
between two (non necessarily unital) locality algebras is called a
locality algebra homomorphism
if

f(u\cdot_{A}v)=f(u)\cdot_{B}f(v)\ \ \forall(u,v)\in\top_{A}.

(3.4)

6.

A locality algebra
A
with a linear grading
A=\oplus_{n\geqslant 0}A_{n}
is called a locality graded algebra if
m_{A}((A_{m}\times A_{n})\cap\top_{A})\subseteq A_{m+n}
for all
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

It is easy to check that a locality linear map
f:(A,\top_{A},\cdot_{A})\to(B,\top_{B},\cdot_{B})
between two locality algebras is a locality algebra homomorphism if and only if
\ker f
is a locality ideal of
A
,
by the same argument as the one for the corresponding result on an algebra homomorphism.

Remark 3.1.17
.

1.

For a locality algebra
(A,\top)
we have
\{0,1_{A}\}\subset A^{\top}
since
0\in A^{\top}
by Remark
3.1.8
.

2.

If
A\times_{\top}A
is
A\times A
in a locality monoid and locality algebra, we recover the usual notions of monoid and algebra.

3.1.3
Locality Rota-Baxter algebras and projection maps

Rota-Baxter operators and algebras are a classical topic (see for example
[
Guo12
]
). Let us now give their locality version.

Definition 3.1.18
.

A linear operator
P:A\to A
on a locality algebra
(A,\top)
over a field
K
is called
a
locality Rota-Baxter operator of
weight

\lambda\in K
, or simply a
Rota-Baxter operator
, if it is a locality map, independent of
\mathrm{Id}_{A}
, and satisfies the following
locality Rota-Baxter relation
:

P({a})\,P({b})=P(P({a})\,{b})+P({a}\,P({b}))+\lambda\,P({a}\,{b})\quad\forall(a,b)\in\top.

(3.5)

We call the triple
(A,\top,P)
a
locality Rota-Baxter algebra
.

Remark 3.1.19
.

1.

The right hand side of Eq. (
3.5
) is well defined due to the condition that
P
is independent of the identity. Then for any
(a,b)\in\top
we have
a\top P(b)
and
P(a)\top b
. Since
P
is a locality map
2
2

2

which is actually implied from being independent of the identity, so that this requirement was actually not necessary. We leave it for readability.
, we also have then
P(a)\top P(b)
so that the left hand side is well-defined

2.

As in the classical setup
[
Guo12
, Proposition 1.1.12]
, if
P
is a locality Rota-Baxter operator of weight
\lambda
, then
-\lambda-P
is also a locality Rota-Baxter of weight
\lambda
.

An important class of locality Rota-Baxter algebras arises from idempotent operators, i.e. projections.

Proposition 3.1.20
.

Let
\left(A,\top,m_{A}\right)
be a locality algebra. Let
P:A\longrightarrow A
be a locality linear idempotent operator in which case there is a linear decomposition
A=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
with
A_{1}=\ker\,(\mathrm{Id}-P)
and
A_{2}=\ker\,(P)
so that
P
is the projection onto
A_{1}
along
A_{2}
. The following statements are equivalent:

1.

P
or
\mathrm{Id}-P
is a locality Rota-Baxter operator of weight
-1
;

2.

A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are locality subalgebras of
A
, and
P
and
\mathrm{Id}-P
are independent locality maps.

If one of the conditions holds, then
P
is a locality multiplicative map if and only if
A_{2}
is a locality ideal of
A
.

Proof.

We write
\pi_{1}=P
and
\pi_{2}=\mathrm{Id}-P
.

•

((
1
)
\Longrightarrow
(
2
))
It follows from the locality Rota-Baxter identity (
3.5
) that
A_{1}=P(A)
is
a sub-locality algebra of
A
. Since
\mathrm{Id}-P
is again an idempotent locality Rota-Baxter operator,
A_{2}=(\mathrm{Id}-P)(A)
is also a sub-locality algebra of
A
. Finally,
P
and
\mathrm{Id}-P
are independent locality maps as a consequence of Definition
3.1.18
: for any
(a,b)\in\top
we have
P(a)\top P(b)
and
P(a)\top b
since
P
is a locality map independent from the identity. Then, since
P(a)^{\top}
is a vector subspace of
A
we have
P(a)\top(\mathrm{Id}-P(b)
.

•

((
2
)
\Longrightarrow
(
1
))
Since
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}=\mathrm{Id}-\pi_{1}
are locality Rota-Baxter operators of weight
-1
at the same time in view of Remark
3.1.19
, we only need to verify that
\pi_{1}
is a locality Rota-Baxter operator of weight
-1
:

\pi_{1}({a})\,\pi_{1}({b})+\pi_{1}(a\,b)=\pi_{1}(\pi_{1}({a})\,{b})+\pi_{1}({a}\,\pi_{1}({b}))\quad\forall({a},{b})\in\top.

(3.6)

Write
a=a_{1}+a_{2}
and
b=b_{1}+b_{2}
. Since the projections
\pi_{i},i=1,2,
are independent locality maps, it follows that
\{a_{1},a_{2}\}\top\{b_{1},b_{2}\}
. Thus every term in

ab=a_{1}b_{1}+a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2}

is well defined, with
a_{1}b_{1}\in A_{1}
and
a_{2}b_{2}\in A_{2}
.
Then the left hand side of Eq. (
3.6
) becomes

a_{1}b_{1}+\pi_{1}(a_{1}b_{1}+a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2})=2a_{1}b_{1}+\pi_{1}(a_{1}b_{2})+\pi_{1}(a_{2}b_{1}).

The right hand side of Eq. (
3.6
) becomes

\pi_{1}(a_{1}b)+\pi_{1}(ab_{1})=\pi_{1}(a_{1}b_{1}+a_{1}b_{2})+\pi_{1}(a_{1}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{1})=\pi_{1}(a_{1}b_{2})+\pi_{1}(a_{2}b_{1})+2a_{1}b_{1},

as needed and since
\pi_{1}
is linear.

•

For the last statement, let us first assume that
P
is multiplicative. Then for any
a\in A_{2}
and
b\in A_{2}^{\top}
we have
P(ab)=P(a)P(b)=0
thus
ab\in{\rm Ker}(P)=A_{2}
and
A_{2}
is a locality ideal. On the other hand if
A_{2}
is an ideal, then for any
(a,b)\in\top
, if we write as before their decomposition
a=a_{1}+a_{2}
,
b=b_{1}+b_{2}
we have on the one hand
P(a)P(b)=a_{1}b_{1}
. On the other hand
P(ab)=P(a_{1}b_{1}+a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2})
. We have
a_{1}b_{1}\in A_{1}
as before but
(a_{1}b_{2}+a_{2}b_{1}+a_{2}b_{2})\in A_{2}
since
A_{2}
is a locality ideal. Thus by linearity
P(ab)=P(a_{1}b_{1})=a_{1}b_{1}
as needed.

∎

The structures we have introduced so far, from locality sets to locality Rota-Baxter algebras, will be used to build a multivariate renormalisation scheme. Our target algebra will be a space of multivariate meromorphic germs, to which we now turn our attention and describe its relevant locatity structures.

3.2
The example of multivariate meromorphic germs

The key ideas of this section are taken from
[
GPZ15
,
GPZ13b
,
GPZ20b
]
from other authors. These constructions are necessary for the rest of this chapter.

3.2.1
Multivariate meromorphic germs

We briefly recall the construction of the algebra of multivariate meromorphic germs and refer the readers to
[
GPZ15
,
GPZ13b
,
GPZ20b
]
for details. This algebra will be the target algebra for the multivariate renormalisation scheme we aim at building.

First recall (for example from
[
Hö68
]
) that a function
f:\mathbb{C}^{k}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is
holomorphic
if each of its partial functions are holomorphic. Equivalently, it obeys the Cauchy-Riemann equations

\overline{\partial}f:=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}_{i}}d\bar{z}_{i}=0.

A
meromorphic function
is a quotient of two holomorphic functions.

As already stated, we are interested in building a multivariate renormalisation scheme. In general, a renormalisation scheme consists of identifying ”problematic“ terms in a regularised expression and removing them in a way that preserves that desired property of the object being renormalised. These problematic terms are typically determined via a Laurent expansion. But Laurent expansion are naturally associated to germs and not function, but this detail is not relevant in this preliminary discussion. It is enough to recall that germs are equivalence classes of functions.
In practice we will always write a germ as one of its representant.

In their seminal article
[
GPZ15
]
, Li Guo, Sylvie Paycha and Bin Zhang developed a theory of Laurent expansions for meromorphic germs in several variable using tools from lattice spaces and cones. We recall now some of these tools that we will use.

Consider the filtered rational Euclidean lattice space

\Big(\mathbb{R}^{\infty}=\bigcup_{\geqslant 1}\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{Z}^{\infty}=\bigcup_{\geqslant 1}\mathbb{Z}^{k},Q=(Q_{k}(.,.))_{k\geqslant 1}\Big),

where

Q_{k}(.,.):\mathbb{R}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R},\quad k\geqslant 1,

is an inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
such that
Q_{k}(\mathbb{Z}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{Z}^{k})\subset\mathbb{Q}
and
Q_{k+1}\circ(j_{k}\otimes j_{k})=Q_{k}
, where
j_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
is the canonical embedding.
Q
induces a locality relation
\perp^{Q}
on
\mathbb{R}^{\infty}
. For example, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
we set

x\perp^{Q}y\penalty\ :\Longleftrightarrow\penalty\ Q_{k+1}(j_{k}(x),y)=0.

(3.7)

The generalisation to any pair
(x,y)\in(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})^{2}
is obvious but cumbersome and will not be written. Using the canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{R}^{k}\cong(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}
we also have a locality relation on
(\mathbb{R}^{\infty})^{*}:=\bigcup_{\geqslant 1}(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}
. We denote this other relation by the same symbol
\perp^{Q}
.

Remark 3.2.1
.

As the name suggests, the construction above is only an example of filtered rational Euclidean lattice space. We will not need other examples of such a structure here and have therefore chosen not to give its definition in full generality, and we refer instead the reader to
[
GPZ20b
, Definition 2.1]
.

In order to see how this framework can be used for meromorphic germs we need a somewhat unusual definition of these objects, which can be worth explaining.

A meromorphic function or germ with
k
variable can be seen as a meromorphic function of germs of linear forms over
\mathbb{C}^{k}
. For example the meromorphic function or germs over
\mathbb{C}^{3}

f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\frac{z_{1}(z_{1}+2z_{3})^{2}}{z_{2}+z_{3}}

is built from the linear maps
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto z_{1}
,
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto(z_{1}+2z_{3})
and
(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto(z_{2}+z_{3})
. Using the canonical isomorphism
Lin(\mathbb{C}^{k},\mathbb{C})\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
we obtain that meromorphic germs can be seen as germs over
(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
.

This space
(\mathbb{C}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
, where the tensor product is taken over
\mathbb{C}
, has complex dimension
k
, so real dimension
2k
. This is the same than the space
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
, where this times the tensor product is taken over
\mathbb{R}
. Thus these spaces are isomorphic and we understand that meromorphic functions or germs with
k
variables can be seen as meromorphic functions or germs over
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
.

We can now understand why the following definition, taken from
[
GPZ15
]
, is adapted to our context. We slightly modified it to fit the framework of
[
CGPZ18
]
.

Definition 3.2.2
.

1.

A
meromorphic function
on
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
is the quotient of two holomorphic functions on
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
with respect to the canonical complex structure on
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
.

2.

A
germ of meromorphic functions at 0
or
meromorphic germ
in short on
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
is the equivalence class of meromorphic functions on
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
for the following equivalence relation: for
f=u_{1}/u_{2}
and
g=v_{1}/v_{2}
two meromorphic functions,
f\sim g
if, and only if, it exists
U
neighborhood of the origin of
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
and
u:U\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
holomorphic such that

u_{1}|_{U}=uv_{1}|_{U}\wedge u_{2}|_{U}=uv_{2}|_{U}.

(essentially,
f
and
g
coincide on some neighborhood of the origin).

3.

A germ of meromorphic functions
f
on
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}
is said to have
linear poles at zero with rational
coefficients
if there exist vectors
L_{1},\cdots,L_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{k}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
(possibly with repetitions)
such that
f\prod L_{i}
is a holomorphic germ at zero whose Taylor expansion for coordinates
in the dual basis
\{e^{*}_{1},\cdots,e^{*}_{k}\}
of a given (and hence every) basis
\{e_{1},\cdots,e_{k}\}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{k}
has
coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}
.

4.

We write
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left((\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}\right)
the vector space of these meromorphic germs with linear poles at zero and rational coefficients.

3.2.2
Splitting of meromorphic germs

Now,
Q_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
induces an isomorphism
Q_{k}^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\longrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}
defined by

Q_{k}^{*}:u\mapsto\Big(Q_{k}(u,.):v\mapsto Q_{k}(u,v)\Big).

This induces a projection
(\mathbb{R}^{k+1})^{*}\twoheadrightarrow(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}
which in turn induces an embedding
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}((\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}((\mathbb{R}^{k+1})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})
. Thus we have a directed system and we can set

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}:=\varinjlim_{k}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}\left((\mathbb{R}^{k})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}\right).

(3.8)

By
[
GPZ20b
, Corollary 4.18]
, any element of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
can be written as a sum of a holomorphic germ and elements the form

\frac{h(\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{m})}{L_{1}^{s_{1}}\cdots L_{n}^{s_{n}}},\quad s_{1},\cdots,s_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0},

(3.9)

where
h
is a holomorphic germ with rational coefficients in linear forms
\ell_{1},\cdots,\ell_{m}\in(\mathbb{Q}^{k})^{*}
, and
L_{1},\cdots,L_{n}
are linearly independent linear forms in
(\mathbb{Q}^{k})^{*}
,
\ell_{i}\perp^{Q}L_{j}
for all
i\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
and
j\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
. An element of the form (
3.9
) which is called a
polar germ
(for the inner product
Q
).

In other words, writing
\mathcal{M}_{+}
the space of holomorphic germs and
\mathcal{M}_{-}^{Q}
the set of polar germs of the form (
3.9
) we have a splitting (
[
GPZ20b
, Corollary 4.18]
)

\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathcal{M}_{+}\oplus\mathcal{M}_{-}^{Q}.

(3.10)

Remark 3.2.3
.

This splitting depends of the chosen family of inner products
Q
, so a more appropriate notation would be
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\mathcal{M}_{+}\oplus_{Q}\mathcal{M}_{-}^{Q}
which we elected not to use to improve readibility. Notice also that while the set
\mathcal{M}_{-}^{Q}
depends on the chosen
Q
, the set of holomorphic germs
\mathcal{M}_{+}
does not.

Below are two examples of this decomposition on meromorphic germs.

Example 3.2.4
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}((\mathbb{R}^{2})^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})\ni g
defined by

f(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{z_{1}}{z_{1}-z_{2}},\qquad g(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{z_{1}z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}.

To find their holomorphic and singular parts with respect to the canonical scalar product on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
we decompose them as

f(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{z_{1}-z_{2}+z_{2}+z_{1}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right).

Thus, we find that the holomorphic part of
f
is the constant germ
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto 1/2
and its singular part is
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}
. The latter is indeed singular w.r.t. the canonical scalar product since
\langle e_{1}+e_{2},e_{1}-e_{2}\rangle=0
.

For
g
we have

\displaystyle g(z_{1},z_{2})=\frac{z_{2}}{2}\left(1+\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{4}(z_{2}-z_{1}+z_{1}+z_{2})\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{2}}{2}-\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{4}+\frac{1}{4}\frac{(z_{1}+z_{2})^{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}.

Thus the holomorphic part of
g
is
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\frac{z_{2}}{2}-\frac{z_{1}+z_{2}}{4}
and its singular part is
(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\frac{1}{4}\frac{(z_{1}+z_{2})^{2}}{z_{1}-z_{2}}
(both w.r.t. the canonical scalar product).

3.2.3
The locality structure

We can now define an independence relation on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
which is induced by
\perp^{Q}
and that we therefore also denote by this symbol.

Definition 3.2.5
.

(
[
GPZ20a
]
)
For a meromorphic function
f
, the
dependence subspace

{\rm Dep}(f)
is the smallest subspace for the inclusion of
(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{*}
on which it depends. For a meromorphic germ, the dependence subspace is the dependence subspace of any of its representing elements.

Two meromorphic germs with rational coefficients
f
and
g
are
orthogonal
(with respect to the given
Q
) if
{\rm Dep}(f)\perp^{Q}{\rm Dep}(g)
. Then we denote
f\perp^{Q}g
. Let
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\perp^{Q})
denote the resulting locality set.

Other locality structures on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
exist, see
[
CGPZ18
,
CGPZ19
]
. This one is a good one for our application in that it encodes and makes rigorous the simple idea that two germs are independent if they “don’t depend on the same set of variables”
or if they can be written as depending on orthogonal variables
(w.r.t. the chosen scalar product). We illustrate this concept in the follwing example.

Example 3.2.6
.

Let
f,g:\mathbb{C}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be defined by

f(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=z_{1}-z_{3},\qquad g(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=z_{2}(z_{1}+z_{3}).

Then Dep
(f)=\langle e^{*}_{1}-e^{*}_{3}\rangle
and Dep
(g)=\langle e^{*}_{2},e^{*}_{1}+e^{*}_{3}\rangle
with
\{e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*},e_{3}^{\}}
the canonical basis of
(\mathbb{R}^{3})^{*}
. Then
f\perp^{Q}g
for the canonical scalar product since Dep
(f)
and Dep
(g)
are orthogonal.

It is clear that
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\perp^{Q})
actually belongs to a more sophisticated locality category.

Proposition 3.2.7
.

(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\perp^{Q})
is a locality vector space. Furthermore, the restricted multiplication
m:\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\perp^{Q}\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
endows
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\perp^{Q})
with a locality algebra structure.

Thus, the splitting given by Equation (
3.10
) together with Propositions
3.1.20
and
3.2.7
directly imply a result that will be crucial for our multivariate renormalisation scheme.

Proposition 3.2.8
.

In the decomposition in Equation (
3.10
), the space
\mathcal{M}_{+}
is a subalgebra and a locality subalgebra of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. The space
\mathcal{M}_{-}^{Q}
is not a subalgebra but a locality subalgebra, in fact a locality ideal of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. Consequently, the projection
\pi_{+}^{Q}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}\to\mathcal{M}_{+}
is a locality algebra homomorphism and
(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\pi_{-}^{Q})
is a locality Rota-Baxter algebra..

In contrast to the multivariate case, the space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{Q},-}^{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})=\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbb{C}[\varepsilon^{-1}]
is a subalgebra in the space
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})
of meromorphic functions in one variable.
This is a major difference between our multivariate setup and the usual single variable framework used for renormalisation purposes. We circumvent the difficulty in relaxing ordinary multiplicativity to a multiplicativity allowed only on independent elements. In fact,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q},-}^{Q}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})
is a locality ideal of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})
under the restriction of independence relation since the locality relation
\perp^{Q}
restricted to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})
is simply
(\mathbb{C}\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C}))\cup(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})\times\mathbb{C})
. Thus, the locality algebra homomorphism
\pi_{+}^{Q}
restricts to a mere linear map on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\mathbb{R}^{*}\otimes\mathbb{C})
with no additional multiplicativity property.

3.3
Locality tensor products

The next locality categories we are interested in are the pendant of coalgebraic structures. For this, we need locality tensors which we now present. This presentation is from
[
CFLVP22
]
.

Let us recall that the tensor product of two vector spaces
V
and
W
reads

V\otimes W:=\mathbb{K}(V\times W)\big/{\rm I_{bil}},

(3.11)

where
\mathbb{K}(V\times W)
is the vector space freely spanned by
V\times W
and
{\rm I_{bil}}
defined as its vector subspace generated by all elements of the form

\displaystyle(a+b,x)-(a,x)-(b,x),\qquad

\displaystyle(a,x+y)-(a,x)-(a,y),

\displaystyle(ka,x)-k(a,x),\qquad

\displaystyle(a,kx)-k(a,x).

We will build locality tensor products in the same fashion and want to endow them will a locality relation, so we turn our attention to the more general problem of endowing quotient of locality vector space with a locality relation.

3.3.1
Quotient locality as a final locality relation

We define a final locality in much the same way as a final topology.
Recall that given two topologies
\tau_{1}
,
\tau_{2}
on some set
X
,
\tau_{1}
is said to be
coarser (weaker or smaller)
than
\tau_{2}
, or equivalently
\tau_{2}

finer (stronger or larger)

than
\tau_{1}
if, and only if
\tau_{1}\subset\tau_{2}
.
Also, given a set
X
and
(X_{i},\tau_{i})_{i\in I}
a family of topological spaces together with a family of maps
f_{i}:X_{i}\to X
, the
final topology (or strong, colimit, coinduced, or inductive topology)

\bar{\tau}
is the finest topology on
X
such that all maps
f_{i}
are continuous.
With a small abuse of language, one says that the topology
\bar{\tau}
is final with respect to the maps
f_{i}
.

Let us now transpose this terminology to the locality setup.

Definition 3.3.1
.

•

Let
\top_{1}
and
\top_{2}
be two locality relations over a set
A
. We say
\top_{1}
is
coarser
than
\top_{2}
or equivalently, that
\top_{2}
is

finer
than
\top_{1}
if, and only if
\top_{1}\subset\top_{2}
.

•

Let
X
be a set,
(X_{i},\top_{i})_{i\in I}
a family of locality sets, and
f_{i}:X_{i}\to X
a family of maps. The
final locality relation
{\overline{\top}}

on
X
is the coarsest locality relation among the locality relations
\top
on
X
for which

f_{i}:(X_{i},\top_{i})\longrightarrow(X,\top),\quad i\in I

are locality maps.

As before, with a slight abuse of language, we shall say that
{\overline{\top}}
is a
final locality relation
on
X
for the maps
f_{i}
.

Let us see with an example how taking inspiration from topologies is relevant for locality.

Example 3.3.2
.

Let
X
be a set and
{\mathcal{P}}(X)
its power set. Disjointness of sets:

A\top B\Longleftrightarrow A\cap B=\emptyset

defines a locality relation on any subset
{\mathcal{O}}
of
{\mathcal{P}}(X)
.
If
(X,{\mathcal{O}})
is a topological space with topology
{\mathcal{O}}\subset{\mathcal{P}}(X)
, this disjointness relation gives rise to
another locality relation (which with some abuse of notation, we denote by the same notation) given by the separation of points:

x\top y\Longleftrightarrow\exists U,V\in{\mathcal{O}},\quad\left(U\,\top\,V\right)\,\wedge\,\left(x\in U\wedge y\in V\right).

The finer (coarser) the topology
{\mathcal{O}}
, the larger (smaller) the graph
\{(x,y),\,x\top y\}
of the locality relation, hence the terminology we have chosen.

We can characterise final locality relations.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

Given a surjective map
{\phi:A\to B}
, the locality relation
\top
on
A
induces a locality relation
{\overline{\top}}
on
B
defined by

b_{1}{\overline{\top}}b_{2}\Longleftrightarrow(\exists(a_{1},a_{2})\in A\times A:\phi(a_{i})=b_{i}\ \rm{and}\ a_{1}\top a_{2}),

which is the final locality relation for the map
\phi
.

Proof.

It is clear from the definition of
{\overline{\top}}
, that
\phi:(A,\top)\longrightarrow(B,{\overline{\top}})
is a locality map.

Let
\top_{B}
be a locality relation on
B
such that
{\phi:(A,\top)\longrightarrow(B,\top_{B})}
is a locality map. For any
(b_{1},b_{2})\in B^{2}
we have

\displaystyle b_{1}{\overline{\top}}b_{2}\penalty\

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\penalty\ \left(\exists(a_{1},a_{2})\in A^{2}|\phi(a_{i})=b_{i}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ a_{1}\top a_{2}\right)\quad\text{for }i\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\penalty\ \left(\exists(a_{1},a_{2})\in A^{2}|\phi(a_{i})=b_{i}\penalty\ \wedge\penalty\ \phi(a_{1})\top_{B}\phi(a_{2})\right)\quad\text{since }\phi\text{ is a locality map}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\penalty\ b_{1}\top_{B}b_{2}.

Therefore
{\overline{\top}}\subseteq\top_{B}
.
∎

Applying Proposition
3.3.3
to the canonical projection map
\pi:V\to V/W
of a locality vector space
(V,\top)
to its quotient
V/W
by a linear subspace
W
, we equip the quotient with the quotient locality relation.

Definition 3.3.4
.

For a subspace
W
of a locality vector space
(V,\top)
, we call
quotient locality
on
the quotient
V/W
, the final locality relation

\left([u]{\overline{\top}}[v]\penalty\ \Longleftrightarrow\exists(u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in[u]\times[v]:\penalty\ u^{\prime}\top v^{\prime}\right)\quad\quad\forall([u],[v])\in(V/W)^{2}

for the canonical projection map
\pi:V\to V/W
.

Notice that
(V/W,{\overline{\top}})
is a vector space with a locality relation but it is a priori
not
in general a locality vector space
3
3

3

it is however a “pre-locality vector space”, a notion developed in
[
CFLVP22
]
to tackle this type of issue.
. The general question “when is a quotient of locality vector spaces a locality vector space for the quotient locality” seems very interesting but deeply out of reach. We just give a couterexample to show that the answer to this question cannot always be positive.

Counterexample 3.3.5
.

We equip the vector space
V
of real valued maps on
\mathbb{R}
with the locality relation
\top
given by disjoint supports:
f\top g\Longleftrightarrow{\rm supp}(f)\cap{\rm supp}(g)=\emptyset
. Let
W
denote the linear subspace of constant functions. Consider the three functions
u,v,w
in
V
defined by

\displaystyle v:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{R}&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
x&\longmapsto&\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }x>1,\\
0\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle u:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{R}&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
x&\longmapsto&\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }x>0,\\
0\mbox{ otherwise},\end{cases}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle w:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{R}&\longrightarrow&\mathbb{R}\\
x&\longmapsto&\begin{cases}1\mbox{ if }x>2,\\
0\mbox{ otherwise}.\end{cases}\end{array}\right.

-2
-1
1
2
1

-2
-1
1
2
1

-2
-1
1
2
1

Then, in
V/W
, we have
[u]{\overline{\top}}[v]
since
u\top(v-1)
and
[u]{\overline{\top}}[w]
since
(u-1)\top w
. However,

[u]\cancel{{\overline{\top}}}[v+w]
. Thus
V/W
is not a locality vector space for
{\overline{\top}}
.

3.3.2
Locality tensor products

For two vector subspaces
V
and
W
of an ambient locality vector space
E
, we want the locality version of the tensor product to depend on the locality on
E
. We will define this locality tensor product in a similar fashion than the usual tensor (Equation (
3.11
)).

Definition 3.3.6
.

Given
V
and
W
subspaces of a locality vector space
(E,\top)
, the locality tensor product is the vector space

V\otimes_{\top}W:={\mathbb{K}(V\times_{\top}W)}\big/{I_{\rm bil}^{\top_{\times}}}

(3.12)

with
I_{\rm bil}^{\top_{\times}}:=\mathbb{K}(V\times_{\top}W)\cap I_{\rm bil}
.

Remark 3.3.7
.

Since
V\times_{\top}W\subset V\times W
and
I_{\rm bil}^{\top_{\times}}:=\mathbb{K}(V\times_{\top}W)\cap I_{\rm bil}
, we have an inclusion of vector spaces
V\otimes_{\top}W\subset V\otimes W
. This inclusion of vector spaces is what motivated this choice of locality tensor product over other options. If
V\times_{\top}W=V\times W
, then
V\otimes_{\top}W=V\otimes W
.

Many usual properties of usual tensor products are conserved by this locality tensor product, and in particular various form of their well-known universal property. This is beyond the scope of this thesis and we refer the readers to
[
CFLVP22
, Part I]
for details.

We will also need higher locality tensor products. These need further construction to be properly defined. First, for
(E,\top)
a locality vector space over
\mathbb{K}
, and
V_{1},\cdots,V_{n}
linear subspaces of
E
, let
I_{{\rm mult}}{(V_{1},\cdots,V_{n})}
generated by all elements of the form

\displaystyle(x_{1},...,x_{i-1},a_{i}+b_{i},x_{i+1},...,

\displaystyle x_{n})-(x_{1},...,x_{i-1},a_{i},x_{i+1},...,x_{n})-(x_{1},...,x_{i-1},b_{i},x_{i+1},...,x_{n})

\displaystyle(x_{1},...,kx_{i},...,x_{n})-k(x_{1},...,x_{i},...,x_{n})

for every
i\in[n]
,
k\in\mathbb{K}
and
a_{i},b_{i},x_{i}\in V_{i}
. If
V_{1}=\cdots=V_{n}=V
, we write
I_{{\rm mult},n}(V)
.

Definition 3.3.8
.

We define

•

the
locality cartesian product

V_{1}\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}V_{n}:=\{(x_{1},...,x_{n})\in V_{1}\times\cdots\times V_{n}|\forall(i,j\in[n]):i\neq j\Rightarrow(x_{i},x_{j})\in V_{i}\times_{\top}V_{j}\};

If
V_{i}=V
for any
i\in[n]
, we set
V^{\times_{\top}^{n}}:=V_{1}\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}V_{n}=V\times_{\top}\dots\times_{\top}V
.
In particular
V^{\times_{\top}2}=\top
,
V^{\times_{\top}1}=V
and we set by convention
V^{\times_{\top}0}=\mathbb{K}
.
Note that
V_{1}\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}V_{n}\ni(0_{E},0_{E},\cdots,0_{E})
where
0_{E}
is the zero element in
E
, since
0_{E}\top 0_{E}
by definition of a locality vector space.

•

the
locality tensor product

V_{1}\otimes_{\top}\cdots\otimes_{\top}V_{n}\penalty\ :=\penalty\ {\mathbb{K}(V_{1}\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}V_{n})}\big/I^{\top_{\times}}_{{\rm mult}}(V_{1},\cdots,V_{n})

(3.13)

with
I^{\top_{\times}}_{{\rm mult}}(V_{1},\cdots,V_{n}):=(I_{{\rm mult}}{(V_{1},\cdots,V_{n})}\cap\mathbb{K}(V_{1}\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}V_{n}))
.

If
V_{i}=V
for any
i\in[n]
, we set
V^{\otimes_{\top}^{n}}:=V_{1}\otimes_{\top}\cdots\otimes_{\top}V_{n}
.

•

We endow the locality tensor product
V_{1}\otimes_{\top}\cdots\otimes_{\top}V_{n}
with the locality relation
\top_{\otimes n}
defined as the quotient locality (see Definition
3.3.4
) for the quotient map
\pi_{n}:\mathbb{K}(V_{1}\times_{\top}\cdots\times_{\top}V_{n})\longrightarrow V_{1}\otimes_{\top}\cdots\otimes_{\top}V_{n}
.

This higher locality tensor products allow us to define more sophisticated objects such as the locality tensor algebra and the locality universal envelopping algebra of a (locality) Lie group. These objects in turn have universal properties in suitable locality categories
4
4

4

at least conjecturally. The proof only exists in a weaker case, for
pre
-locality vector space, a structure defined in
[
CFLVP22
]
.
. Once again, this lies beyond the scope of this thesis and we refer the reader to
[
CFLVP22
]
for more elaborate constructions.

Let us finish this section with an intriguing remark. The quotient locality relation allow us to endow locality tensor products with a locality structure. These are easy enough to describe explicitly: let
(E,\top)
be a locality vector space. Then for
X,Y\in E\otimes_{\top}E
, we have
X\top_{\otimes 2}Y
if we can write

X=\sum_{i}v_{i}\otimes w_{i},\qquad Y=\sum_{i}v^{\prime}_{i}\otimes w^{\prime}_{i}

such that
(v_{i},w_{i},v^{\prime}_{j},w^{\prime}_{j})\in E^{\times_{\top}4}
for any
i
and
j
. An obvious generalisation exists for higher tensors products.

However, it is still a conjecture that
(E\otimes_{\top}E,\top_{\otimes 2})
is a locality vector space. We call it conjecture rather than open question since it is a locality vector space for many cases of interest and numerous attempts to build counterexamples have all failed. To tackle this question might very well require tools that are beyond the theory of locality structures, or a rather large enlargement of that theory.

3.4
Coalgebraic locality structures

3.4.1
Locality coalgebras

We recall that
a coalgebra
(C,\Delta)
over a field
\mathbb{K}
is
counital
if there is a map
\varepsilon:C\to\mathbb{K}
such that
(\varepsilon\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{C})\Delta=(\mathrm{Id}_{C}\otimes\varepsilon)\Delta=\mathrm{Id}_{C}
. It is
(
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
-)graded
if

C=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}}C_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta(C_{n})\subseteq\bigoplus_{p+q=n}C_{p}\otimes C_{q},\quad\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 1}C_{n}\subseteq\ker\varepsilon.

Thus
C=C_{0}+\ker\varepsilon
.
Moreover a graded coalgebra is
called
connected
if
C=C_{0}\oplus\ker\varepsilon
. Consequently,
\varepsilon
restricts to a linear bijection
\varepsilon:C_{0}\cong K
and
\ker\varepsilon=\oplus_{n\geqslant 1}C_{n}
.

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
(C,\top)
be a locality vector space and let
\Delta:C\to C\otimes C
be a linear map.
(C,\top,\Delta)
is a
locality noncounital coalgebra
if it satisfies the following two conditions

1.

for any
U\subset C
(compare with Eq. (
3.2
))

\Delta(U^{\top})\subset U^{\top}\otimes_{\top}U^{\top}.

(3.14)

In particular,
\Delta(C)\subseteq C\otimes_{\top}C
;

2.

the following
coassociativity
holds:

(\mathrm{Id}_{C}\otimes\Delta)\,\Delta=(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{C})\,\Delta.

•

If in addition, there is a
counit
, namely a linear map
\varepsilon:C\to\mathbb{K}
such that

({\rm Id}_{C}\otimes\varepsilon)\,\Delta=(\varepsilon\otimes{\rm Id}_{C})\,\Delta={\rm Id}_{C}
, then
(C,\top,\Delta,\varepsilon)
is called a
locality coalgebra.

•

A
connected locality coalgebra
is a locality coalgebra
(C,\top,\Delta)
with a grading
C=\oplus_{n\geqslant 0}C_{n}
such that, for any
U\subseteq C
,

\Delta(C_{n}\cap U^{\top})\subseteq\bigoplus_{p+q=n}(C_{p}\cap U^{\top})\otimes_{\top}(C_{q}\cap U^{\top}),\quad\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 1}C_{n}=\ker\varepsilon.

(3.15)

We denote by
J
the unique element of
C_{0}
with
\varepsilon(J)=1_{\mathbb{K}}
, giving
C_{0}=\mathbb{K}\,J
.

Remark 3.4.2
.

Notice that whereas the conditions for a locality algebra are weaker than those for an algebra, the conditions for a locality coalgebra are stronger than those for a coalgebra. In particular, a locality coalgebra is a coalgebra and a connected locality coalgebra is a connected coalgebra.

Let us list a few useful general properties of locality coalgebras.

Lemma 3.4.3
.

Let
(C,\top,\Delta)
be a locality coalgebra.

1.

For any
n\geqslant 2
and
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
,

\mathrm{Id}_{C}^{\otimes i}\otimes\Delta\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{C}^{\otimes(n-i-1)}:C^{\otimes_{\top}n}\to C^{\otimes_{\top}(n+1)}

(3.16)

2.

We have

(\Delta\otimes\Delta)(C\otimes_{\top}C)\subseteq C^{\otimes_{\top}4}
;

3.

\Delta:(C,\top)\longrightarrow(C\otimes_{\top}C,\top_{\otimes 2})
is a locality map, i.e.:

(\Delta\times\Delta)(C\times_{\top}C)\subseteq(C\otimes_{\top}C)\times_{\top}(C\otimes_{\top}C)
;

4.

For any locality linear map
\phi
independent of
\mathrm{Id}
,
n\geqslant 2
and
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we have

\mathrm{Id}^{\otimes i}\otimes\phi\otimes\mathrm{Id}^{\otimes(n-i-1)}:C^{\otimes_{\top}n}\to C^{\otimes_{\top}n}
.

Proof.

1.

Let
n\geqslant 2
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
be given. By the definition of
C^{\otimes_{\top}n}
, any of its elements is a finite sum of pure tensors
c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n}
with
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n})\in C^{{}_{\top}n}
. Let
U=\{c_{j}\,|\,j\not=i+1\}
. Then
c_{i+1}\in U^{\top}
,
so by Equation (
3.14
) there exist
(d_{1},e_{1}),\cdots,(d_{k},e_{k})\in U^{\top}\times_{\top}U^{\top}
, such that

\Delta(c_{i+1})=\sum_{\ell}d_{\ell}\otimes e_{\ell}.

Now

(\mathrm{Id}_{C}^{\otimes i}\otimes\Delta\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{C}^{\otimes(n-i-1)})(c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n})=\sum_{\ell}c_{1}\otimes\cdots c_{i}\otimes d_{\ell}\otimes e_{\ell}\otimes c_{i+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n},

and
c_{1}\otimes\cdots c_{i}\otimes d_{\ell}\otimes e_{\ell}\otimes c_{i+2}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n}\in C^{\otimes_{\top}(n+1)}
.

2.

Since
(\Delta\otimes\Delta)=(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{Id})(\mathrm{Id}\otimes\Delta)
, from Equation (
3.16
) we obtain

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\Delta)(C\otimes_{\top}C)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{Id})(\mathrm{Id}\otimes\Delta)(C\otimes_{\top}C)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle(\Delta\otimes\mathrm{Id})(C\otimes_{\top}C\otimes_{\top}C)

\displaystyle\subseteq

\displaystyle C\otimes_{\top}C\otimes_{\top}C\otimes_{\top}C.

3.

Let
(c_{1},c_{2})\in C\times_{\top}C
. Then
c_{2}\in\{c_{1}\}^{\top}
. So by Equation (
3.14
),
\Delta(c_{2})=\sum_{(c_{2})}c_{2,(1)}\otimes c_{2,(2)}
with
c_{2,(1)}\top c_{2,(1)}
and
\{c_{2,(1)},c_{2,(2)}\}\subseteq\{c_{1}\}^{\top}.
Thus
c_{1}\in\{c_{2,(1)},c_{2,(2)}\}^{\top}
. By Equation (
3.14
) again,
\Delta(c_{1})=\sum_{(c_{1})}c_{1,(1)}\otimes c_{2,(2)}
with
c_{1,(1)}\top c_{1,(2)}
and
\{c_{1,(1)},c_{1,(2)}\}\subseteq\{c_{2,(1)},c_{2,(2)}\}^{\top}.
This shows that
(c_{1,(1)},c_{1,(2)},c_{2,(1)},c_{2,(2)})
is in
C^{{}_{\top}4}
and hence
\big((c_{1,(1)}\otimes c_{1,(2)}),(c_{2,(1)}\otimes c_{2,(2)})\big)
is in
\top_{\otimes 2}
since this relation is defined as a final locality relation.

4.

Again any element of
C^{\otimes_{\top}n}
is a sum of pure tensors
c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n}
with
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{n})\in C^{{}_{\top}n}
. Thus
(c_{1},\cdots,c_{i-1},\phi(c_{i}),c_{i+1},\cdots,c_{n})
is in
C^{{}_{\top}n}
. This is what we want since
(\mathrm{Id}^{\otimes i}\otimes\phi\otimes\mathrm{Id}^{\otimes(n-i-1)})(c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n})=c_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{i-1}\otimes\phi(c_{i})\otimes c_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{n}
.

∎

We now define and state properties of the reduced locality coproduct which will be of use in the sequel.

Lemma 3.4.4
.

Let
(C=\oplus_{n\geqslant 0}C_{n},\top,\Delta)
be a connected locality coalgebra. Define the
reduced coproduct

\tilde{\Delta}(c):=\Delta(c)-J\otimes c-c\otimes J
.
Recursively define

\tilde{\Delta}^{(1)}=\tilde{\Delta},\quad\tilde{\Delta}^{(k)}:=\left(\mathrm{Id}\otimes\tilde{\Delta}^{(k-1)}\right)\tilde{\Delta},\ \ k\geqslant 2.

(3.17)

1.

For
c\in\oplus_{n\geqslant 1}C_{n}
,

\tilde{\Delta}(c)=\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}
with
\deg(c^{\prime}),\deg(c^{\prime\prime})>0
and
(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})\in C\times_{\top}C
;

2.

If in addition
c\in U^{\top}
for some
U\subset C
, then the above pairs

(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime})
are in
U^{\top}\times_{\top}U^{\top}
;

3.

\tilde{\Delta}^{(k)}(x)
is in
C^{\otimes_{\top}(k+1)}
for all
x\in C,k\in\mathbb{N}
;

4.

\tilde{\Delta}^{(k)}(C_{n})=\{0\}
for all
k\geqslant n.

Proof.

We only need to prove the second point since the first one is the special case when
U=\{0\}
.

Let
c\in C_{n}\cap U^{\top}
. By Eq. (
3.15
),
we can write

\Delta(c)=y\otimes J+J\otimes z+\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}

with
y,z\in C_{n}
,
c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}\in U^{\top}
and each
c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}\in C_{p}\otimes_{\top}C_{q},p+q=n,p,q\geqslant 1
. Then by the same argument for a connected coalgebra
[
Guo12
, Theorem 2.3.3]
, we obtain
y=z=x
. This proves (
2
).

Then (
3
) follows from an easy induction on
k
by the locality property of
\Delta
; while the proof of (
4
) is similar to the case without a locality structure
[
Man03
, Proposition II.2.1]
.
∎

3.4.2
Locality bialgebras and locality Hopf algebras

As in the usual, non-locality case, we can now merge the locality algebra and locality coalgebra structures to obtain locality bialgebras.

Definition 3.4.5
.

1.

An
locality bialgebra
is a sextuple
(B,\top,m,u,\Delta,\varepsilon)
consisting of a locality algebra
(B,m,u,\top)
and a locality coalgebra
\left(B,\Delta,\top,\varepsilon\right)

that are locality compatible in the sense that
\Delta
and
\varepsilon
are locality algebra homomorphisms.

2.

A locality bialgebra
B
is called
connected
if there is a
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
-grading
B=\oplus_{n\geqslant 0}B_{n}
with respect to which
B
is both a locality graded algebra in the sense of Definition
3.1.16
and a connected locality coalgebra in the sense of Definition
3.4.1
. Then
J=1_{B}
.

For cases in which the unit and counit play no role, we will write
(B,\top,m,\Delta)
for a locality bialgebra.

The last structure we need to introduce is the Hopf algebra. It is define through the convolution product which we now introduce.

Definition 3.4.6
.

Let
\left(C,\top_{C},\Delta\right)
be a locality coalgebra and let
\left(A,\top_{A},m_{A}\right)
be a locality algebra. Let
{\mathcal{L}}:=\Hom_{\rm loc}(C,A)
be the space of locality linear maps.
Define

\top_{\mathcal{L}}:=\left\{(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{L}\times\mathcal{L}\,|\,(\phi\times\psi)(C\times_{\top}C)\subseteq A\times_{\top}A\right\}.

For
(\phi,\psi)\in\top_{\mathcal{L}}
, define the
convolution product
of
\phi
and
\psi
by

\phi\star\psi:C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Delta_{C}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\otimes_{\top}C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi\otimes\psi}}{{\longrightarrow}}A\otimes_{\top}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m_{A}}}{{\longrightarrow}}A.

(3.18)

We postpone to the next subsection the study of the convolution product. For now, let us just point out that for
(\phi,\psi)\in\top_{\mathcal{L}}
, since
(\phi\times\psi)(C\times_{\top}C)\subseteq A\times_{\top}A
, we have

(\phi\otimes\psi)(C\otimes_{\top}C)\subseteq A\otimes_{\top}A.

Hence the composition in Equation (
3.18
) is well-defined, giving a well-defined convolution product.

Definition 3.4.7
.

A
locality Hopf algebra
is a locality bialgebra
\left(B,\top,m,\Delta,u,\varepsilon\right)
with an antipode, defined to be a linear map
S:B\to B

such that
S
and
\mathrm{Id}_{B}
are mutually independent (in the sense of Definition
3.1.10
) and

S\star\mathrm{Id}=\mathrm{Id}\star S=u\varepsilon.

The usual proof (see e.g.
[
Guo12
,
Man03
]
) for the existence of the antipode on connected bialgebras extends to locality bialgebras as follows. For
k\geqslant 1
, denote
m_{1}=m
and
m_{k}=m(\mathrm{Id}_{B}\otimes m_{k-1})
. We omit it here and simply recall its spirit. First prove.

Lemma 3.4.8
.

Let
\left(B,\top,m,u,\Delta,\varepsilon\right)
be a connected locality bialgebra,
\tilde{\Delta}^{\otimes k}
as in Eq. (
3.17
) and
\alpha:B\to B
a locality linear map with
\alpha(1_{B})=0
. Then

1.

\alpha^{\star k}=m_{k-1}\alpha^{\otimes k}\tilde{\Delta}^{(k-1)}
for all
k\geqslant 2
;

2.

\alpha^{\star k}(B_{n})=\{0\}
for all
k\geqslant n+1.

(The first point is easily proven by induction and the second is a direct consequence of the first one and Lemma
3.4.4
.(
4
).

We then have the locality version of the Sweedler-Takeuchi formula
[
Tak71
]
:

Proposition 3.4.9
.

Let
\left(B,\top,m,u,\Delta,\varepsilon\right)
be a graded connected locality bialgebra.
There is a linear map
S:{B}\to{B}
with the properties of the antipode stated above. It is given by

S=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(u\varepsilon-\mathrm{Id})^{\star k}.

This is proven with the previous Lemma by setting
\alpha:B\to B
defined by
\alpha=\mathrm{Id}-u\varepsilon
, which is locality linear, and
\alpha(1_{B})=0
. The geometric series
S=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\alpha^{\star k}
which is locally
finite by Lemma
3.4.8
.(
2
) and hence well-defined, gives the inverse of the identity for the convolution product. We postpone to the next section the proof that
\alpha
is a locality map and the
S
is independent from the identity map.

3.4.3
Locality and the convolution product

We show that the locality (independence) of linear maps are preserved under the convolution product. We start with a Lemma that is of use to prove Proposition
3.4.9
.

Lemma 3.4.10
.

Let
(C,\top_{C},\Delta)
be a locality coalgebra with counit
\varepsilon_{C}:C\longrightarrow\mathbb{K}
. Let
(A,\top_{A})
be a locality algebra with unit
u_{A}:\mathbb{K}\longrightarrow A
.
The map
e:=u_{A}\varepsilon_{C}:C\longrightarrow A
is independent to any linear map
\phi:C\longrightarrow A
. In particular, the map
e
is a locality linear map.

Proof.

This is because
\mathrm{im}\,e=\mathbb{K}\cdot 1_{A}\subset A^{\top_{A}}
as we can see from Remark
3.1.17
.
∎

We now prove the aforementioned results regarding convolution of locality maps. We state together the main points of
[
CGPZ18
, Proposition 4.9 and Theorem 5.7]
.

Theorem 3.4.11
.

Let
\left(C,\top_{C},\Delta\right)
(resp.
(B,\top_{B},m,\Delta)
) be a locality coalgebra (resp. a locality bialgebra) and
\left(A,\top_{A},\cdot\right)
be a locality commutative algebra. Let

\phi,\psi:\left(C,\top_{C}\right)\longrightarrow\left(A,\top_{A}\right)

(resp.
\phi,\psi:\left(B,\top_{B}\right)\longrightarrow\left(A,\top_{A}\right)
) be independent locality linear maps (resp. locality algebra homomorphisms).

1.

\phi\star\psi
is a locality linear map (resp. a locality algebra homomorphism) and
the triple
({\mathcal{L}},\top_{\mathcal{L}},\star)
is a locality algebra.

2.

If moreover
C
(resp.
B
) is connected then

\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}:=\{\phi\in\mathcal{L}\ |\ \phi(J)=1_{A}\}

is a locality group for the convolution product.

3.

Resp., if
B
is connected and
\phi
is a homomorphism of locality algebras, then so is its convolution inverse
\phi^{\star(-1)}
. So the set
\mathcal{G}
of homomorphisms of locality algebras from
(B,\top_{B})
to
(A,\top_{A})
is a locality group with respect to the independent relation of locality linear maps.

Proof.

1.

We separate the three statements of this first point.

•

We first verify that
\phi\star\psi
is a locality linear map. For
c_{1}\top_{C}\,c_{2}
,
by Lemma
3.4.3
.(
3
), there are finitely many
(d_{i},e_{i}),(f_{j},g_{j})\in C\times_{\top}C
with
(d_{i},e_{i},f_{j},g_{j})\in C^{{}_{\top}4}
, such that

\Delta(c_{1})=\sum_{i}d_{i}\otimes e_{i}\quad\text{ and}\quad\Delta(c_{2})=\sum_{j}f_{j}\otimes g_{j}.

Then

\phi\star\psi(c_{1})=\sum_{i}\phi(d_{i})\psi(e_{i})\quad\text{and }\quad\phi\star\psi(c_{2})=\sum_{j}\phi(f_{j})\psi(g_{j}).

From
(\phi,\psi)\in\top_{\mathcal{L}}
, we obtain

\left(\phi(d_{i}),\psi(e_{i}),\phi(f_{j}),\psi(g_{j})\right)\in A^{{}_{\top}4}
. So

\big(\sum_{i}\phi(d_{i})\psi(e_{i}),\sum_{j}\phi(f_{j})\psi(g_{j})\big)

is in
A\times_{\top}A
and
thus
(\phi\star\psi(c_{1}))\top_{A}(\phi\star\psi(c_{2}))
.

•

Next we need to verify the axioms for a locality semigroup: the closeness of
U^{\top_{\mathcal{L}}}
under the convolution product for every
U\subseteq{\mathcal{L}}
and the associativity.

Let
\psi
and
\chi
be independent locality linear maps in
U^{\top_{\mathcal{L}}}
and let
\phi
be in
U
. Then

\phi,\psi,\chi
are pairwise independent. Therefore

\phi\times\psi\times\chi:C^{{}_{\top}3}\longrightarrow A^{{}_{\top}3}

is well defined.
For
(c_{1},c_{2})\in C^{{}_{\top}2}
, that is
c_{1}\in\{c_{2}\}^{\top}
, there exist

(d_{1},e_{1}),\cdots,(d_{k},e_{k})\in\{c_{2}\}^{\top}\times_{\top}\{c_{2}\}^{\top}
, such that

\Delta(c_{1})=\sum_{i}d_{i}\otimes e_{i},

with

(d_{i},e_{i},c_{2})\in C^{{}_{\top}3}
.
Then

(\psi(d_{i}),\chi(e_{i}),\phi(c_{2}))\in A^{{}_{\top}3}
and hence
(\psi(d_{i})\chi(e_{i}))\top_{A}\phi(c_{2}).

So we have

(\psi\star\chi)(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi(c_{2}),

which means

\psi\star\chi
is in
\phi^{\top_{\mathcal{L}}}.

Thus
\psi\star\chi\in U^{\top_{\mathcal{L}}}
. This verifies the first axiom. The associativity of
\star
follows from the associativity of
m
and coassociativity of
\Delta
as in the classical case.

•

For
(c,d)\in\top_{B}
, by the proof of Lemma
3.4.3
.(
3
), we can write

\Delta(c)=\sum_{i}c_{i1}\otimes c_{i2},\quad\Delta(d)=\sum_{j}d_{j1}\otimes d_{j2}

with

(c_{i1},c_{i2},d_{j1},d_{j2})\in B^{{}_{\top}4}.

Then

\Delta(cd)=(m\otimes m)\tau_{23}(\Delta\otimes\Delta)(c\otimes d)=\sum_{i,j}c_{i1}d_{j1}\otimes c_{i2}d_{j2}.

So

\displaystyle(\phi\star\psi)(c\,d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\phi\left(c_{i1}d_{j1}\right)\,\psi\left(c_{i2}d_{j2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j}\phi\left(c_{i1}\right)\,\phi\left(d_{j1}\right)\,\psi\left(c_{i2}\right)\,\psi\left(d_{j2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\phi\left(c_{i1}\right)\,\psi\left(c_{i2}\right)\,\sum_{j}\phi\left(d_{j1}\right)\,\psi\left(d_{j2}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\phi\star\psi(c)\right)\,\left(\phi\star\psi(d)\right).

2.

For the second point, the bialgebra structure plays no role, so we will omit the various “(resp.” in this item.

We assume that
C
is a connected locality coalgebra.
For a locality linear map
\phi:C\to A
, we now prove by induction on the degree of
c_{1}
that the map

\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1_{A},&c_{1}=J,\\
-\phi(c_{1})-\sum_{(c_{1})}\phi(c_{1}^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime}),&c_{1}\in\ker\varepsilon,\end{array}\right.

(3.19)

is well defined, and that
c_{1}\top_{C}\,c
implies
\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi(c).

This is trivial for degree
0
since
\phi^{\star(-1)}(J)=1_{A}
. Assume for any
c_{1}\in C
of degree
\leq n
,
\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})
is well defined, and for
c
with
c_{1}\top_{C}\,c
,

\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi(c)
holds.

Now for any
c_{1}
of degree
n+1\geq 1
with
c_{1}\top_{C}\,c
, by Lemma
3.4.4
.(
2
), we have

\Delta(c_{1})=c_{1}\otimes J+J\otimes c_{1}+\sum_{(c_{1})}c_{1}^{\prime}\otimes c_{1}^{\prime\prime}\ \text{ with }(c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime},c)\in C^{{}_{\top}3}.

By the induction hypothesis,
\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime})
is well defined, such that
\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime})\top_{A}\phi(c)
and
\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime})\top_{A}\phi(c_{1}^{\prime})
. Since
\phi
is a locality linear map, we also have

\phi(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi(c)
and
\phi(c_{1}^{\prime})\top_{A}\phi(c).

Thus

\phi(c_{1}^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime})
is well defined and

(\phi(c_{1}^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime}))\top_{A}\phi(c).

So,
\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})
is well defined and

\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi(c)
, which means
\phi\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi^{\star(-1)}
.

Again by induction on the degree of
c_{1}
, we now prove that

\phi^{\star(-1)}
is a locality linear map by checking

\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{2})\quad\forall c_{2}\in C,c_{1}\top_{C}c_{2},

(3.20)

a fact which is obvious at degree 0. Assume that, for a given
n\geqslant 0
and any
c_{1}
of degree
\leq n

the equation holds. Consider
c_{1}
of degree
n+1\geq 1
. Since
c_{1}\top_{C}c_{2}
, we can choose

\Delta(c_{1})=c_{1}\otimes J+J\otimes c_{1}+\sum_{(c_{1})}c_{1}^{\prime}\otimes c_{1}^{\prime\prime},

such that

\{c_{1},c_{1}^{\prime},c_{1}^{\prime\prime}\}\top_{C}\,c_{2}.

From this we have
\{\phi(c_{1}),\phi(c_{1}^{\prime}),\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1}^{\prime\prime})\}\top_{A}\,\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{2}).

So Eq. (
3.19
) gives

\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{1})\top_{A}\phi^{\star(-1)}(c_{2}).

Therefore, we conclude that
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{L}
is a locality group with unit
u_{A}\varepsilon_{C}
by Lemma
3.4.10
.

3.

For this last item, we use an induction on the sum of degrees of
c
and
d
,
c\top d
to prove

\phi^{\star(-1)}(c)\phi^{\star(-1)}(d)=\phi^{\star(-1)}(cd),

which is true if the sum of degrees is 0.

In general, by Lemma
3.4.4
.(
2
), we write

\Delta(c)=c\otimes J+J\otimes c+\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime},\quad\Delta(d)=d\otimes J+J\otimes d+\sum_{(d)}d^{\prime}\otimes d^{\prime\prime}

with

(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime})\in B^{{}_{\top}4}.

So by
\Delta(cd)=\Delta(c)\Delta(d)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Delta(cd)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle cd\otimes J+J\otimes cd+c\otimes d+d\otimes c+\sum_{(d)}cd^{\prime}\otimes d^{\prime\prime}+\sum_{(d)}d^{\prime}\otimes cd^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle+\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}d\otimes c^{\prime\prime}+\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}d+\sum_{(c)(d)}c^{\prime}d^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}d^{\prime\prime}.

By Eq. (
3.19
) we obtain

\displaystyle\phi^{\star(-1)}(cd)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\phi(cd)-\phi(c)\phi^{\star(-1)}(d)-\phi(d)\phi^{\star(-1)}(c)

\displaystyle-\sum_{(d)}\phi(cd^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})-\sum_{(d)}\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(cd^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle-\sum_{(c)}\phi(c^{\prime}d)\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})-\sum_{(c)}\phi(c^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime}d)

\displaystyle-\sum_{(c)(d)}\phi(c^{\prime}d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime}d^{\prime\prime}).

By Eq. (
3.19
) applied to
c
and
d
, the locality multiplicativity of
\phi
, the commutativity of
A
and induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\phi^{\star(-1)}(cd)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(c)\phi(d)+\sum_{(d)}\phi(c)\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{(c)}\phi(c^{\prime})\phi(d)\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{(c)(d)}\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})\phi(c^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{(c)(d)}\phi(c^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle-\sum_{(c)(d)}\phi(c^{\prime}d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime}d^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(c)\phi(d)+\sum_{(d)}\phi(c)\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})+\sum_{(c)}\phi(c^{\prime})\phi(d)\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle+\sum_{(c)(d)}\phi(c^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\phi(c)+\sum_{(c)}\phi(c^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(c^{\prime\prime})\big)\big(\phi(d)+\sum_{(d)}\phi(d^{\prime})\phi^{\star(-1)}(d^{\prime\prime})\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi^{\star(-1)}(c)\phi^{\star(-1)}(d).

This completes the induction.

∎

Notice that the proof of Proposition
3.4.9
follows with an easy induction from the results of this Theorem.

3.4.4
The locality Birkhoff-Hopf factorisation

Theorem 3.4.12
.

(Algebraic Birkhoff factorisation, locality Hopf algebra version)

Let
\left(H,\top_{H}\right)
be a locality connected Hopf algebra,
H=\oplus_{n\geqslant 0}H_{n}
,
H_{0}=\mathbb{K}e
.
Let
\left(A,\top_{A},\cdot\right)
be a commutative locality algebra with decomposition
A=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
as a vector space such that the linear projections
\pi_{i}
onto
A_{i}
along
A_{\hat{i}},\{\hat{i}\}:=[2]\backslash\{i\},i=1,2,
are independent locality linear maps and
1_{A}
is in
A_{1}
.
Let

\phi:\left(H,\top_{H}\right)\longrightarrow\left(A,\top_{A}\right)

be a locality algebra homomorphism.
Then there are unique independent locality algebra homomorphisms
\phi_{i}:H\to\mathbb{K}+A_{i}
with
\phi_{i}(\ker\varepsilon)\subseteq A_{i},i=1,2,
such that

\phi=\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}\star\phi_{2}.

(3.21)

The map
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
is also a locality algebra homomorphism and
\phi_{1}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\{\phi,\phi_{2}\}
,
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\{\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\}
.

If
\psi:\left(C,\top_{C}\right)\longrightarrow\left(A,\top_{A}\right)

is also a locality linear map independent of
\phi
with
\psi(J)=1_{A}
, then
\phi_{i}
and
\psi_{j}
are independent for
i,j=1,2
.

1.

If in addition
A_{1}
is a sub-locality algebra of
A
, then
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}:C\to\mathbb{K}+A_{1}
.

2.

If in addition
A_{2}
is a locality ideal of
A
, then
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}=\pi_{1}\phi

and
\phi_{2}
is recursively given by

\phi_{2}(1_{H})=1_{A},\quad\phi_{2}(c)=(\pi_{2}\phi)(c)-\sum_{(c)}(\pi_{1}\phi)(c^{\prime})\phi_{2}(c^{\prime\prime})\quad\forall c\in\ker\varepsilon.

(3.22)

with
\tilde{\Delta}(c)=\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}
the reduced coproduct defined in Lemma
3.4.4
.

Remark 3.4.13
.

All the statements that make sense if
H
is only a graded connected locality coalgebra (i.e. all the statements except that
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
are algebra homomorphisms) are true in the category of locality coalgebras. We do not state this level of generality since it will not be so useful later. The interested reader is refered to
[
CGPZ18
, Theorem 4.10]
for this purely coalgebraic case.

Proof.

We prove separately the various statements of the theorem.

•

existence of the
\phi_{i}
:
Let
n\geqslant 1
and
c\in H_{n}
. Since
H
is a connected locality Hopf algebra, we can write

\Delta(c)=J\otimes c+c\otimes J+\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime}

with
\deg(c^{\prime}),\deg(c^{\prime\prime})>0
and
c^{\prime}\top_{H}c^{\prime\prime}
.

We first prove by induction on the degree
n
of
c
that the map given by

\phi_{1}(c)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1_{A},&c=J,\\
-\pi_{1}\Big(\phi(c)+\sum_{(c)}\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\phi(c^{\prime\prime})\Big),&c\in H_{n},n>0,\end{array}\right.

(3.23)

is well-defined, and for any
d\in H
with
d\top_{H}c
, there is

\phi_{1}(c)\top_{A}\phi(d),

which clearly hold for
c
of degree
0
.

Assume that these hold true for
c
of degree less or equal to
n
. Then for
c
of degree
n+1
, according to Lemma
3.4.4

c^{\prime}
is of degree less or equal to
n
, so
\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})
is defined and
\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\top_{A}\phi(c^{\prime\prime})
. Therefore
\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\phi(c^{\prime\prime})
makes sense by the induction hypothesis, and
\phi_{1}(c)
is well-defined.

Now for any
c\top_{H}d
, we have
\phi(c)\top_{A}\phi_{1}(d)
. By a similar induction on the degree of
c
, we obtain

\phi_{1}(c)\top_{A}\phi_{1}(d),

so

\phi_{1}
is a locality linear map.
Therefore, the map

\phi_{2}(c):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1_{A},&c=J,\\
\pi_{2}\big(\phi(c)+\sum_{(c)}\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\phi(c^{\prime\prime})\big),&c\in H_{n},n>0,\end{array}\right.

(3.24)

is well-defined.

Notice that for
c\in C_{n},n>0
, Equation (
3.24
) means

\phi_{2}(c)=\phi(c)+\phi_{1}(c)+\sum_{(c)}\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\phi(c^{\prime\prime}).

(3.25)

With the condition on
J
, this in turn reads

\phi_{2}=\phi_{1}\star\phi
and hence
\phi=\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}\star\phi_{2}.

•

uniqueness of the
\phi_{i}
:
The proof of the uniqueness of the maps
\phi_{i}
is the same as the proof
[
GPZ13b
, Theorem 4.4]
for the case of a trivial locality relation i.e., when
\top=C\times C
.

•

Independence properties of
\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
:
We perform the same induction than in the first point. For any
d\in H
with
c\top_{H}d
, we can take
\{c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime}\}\top_{H}d
. Since
\phi
is a locality map, we obtain

\phi(c)\top_{A}\phi(d)
and
\phi(c^{\prime\prime})\top_{A}\phi(d)
. Also the induction hypothesis gives

\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\top_{A}\phi(d).

Thus

(\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\phi(c^{\prime\prime}))\top_{A}\phi(d)
since
(A,\top_{A},.)
is a locality algebra.
Therefore,

\Big(\phi(c)+\sum_{(c)}\phi_{1}(c^{\prime})\phi(c^{\prime\prime})\Big)\top_{A}\phi(d).

Now since
\pi_{1}
is a locality map and
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are independent for any
c\top_{H}d
we have
\pi_{1}(c)\top_{A}\pi_{1}(d)
and
\pi_{1}(c)\top_{A}\pi_{2}(d)
. Therefore
\pi_{1}(c)\top_{A}(\pi_{1}(d)+\pi_{2}(d))=d
since
\mathrm{Id}_{A}=\pi_{1}+\pi_{2}
. Thus

\pi_{1}
and
\mathrm{Id}_{A}
are independent. Thus

\phi_{1}(c)\top_{A}\phi(d).

Therefore we have proved that
\phi_{1}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi
.

From Equation (
3.25
), we easily obtain

\phi_{1}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi_{2}.

By Equation (
3.19
), an easy induction on the degree of
c
shows that

\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\,\{\phi_{1},\ \phi_{2}\}.

A similar induction shows that if
\psi:\left(C,\top_{C}\right)\longrightarrow\left(A,\top_{A}\right)

is also a locality map, independent of
\phi
with
\phi(J)=1_{A}
, then
\phi_{i}
and
\psi_{j}
are independent for
i,j=1,2
, proving the last statement of the theorem.

•

\phi_{i}
and
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
are locality algebra homomorphisms:
For
c\top_{H}d
, by Lemma
3.4.4
.(
2
), we can write

\Delta(c)=c\otimes J+J\otimes c+\sum_{(c)}c^{\prime}\otimes c^{\prime\prime},\quad\Delta(d)=d\otimes J+J\otimes d+\sum_{(d)}d^{\prime}\otimes d^{\prime\prime}

with

(c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime},d^{\prime},d^{\prime\prime})\in H^{{}_{\top}4}.

According to Proposition
3.1.20
,
\pi_{1}
is a locality Rota-Baxter operator of weight
-1
. Using (
3.5
), by a similar argument as in the non-locality case
[
Guo12
, Theorem 2.4.3]
, we can prove that
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
are homomorphisms of locality algebras. Then, by Theorem
3.4.11
,
3
,
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
is also a homomorphism of locality algebras.

•

\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
takes values in
\mathbb{K}+A_{1}
:
Assume that
A_{1}
is a sub-locality algebra. Equation (
3.19
)) and a simple induction on
n\geqslant 0
show
that
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}(c)\in\mathbb{K}+A_{1}
for any
c\in H_{n}
.

•

Formulas for
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
and
\phi_{2}
:
Assume further that
A_{1}
is a sub-locality algebra and
A_{2}
is a locality ideal. We prove by induction on
n\geqslant 0
that

\phi_{1}^{\star-1}({c})=(\pi_{1}\phi)({c})\quad\forall c\in H_{n}.

(3.26)

Notice that
\phi(J)=\phi_{1}(J)=1_{A}
implies
(\phi_{1}\,\star\,(\pi_{1}\phi))(J)=1_{A}
, so Equation (
3.26
) holds for
n=0

since
H_{0}=KJ
. Assuming that Equation (
3.26
) holds for any
c
in
H
of degree
\leqslant n
, we prove that Equation (
3.26
) holds for any element
c\in H_{n+1}
.

We write

\Delta(c)={c}\otimes J+J\otimes{c}+\sum_{({c})}{c}^{\prime}\otimes{c}^{\prime\prime}

with
{c}^{\prime}\top_{C}{c}^{\prime\prime}
of degree
\leq n
.
By the definition of
\phi_{1}
,

\phi_{1}({c})=-\pi_{1}\left(\phi(c)+\sum_{({c})}\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\,\phi({c}^{\prime\prime})\right)

and we have already proven that

\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\top_{A}\phi({c}^{\prime\prime})
. We have also already proven that
\pi_{1}
is independent to
\mathrm{Id}_{A}
and this straightforwardly implies that
\pi_{2}
is also independent to
\mathrm{Id}_{A}
. Thus

\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\top_{A}\{\pi_{1}\phi({c}^{\prime\prime}),(\pi_{2}\phi)({c}^{\prime\prime})\}.

Then

\displaystyle\phi_{1}({c})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi_{1}\Big(\phi(c)-\sum_{({c})}\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\big((\pi_{1}\phi)({c}^{\prime\prime})+(\pi_{2}\phi)({c}^{\prime\prime})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\pi_{1}\phi)(c)-\sum_{({c})}\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})(\pi_{1}\phi)({c}^{\prime\prime})

where we have used the facts that
A_{1}
is a locality subalgebra, so
\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\pi_{1}\phi({c}^{\prime\prime}\in A_{1}
and that
A_{2}
is a locality ideal so
\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\pi_{2}\phi({c}^{\prime\prime})\in A_{2}
.

Consequently,

\big(\phi_{1}\star(\pi_{1}\phi)\big)({c})=\phi_{1}({c})+\pi_{1}\phi(c)+\sum_{({c})}\phi_{1}({c}^{\prime})\,\pi_{1}\phi({c}^{\prime\prime})=0.

We conclude that
\phi_{1}\,\star\,\left(\pi_{1}\phi\right)=e
, leading to
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}=\pi_{1}\phi
since
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
has been shown to exist. The locality algebraic Birkhoff factorization
\phi=\phi_{1}^{\star-1}\star\phi_{2}
then yields for
c{\in\ker}(\varepsilon)
:

\pi_{1}(\phi(c))+\phi_{2}(c)+\sum_{(c)}\pi_{1}(\phi(c^{\prime}))\phi_{2}(c^{\prime\prime})=\phi(c)

Using
\pi_{2}={\mathrm{Id}}-\pi_{1}
gives the recursive expression (
3.22
) for
\phi_{2}
in terms of
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
.

∎

Remark 3.4.14
.

As in the usual (non locality) case, the decomposition
\phi=\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}\star\phi_{2}
is a
Birkhoff-Hopf decomposition
of the map
\phi
. Let us repeat the introduction: one striking result of Connes and Kreimer
[
CK00
]
is that the the combinatorics of renormalisation is actually encoded by such a decomposition. In particular, if
\phi=\Phi_{\tau}
is the unrenormalised Feynman rules of some QFT
\tau
, then
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
is their renormalised counterpart, while
\phi_{2}
gives the counter-terms. Since this decomposition still exists in the context of locality structure we can define a renormalisation scheme with multiple regularisation variables. The striking fact that
\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}
is a locality map is summed up by the motto

“Renormalisation preserves locality”.

One possibly even more surprising result is that this renormalised map takes a particularly simple form in this framework of locality structures:

\phi_{1}^{\star(-1)}=\pi_{1}\phi.

(3.27)

This is sharp contrast with the non locality case, where the renormalised map is given by a complicated BPHZ
[
BP57
,
Hep66
,
Zim70
]
formula. This can be understood as what we obtain for the price of working with locality structures instead of usual total structures.

To be more specific, let us say that the need for a Birkhoff-Hopf factorisation, or BPHZ formula (as opposed to a minimal subtraction) is because we need to take into account the inner parts of a Feynman graph to obtain a renormalised map that is an algebra morphism, i.e. that preserves locality. In the locality framework, this is done by construction, by requiring for example that the vertices of the Feynman graphs are decorated by elements of a locality set that are pairwise independent. In practice, one needs to implement this locality framework using a suitable locality algebra for the regularisation. The simplest example seems to be an algebra of multivariate meromorphic germs: the multivariate renormalisation scheme.

Applying Theorem
3.4.12
to
(A,\top_{A},\cdot)=\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\perp^{Q},\cdot\right)
yields the following result, which is the application of the tools defined above to build a multivariate renormalisation scheme.

Corollary 3.4.15
.

Let
(H,\top_{H})
be a connected locality Hopf algebra.
Let

\phi:\left(H,\top_{H}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}},\perp^{Q}\right)

be a locality linear map such that
\phi(1_{H})=1_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
.
Let
\phi=(\phi_{1}^{Q})^{\star(-1)}\star\phi_{2}^{Q}
be the algebraic Birkhoff factorisation in Eq. (
3.21
) with
\phi_{1}^{Q}(1_{H})=\phi_{2}^{Q}(1_{H})=1_{\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}}
. Then

1.

\pi_{1}^{Q}\phi
is a locality linear map;

2.

(\phi_{1}^{Q})^{\star(-1)}=\pi_{1}^{Q}\,\phi
so that

\phi=(\pi_{1}^{Q}\,\phi)\star\phi_{2}^{Q};

(3.28)

3.

the maps
\phi_{1}^{Q}
,
\phi_{2}^{Q}
are locality linear maps and
\phi_{1}^{Q}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi
,
\pi_{1}^{Q}\,\phi\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi_{2}^{Q}
;

4.

assuming in addition that
\phi
is a locality algebra homomorphism, then the maps

\pi_{1}^{Q}\phi,\phi_{1}^{Q}
and
\phi_{2}^{Q}
are locality algebra homomorphisms.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward; let us nevertheless mention that
\pi_{1}^{Q}\,\phi\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi_{2}^{Q}
follows from
\phi_{1}\top_{\mathcal{L}}\phi_{2}
in Equation
3.21
combined with the fact that the convolution inverse preserves locality. The crucial fact that
(\phi_{1}^{Q})^{\star(-1)}=\pi_{1}^{Q}\,\phi
comes from the fact that
\mathcal{M}_{+}
is a locality subalgebra of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{-}^{Q}
is a locality ideal of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
(Proposition
3.2.8
).
∎

This is our multivariate renormalisation scheme: given a connected graded locality Hopf algebra
(H,\top,\Delta,.)
and a locality map
\phi:H\longrightarrow\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, the renormalised map is simply
\pi_{+}^{Q}\phi
.

3.5
Application to Kreimer’s toy model

We apply our multivariate renormaisation scheme to Kreimer’s toy model, introduced in
[
Kre97
]
which has some of the essential features of QFT (see
[
Kre13
]
). We first need the locality version of operated structures.

3.5.1
Locality operated structures

We now merge operated structures (Definition
2.2.10
) with locality. These definitions are from
[
CGPZ20a
]
.

Definition 3.5.1
.

Let
(\Omega,\top)
be a locality set. An
(\Omega,\top)
-operated locality set
or simply a
locality operated set
is a locality set
(U,\top_{U})
together with a
partial action

\beta
of
\Omega
on
U
on a subset

\top_{\Omega,U}:=\Omega\times_{\top}U\subseteq\Omega\times U

\beta:\Omega\times_{\top}U\longrightarrow U,\ (\omega,x)\mapsto\beta^{\omega}(x),

satisfying the following compatibility conditions.

1.

For

\Omega\times_{\top}U\times_{\top}U:=\{(\omega,u,u^{\prime})\in\Omega\times U\times U\,|\,(u,u^{\prime})\in\top_{U},(\omega,u),(\omega,u^{\prime})\in\Omega\times_{\top}U\},

the map
\beta\times\mathrm{Id}_{U}:(\Omega\times_{\top}U)\times U\longrightarrow U\times U
restricts to

\beta\times\mathrm{Id}_{U}:\Omega\times_{\top}U\times_{\top}U\longrightarrow U\times_{\top}U.

In other words, if
(\omega,u,u^{\prime})
lies in
\Omega\times_{\top}U\times_{\top}U
, then
(\beta^{\omega}(u),u^{\prime})
lies in
\top_{U}
.

2.

For

\Omega\times_{\top}\Omega\times_{\top}U:=\{(\omega,\omega^{\prime},u)\in\Omega\times\Omega\times U\,|\,(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\in\top_{\Omega},(\omega,u),(\omega^{\prime},u)\in\Omega\times_{\top}U\},

the map
\mathrm{Id}_{\Omega}\times\beta:\Omega\times(U\times_{\top}U)\longrightarrow\Omega\times U
restricts to

\mathrm{Id}_{\Omega}\times\beta:\Omega\times_{\top}\Omega\times_{\top}U\longrightarrow\Omega\times_{\top}U.

In other words, if
(\omega,\omega^{\prime},u^{\prime})
lies in
\Omega\times_{\top}\Omega\times_{\top}U
, then
(\omega,\beta^{\omega^{\prime}}(u))
lies in
\Omega\times_{\top}U
.

We can now define locality operations on more sophisticated locality structures.

Definition 3.5.2
.

Let
(\Omega,\top)
be a locality set.

1.

A
locality
(\Omega,\top)
-operated semigroup
is a quadruple
\left(U,\top_{U},\beta,m_{U}\right)
, where
(U,\top_{U},m_{U})
is a locality semigroup and

\left(U,\top_{U},\beta\right)
is a
(\Omega,\top)
-operated locality set such that

(\omega,u,u^{\prime})\in\Omega\times_{\top}U\times_{\top}U\penalty\ \Longrightarrow\penalty\ (\omega,uu^{\prime})\in\Omega\times_{\top}U;

(3.29)

2.

A
locality
(\Omega,\top)
-operated monoid
is a quintuple
\left(U,\top_{U},\beta,m_{U},1_{U}\right)
, where
(U,\top_{U},m_{U},1_{U})
is a locality monoid
and
\left(U,\top_{U},\beta,m_{U}\right)
is a
(\Omega,\top)
-operated locality semigroup such that
\Omega\times 1_{U}\subset\Omega\times_{\top}U
.

3.

A
(\Omega,\top)
-operated locality nonunitary algebra
(resp.
(\Omega,\top)
-operated locality unitary algebra
) is a quadruple
\left(U,\top_{U},\beta,m_{U}\right)
(resp. quintuple

(U,\top_{U},\beta,

m_{U},1_{U})
) which is a locality algebra (resp. unitary algebra) and a locality
(\Omega,\top)
-operated semigroup (resp. monoid), satisfying the additional condition that for any
\omega\in\Omega
, the set
\{\omega\}^{\top_{\Omega,U}}:=\{u\in U\,|\,\omega\top_{\Omega,U}u\}
is a subspace of
U
on which the action of
\omega
is linear. More precisely, the last condition means that for any
u_{1},u_{2}\in\{\omega\}^{\top_{\Omega,U}}
and for any
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{K}
, we have
k_{1}u_{1}+k_{2}u_{2}\in\{\omega\}^{\top_{\Omega,U}}
and
\beta^{\omega}(k_{1}u_{1}+k_{2}u_{2})=k_{1}\beta^{\omega}(u_{1})+k_{2}\beta^{\omega}(u_{2})

(resp. this condition and
\Omega\times 1_{U}\subset\Omega\times_{\top}U
).

In each case, the
(\Omega,\top)
-operated structure is called
commutative
if the corresponding locality structure is.

Notice that the structures introduced above depend on the chosen set
\top_{\Omega,U}
. Therefore this set should appear in the names of
these various locality operated structures to be completely rigourous. We drop this requirement as it would unnecessarily complicate notations since the set
\top_{\Omega,U}
will always be clear from context.

Example 3.5.3
.

A locality   semigroup
(G,\top,\cdot)
is a locality
(G,\top)
-operated commutative semigroup for the action
\alpha:G\times_{\top}G\to G
given by the product on
G
.

We still have to define categories of locality operated locality structures in order to obtain categories.

Definition 3.5.4
.

Given
(\Omega,\top_{\Omega})
-operated locality structures (sets, semigroups, monoids, nonunitary algebras, algebras)
(U_{i},\top_{U_{i}},\beta_{i})
,
i=1,2,
a

morphism of locality operated locality structures
is a locality morphism (of sets, semigroups, monoids, nonunitary algebras, algebras)
f:U_{1}\to U_{2}
such that

•

(\mathrm{Id}_{\Omega}\times f)(\Omega\times_{\top}U_{1})\subseteq\Omega\times_{\top}U_{2}

and

•

f\circ\beta_{1}^{\omega}=\beta_{2}^{\omega}\circ f
for all
\omega\in\Omega
.

In the following part of this section, we will focus on a locality (Hopf) algebra which has a universal property in the category of locality operated locality algebras. It is described in terms of rooted forests.

3.5.2
The locality Hopf algebra of properly decorated forests

Recall (Definition
2.2.3
) that a rooted forest (or forest for short) is a directed acyclic graph such that there is at most one path between any two vertices and also such that each connected component has a minimal element, called a root. A connected forest is called a (rooted) tree. Recall also that for a set
\Omega
, an
\Omega
-decorated (rooted) forest is a pair
(F,d_{F})
with
F
a (rooted) forest and
d_{F}:V(F)\longrightarrow\Omega
